\title{\textbf{Space--Time Algebra}\\[0.35em]
  \large A compact development of paravectors and their physical applications}
\author{Dr. Luke A. Wendt}
\date{}

\begin{document}
\pagenumbering{roman}
\maketitle
\thispagestyle{empty}

\begin{abstract}
A paravector algebra is formed from a complex scalar and three-vector,
with dot and cross products combined. Quaternions, rotations, Lorentz
boosts, projections, spectral functions, spacetime geometry, relativistic
dynamics, electromagnetism, gauge coupling, Klein--Gordon and Dirac
equations, Schr\"odinger and Pauli limits, and spin amplitudes and
probabilities are expressed in this algebra. These results are connected
through shared definitions and short derivations: proper time,
four-momentum, and the mass shell are obtained from spacetime determinants;
Klein--Gordon is obtained from energy and momentum operators, and Dirac
from first-order factorization. Spin one-half is determined by the rotation
structure of paravector spinors; its magnetic coupling and measurement
probabilities are obtained from the same products and projectors.
\end{abstract}

\clearpage
\begingroup
\setlength{\parskip}{0pt}
\tableofcontents
\endgroup
\clearpage
\pagenumbering{arabic}

\section{Algebra and paravectors}
\label{sec:algebra}

\subsection{Notation and physical conventions}

Natural units $\hbar=c=1$, rationalized electromagnetic units,
and signature $(+,-,-,-)$ are used. SI conversions, including their unit
assignments and explanations, appear in boxes labeled SI units. Within
these boxes, the usual symbols carry the stated SI units; outside them,
natural units apply.
Vectors and matrices of objects are \textcolor{cyan}{\textbf{bold cyan}}; paravectors are \textcolor{magenta}{\textbf{bold magenta}}.
Bold calligraphic Latin lettering is reserved for paravectors and paravector operators; Greek glyphs are retained and bold.
No meaning is assigned by convention to uppercase
or lowercase letters. The ordered sigma triple $\bsig$ is bold;
each individual sigma $\pvsigma_k$ is non-bold.

Derivatives such as $\partial_t$ and $\pdr$ retain bold vector subscripts.
Scalar quantities and coefficient components remain unadorned.
The scalar trip distance $\ell$ is distinguished from the position vector $\br$.
Scalar and paravector quantities are distinguished by their stated domains.
The paravector momentum $\pP$ is distinguished from its vector part $\bp$ by calligraphic lettering and color.


Space--time algebra is developed here using complex paravectors.
Comparisons with terminology in the literature are collected in
Appendix~\ref{sec:conventions}.

$c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ is identified with its coefficient pair
$(c,\bZ)\in\C\oplus\C^3$. Real paravectors lie in $\R\oplus\R^3$;
mixed sums specify the two coefficient spaces separately.
Values are specified by the direct sum; multiplication is defined by the sigma rules.
The sigmas $\pvsigma_k$ are algebraic objects with order-dependent
multiplication rules; $\bsig$ is their ordered triple, not a numerical
vector in $\R^3$.

Domains persist within a section unless a specialization is stated.
Primes and subscripts inherit the declared type unless explicitly redefined.
For fields, membership states the value at each real spacetime point;
function notation specifies the independent variables.
The required regularity is assumed for all differentiations.

\subsection{Sigmas and ordered multiplication}

The sigmas $\pvsigma_n$, $n=1,2,3$, are defined by the multiplication
rules below. The general complex paravector is expressed in terms of
these sigmas~\citeat{baylis2004}{pp.~4--5}{page=4}:

\begin{center}
\setlength{\fboxsep}{1.2em}
\fcolorbox{black}{white}{%
\begin{minipage}{0.88\textwidth}
\begin{align*}
 \pvsigma_n^2&=1,
 &\pvsigma_n\pvsigma_m&=-\pvsigma_m\pvsigma_n\quad(n\ne m),\\
 \ii&=\pvsigma_1\pvsigma_2\pvsigma_3,
 &\ii^2&=-1,\\
 \bsig&=\{\pvsigma_1,\pvsigma_2,\pvsigma_3\},
 &\ii\bsig&=\{\pvsigma_2\pvsigma_3,\pvsigma_3\pvsigma_1,\pvsigma_1\pvsigma_2\},\\
 \pZ&=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\bY\ii)\dotprod\bsig,
 &a,b&\in\R,\quad \bX,\bY\in\R^3.
\end{align*}
\end{minipage}}
\end{center}

Ordinary three-vector operations are defined by
\begin{align*}
 \bX\dotprod\bY&=X_1Y_1+X_2Y_2+X_3Y_3,\\
 \bX\times\bY&=\{X_2Y_3-X_3Y_2,
 X_3Y_1-X_1Y_3,X_1Y_2-X_2Y_1\},\\
 \bX^2&:=\bX\dotprod\bX.
\end{align*}
Thus the square of a vector always denotes its self dot product.
For complex vectors, the same formulas are used for dot and cross products,
with neither factor conjugated.
The elementary Cartesian vectors used below are the real vectors
\[
 \bm e_1=\{1,0,0\},\qquad
 \bm e_2=\{0,1,0\},\qquad
 \bm e_3=\{0,0,1\}.
\]
Thus $\bm e_k\dotprod\bsig=\pvsigma_k$; the scalar basis element is $1$.
Unindexed $e$ denotes only Euler's number.

A basis over $\R$ is provided by the eight elements
\[
 1,\quad \pvsigma_1,\pvsigma_2,\pvsigma_3,\quad
 \ii\pvsigma_1,\ii\pvsigma_2,\ii\pvsigma_3,\quad \ii.
\]
By anticommutation,
$\ii^2=-\pvsigma_1^2\pvsigma_2^2\pvsigma_3^2=-1$.
Two sign changes are introduced when $\pvsigma_n$ is moved through the
other two sigmas, so
\begin{equation*}
 \ii\pvsigma_n=\pvsigma_n\ii.
\end{equation*}
Thus $\ii$ commutes with every sigma and hence with every paravector.

For $\pX,\pY\in\C\oplus\C^3$, the commutator is
\begin{gather*}
 [\pX,\pY]:=\pX \pY-\pY \pX,\\
 [\pY,\pX]=-[\pX,\pY].
\end{gather*}
Thus $[\pX,\pY]=0$ exactly when $\pX$ and $\pY$ commute.
For $n,m,k=1,2,3$, the Kronecker delta is defined by
\begin{equation*}
 \delta_{nm}:=\begin{aligned}[t]
  &1\quad\text{if }n=m,\\
  &0\quad\text{if }n\ne m,
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
and the Levi--Civita symbol is defined by
\begin{equation*}
 \epsilon_{nmk}:=\begin{aligned}[t]
  &+1\quad\text{if }(n,m,k)\text{ is an even permutation of }(1,2,3),\\
  &-1\quad\text{if }(n,m,k)\text{ is an odd permutation of }(1,2,3),\\
  &\phantom{+}0\quad\text{if two or more indices are equal}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
For $n,m=1,2,3$, the ordered multiplication rule and commutator are~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(3)}{page=4}\,\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(10)}{page=5}
\begin{align}
  \pvsigma_n\pvsigma_m=\delta_{nm}
  +\sum_{k=1}^{3}\epsilon_{nmk}\,\ii\pvsigma_k,
 \label{eq:sigma-product}\\
  [\pvsigma_n,\pvsigma_m]
  =2\sum_{k=1}^{3}\epsilon_{nmk}\,\ii\pvsigma_k. \notag
\end{align}
\subsection{Hermitian and sigma conjugation}
\label{sec:conjugate-operators}

\emph{Hermitian conjugation} is defined by
\eqref{eq:hermitian-definition}, and \emph{sigma conjugation}
is defined by \eqref{eq:sigma-conjugation-definition}~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(3)}{page=2}:
\begin{align}
 (\,\cdot\,)^{\mathsf H}
 &:=\text{the multiplication order of the sigmas is reversed},
 \label{eq:hermitian-definition}\\
 (\,\cdot\,)^*
 &:=\text{every $\pvsigma_n$ is replaced by $-\pvsigma_n$ with product order preserved}.
 \label{eq:sigma-conjugation-definition}
\end{align}
Thus, for two sigmas,
\begin{align*}
 (\pvsigma_m\pvsigma_n)^{\mathsf H}&=\pvsigma_n\pvsigma_m,\\
 (\pvsigma_m\pvsigma_n)^*&=(-\pvsigma_m)(-\pvsigma_n).
\end{align*}
Both operations are applied term by term to sums, with real coefficients left unchanged.
Since $\ii=\pvsigma_1\pvsigma_2\pvsigma_3$, under sigma conjugation each
sigma is negated and multiplication order is preserved:
\[
 \ii^*=(-\pvsigma_1)(-\pvsigma_2)(-\pvsigma_3)
 =-\pvsigma_1\pvsigma_2\pvsigma_3=-\ii.
\]
Complex coefficients are therefore conjugated, and the sign of $\bsig$
is reversed. The resulting signs on the basis elements are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c c c c c}
  \toprule
  element & $1$ & $\pvsigma_n$ & $\ii\pvsigma_n$ & $\ii$\\
  \midrule
  $(\cdot)^*$ & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & $-$\\
  $(\cdot)^{\mathsf H}$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$\\
  $(\cdot)^{*\mathsf H}$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & $+$\\
  \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For $\ii\pvsigma_n$, the signs are obtained directly:
\begin{align*}
 (\ii\pvsigma_n)^*&=\ii^*\pvsigma_n^*=\ii\pvsigma_n,\\
 (\ii\pvsigma_n)^{\mathsf H}
 &=\pvsigma_n^{\mathsf H}\ii^{\mathsf H}=-\ii\pvsigma_n.
\end{align*}
For $\pZ,\pZ'\in\C\oplus\C^3$, the definitions give the product
laws~\citeat{baylis2004}{p.~4}{page=4}\,\citeat{chappell-functions}{p.~2}{page=2}
\begin{align}
 (\pZ \pZ')^{\mathsf H}&=\pZ'^{\mathsf H}\pZ^{\mathsf H},
 \label{eq:hermitian-product}\\
 (\pZ \pZ')^*&=\pZ^*\pZ'^*.
 \label{eq:sigma-conjugate-product}
\end{align}
The operations commute with each other: $\pZ^{*\mathsf H}=\pZ^{\mathsf H*}$.
Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix} gives both operations in the Pauli-matrix
representation, where $^{\mathsf H}$ is the usual matrix Hermitian conjugation.

\subsection{Complex paravectors and their components}
\label{sec:paravectors}

$a,b\in\R$ and $\bX,\bY\in\R^3$ are assumed. The two real paravectors
$\pX,\pY\in\R\oplus\R^3$ are combined into the general complex paravector
$\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$:
\begin{align*}
 \pX&=a+\bX\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \pY&=b+\bY\dotprod\bsig,\\[4pt]
 \pZ&=\pX+\ii \pY\\
  &=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\ii\bY)\dotprod\bsig\\
  &=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
Here $c=a+b\ii\in\C$ and $\bZ=\bX+\ii\bY\in\C^3$ are its
complex scalar and vector coefficients.

Thus $\operatorname{sc},\det,\operatorname{tr}$ take values in $\C$,
$\operatorname{vec}$ in $\C^3$, and $\operatorname{re},\operatorname{im}$
in $\R\oplus\R^3$. Conjugates, adjugates, and existing inverses take values
in $\C\oplus\C^3$.
The complex scalar and vector extractions are defined by
\begin{align*}
 \scpart {\pZ}&:=c=a+\ii b,\\
 \vecpart {\pZ}&:=\bZ=\bX+\ii\bY.
\end{align*}
This notation is used throughout unless a real specialization is stated.

\subsection{Conjugates, determinant, and inverse}
\label{sec:algebra-operations}

On the coefficients, both conjugations are expressed by
$c^*=c^{\mathsf H}=a-b\ii$ and
$\bZ^*=\bZ^{\mathsf H}=\bX-\ii\bY$; on the paravector, the two
conjugations differ in the vector sign~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(45)}{page=11}:
\begin{align}
 \pZ^*&=c^*-\bZ^*\dotprod\bsig
 =(a-b\ii)+(-\bX+\ii\bY)\dotprod\bsig,
 \label{eq:algebra-star}\\
 \pZ^{\mathsf H}&=c^*+\bZ^*\dotprod\bsig
 =(a-b\ii)+(\bX-\ii\bY)\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:algebra-H}
\end{align}
Their composition defines the adjugate~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(39)}{page=10}
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{adj} \pZ:=\pZ^{*\mathsf H}=c-\bZ\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:adjugate}
\end{equation}
The trace is twice the scalar part:
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{tr}(\pZ):=\pZ+\operatorname{adj} \pZ=2\scpart {\pZ}=2c.
 \label{eq:trace-scalar}
\end{equation}
The determinant is the complex scalar~\citeat{baylis-applications}{p.~104}{page=115}
\begin{equation}
 \det \pZ:=\adj {\pZ}\,\pZ=\pZ\operatorname{adj} \pZ=c^2-\bZ^2.
 \label{eq:det}
\end{equation}
For $\pZ'\in\C\oplus\C^3$, adjugation reverses product order, giving determinant
multiplicativity:
\begin{align*}
 \adj{\pZ \pZ'}&=\adj{\pZ'}\adj {\pZ},
 \\
 \det(\pZ \pZ')&=\adj{\pZ'}(\adj {\pZ}\,\pZ)\pZ'
           =\det(\pZ)\det(\pZ').
\end{align*}
Since the determinants are scalars, the determinant of any product is
unchanged by reordering its factors.  For a scalar $z\in\C$,
\begin{align}
 \adj{z\pZ}&=z\adj {\pZ}, \notag\\
 \det(z\pZ)&=z^2\det(\pZ).
 \label{eq:det-scalar-scaling}
\end{align}
When $\det(\pZ)\ne0$, the inverse is~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(47)}{page=11}
\begin{equation}
 \pZ^{-1}=\frac{\adj {\pZ}}{\det(\pZ)}
       =\frac{c-\bZ\dotprod\bsig}{c^2-\bZ^2}.
 \label{eq:inverse}
\end{equation}
For $\pZ'=c'+\bZ'\dotprod\bsig$, with $c'\in\C$, $\bZ'\in\C^3$, division is
order-sensitive.  An unambiguous quotient exists when $\pZ$ is invertible
and the factors commute:
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{\pZ'}{\pZ}:=\pZ'\pZ^{-1}=\pZ^{-1}\pZ'
 \qquad\text{if }\bZ\times\bZ'=0.
\end{equation*}
Differentiating $\pZ^{-1}\pZ=1$ gives the inverse rule
\begin{equation*}
 \dd(\pZ^{-1})=-\pZ^{-1}(\dd \pZ)\pZ^{-1}.
\end{equation*}
For $\pZ,\pZ',\pZ''\in\C\oplus\C^3$, scalar part obeys the cyclic laws
\begin{align}
 \scpart{\pZ \pZ'}&=\scpart{\pZ'\pZ},
 \notag\\
 \scpart{\pZ \pZ'\pZ''}&=\scpart{\pZ'\pZ''\pZ}=\scpart{\pZ''\pZ \pZ'}.
 \label{eq:scalar-cyclic-three}
\end{align}
Multiplying these identities by $2$ gives trace cyclicity.  Their similarity
invariants are derived in Section~\ref{sec:preserved-quantities}.

\subsection{Real and imaginary extractions}

The real and imaginary extractions are defined using Hermitian conjugation~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(9)}{page=5}:
\begin{align}
 \repart {\pZ}&:=\tfrac12(\pZ+\pZ^{\mathsf H})=\pX,
 \label{eq:algebra-re}\\
 \impart {\pZ}&:=\frac{\pZ-\pZ^{\mathsf H}}{2\ii}=\pY.
 \label{eq:algebra-im}
\end{align}
Adjugation evaluates the scalar and vector extractions explicitly~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(14)}{page=6}:
\begin{align*}
 \scpart {\pZ}&=\tfrac12(\pZ+\adj {\pZ})=c,
 \\
 \vecpart {\pZ}\dotprod\bsig&=\tfrac12(\pZ-\adj {\pZ})=\bZ\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
The functions $\operatorname{re}$ and $\operatorname{im}$ also act on
complex coefficients.  Composing extractions recovers all four real parts:
\begin{align*}
 a&=\repart c=\scpart{\repart {\pZ}},\\
 b&=\impart c=\scpart{\impart {\pZ}},\\
 \bX&=\repart{\bZ}=\vecpart{\repart {\pZ}},\\
 \bY&=\impart{\bZ}=\vecpart{\impart {\pZ}}.
\end{align*}
Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix} reproduces these intrinsic operations with Pauli
matrices.

\section{Products and normalizations}
\label{sec:paravector-products}

\subsection{Invertibility and normalization}
\label{sec:unit-forms}

Notation: $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$, with $c\in\C$ and $\bZ\in\C^3$.
For $\bZ\in\C^3$, the bilinear square and squared norm are distinguished:
\begin{align*}
 \bZ^2&:=\bZ\dotprod\bZ,\\
 \lVert\bZ\rVert^2&:=\bZ\dotprod\bZ^*.
\end{align*}
For $c\in\C$, $\lVert c\rVert^2:=cc^*$ is defined.  The squared paravector norm is~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(46)}{page=11}
\begin{equation}
 \lVert \pZ\rVert^2:=\lVert c\rVert^2+\lVert\bZ\rVert^2
 =cc^*+\bZ\dotprod\bZ^*.
 \label{eq:coefficient-norm}
\end{equation}
All norms take values in $\R_{\ge0}$ and denote nonnegative real square roots.
Here $\bu\in\C^3$ and $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$.
Each name below specifies a condition; a letter alone implies none.

\Needspace{7\baselineskip}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Vector of square one.} $\bu$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \bu^2=\bu\dotprod\bu=1.
 \label{eq:bilinear-unit-vector}
\end{equation}
For $\bZ^2\ne0$, $\bu=\bZ/\sqrt{\bZ^2}$ is defined with either square root.
Its norm need not be one.  A nonzero vector with $\bZ^2=0$
cannot be normalized this way.
\item \textbf{Vector of unit norm.} $\bu$ satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 \lVert\bu\rVert^2=\bu\dotprod\bu^*=1.
\end{equation*}
For any $\bZ\ne0$, $\bu=\bZ/\lVert\bZ\rVert$ is defined; then
$\bu^2=\bZ^2/\lVert\bZ\rVert^2$ need not be one.
\phantomsection\label{def:real-unit-vector}
For real vectors the two conditions coincide: a \emph{real unit vector}
has $\bu^2=\lVert\bu\rVert^2=1$.  For complex vectors, specify which holds.
\Needspace{14\baselineskip}
\item \textbf{Invertible paravector.} $\pZ$ has an inverse on both sides:
\begin{equation}
 \pZ \pZ^{-1}=\pZ^{-1}\pZ=1.
 \label{eq:unit-paravector}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, $\det(\pZ)\ne0$. Neither unit norm nor determinant one is
required.
\item \textbf{Unitary paravector.} Its Hermitian conjugate is its inverse:
\begin{equation}
 \pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ=1.
 \label{eq:unitary-transform}
\end{equation}
Then $\pZ^{\mathsf H}=\pZ^{-1}$ and $\pZ \pZ^{\mathsf H}=1$.
\item \textbf{Paravector of determinant one.} $\pZ$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \det \pZ=1.
 \label{eq:unit-determinant-paravector}
\end{equation}
It is invertible with $\pZ^{-1}=\adj {\pZ}$; it need not be unitary.
For real $\pZ$, $\operatorname{adj} \pZ=\pZ^*$, so this condition is also $\pZ^*\pZ=1$.
This equivalence need not hold for complex $\pZ$.
\item \textbf{Paravector of unit norm.} $\pZ$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \lVert \pZ\rVert^2=1.
 \label{eq:unit-norm-paravector}
\end{equation}
For $\pZ\ne0$, $\pZ'=\pZ/\lVert \pZ\rVert$ has unit norm.
It need not have an inverse: $(1+\bu\dotprod\bsig)/\sqrt2$ has norm one
and determinant zero for any real unit vector $\bu$.
Unitarity implies unit norm by \eqref{eq:hermitian-norm}.
\item \textbf{Self-inverse paravector.} $\pZ$ is its own inverse:
\begin{equation}
 \pZ^2=1.
 \label{eq:involutory-paravector}
\end{equation}
Then $\pZ^{-1}=\pZ$; it is unitary exactly when $\pZ^{\mathsf H}=\pZ$.
Its parts obey $c^2+\bZ^2=1$ and $2c\bZ=0$:
thus $\pZ=\pm1$ or $\pZ=\bu\dotprod\bsig$ with $\bu^2=1$.
\end{itemize}

The physical roles of the paravector conditions are compared in
Section~\ref{sec:preserved-quantities}.

\subsection{Products and quadratic forms}

For $\bX,\bY\in\C^3$, multiplication combines the dot and cross products;
both products are complex bilinear, with no conjugation.
The sigma rule~\eqref{eq:sigma-product} gives the dot--cross identity~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(38)}{page=10}
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 (\bX\dotprod\bsig)(\bY\dotprod\bsig)
 &=\sum_{n=1}^{3}\sum_{m=1}^{3}
   X_nY_m\pvsigma_n\pvsigma_m\\
 &=\sum_{n=1}^{3}\sum_{m=1}^{3}X_nY_m\delta_{nm}
   +\sum_{n=1}^{3}\sum_{m=1}^{3}\sum_{k=1}^{3}
   X_nY_m\epsilon_{nmk}\ii\pvsigma_k\\
 &=\bX\dotprod\bY+\ii(\bX\times\bY)\dotprod\bsig.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:vector-product}
\end{equation}
For real $\bX,\bY$, the product lies in $\R\oplus\ii\R^3$.
Adding the product with $\bX$ and $\bY$ interchanged gives
\begin{equation*}
 2(\bX\dotprod\bY)
 =(\bX\dotprod\bsig)(\bY\dotprod\bsig)
 +(\bY\dotprod\bsig)(\bX\dotprod\bsig).
\end{equation*}
Consequently, for every nonnegative integer $n$,
\begin{align*}
 (\bX\dotprod\bsig)^2&=\bX^2,\\
 (\bX\dotprod\bsig)^{2n}&=(\bX^2)^n,\\
 (\bX\dotprod\bsig)^{2n+1}&=(\bX^2)^n\bX\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
Also,
\begin{equation*}
 (\bX\dotprod\bsig)\bsig=\bX-\ii\bX\times\bsig.
\end{equation*}
Subtracting the two orders gives
\begin{equation*}
 [\bX\dotprod\bsig,\bY\dotprod\bsig]
 =2\ii(\bX\times\bY)\dotprod\bsig.
\end{equation*}
Hence
\begin{align*}
 [\bX\dotprod\bsig,\bsig]&=-2\ii\bX\times\bsig,\\
 [\bX\dotprod\ii\bsig,\bsig]&=2\bX\times\bsig,\\
 [\bX\dotprod\bsig,\bsig]\dotprod\bY
 &=2\ii(\bX\times\bY)\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}

\subsection{Real products}

For real $\pX=a+\bX\dotprod\bsig$ and $\pY=b+\bY\dotprod\bsig$, with
$a,b\in\R$ and $\bX,\bY\in\R^3$, \eqref{eq:vector-product} gives
\begin{align*}
 \pX \pY&=(ab+\bX\dotprod\bY)
 +(a\bY+b\bX+\ii\bX\times\bY)\dotprod\bsig,
 \\
 [\pX,\pY]&=2\ii(\bX\times\bY)\dotprod\bsig, \\
 \pX^2&=(a^2+\bX^2)+2a\bX\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
Thus $\pX \pY\in\R\oplus\C^3$, $[\pX,\pY]\in\{0\}\oplus\ii\R^3$, and
$\pX^2\in\R\oplus\R^3$. Real paravectors are not closed under multiplication.
The real quadratic identity is \eqref{eq:minimal} with $c=a$.
If $\bX\times\bY=0$, then $\pX \pY=\pY \pX$; when $\det(\pX)\ne0$, also
$\pX^{-1}\pY=\pY \pX^{-1}$.

\subsection{Complex products}

The decompositions $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ and $\pZ'=c'+\bZ'\dotprod\bsig$ are used, where
$c,c'\in\C$ and $\bZ,\bZ'\in\C^3$.  Complex bilinearity extends
\eqref{eq:vector-product} to the general product
\begin{equation}
 \pZ \pZ'=(cc'+\bZ\dotprod\bZ')+
 \bigl(c\bZ'+c'\bZ+\ii\bZ\times\bZ'\bigr)\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:para-product}
\end{equation}

The result $\pZ \pZ'\in\C\oplus\C^3$ has a complex scalar and complex vector part.
Thus
\begin{align*}
 \scpart{\pZ \pZ'}&=cc'+\bZ\dotprod\bZ',\\
 \vecpart{\pZ \pZ'}&=c\bZ'+c'\bZ+\ii\bZ\times\bZ'.
\end{align*}
Equivalently, $\pZ=\pX+\ii \pY$ and $\pZ'=\pX'+\ii \pY'$ are used with all four
factors real.  Their products need not be real, but distributivity gives
\begin{equation*}
 \pZ \pZ'=(\pX \pX'-\pY \pY')+\ii(\pX \pY'+\pY \pX').
\end{equation*}

The decomposition $\pZ=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\ii\bY)\dotprod\bsig$ is used with real
$a,b,\bX,\bY$, and primes are used for the second paravector's real parts.  The real and imaginary
scalar and vector parts of $\pZ \pZ'$ are
\begin{align*}
 \text{real scalar: }&aa'-bb'+\bX\dotprod\bX'-\bY\dotprod\bY',\\
 \text{imaginary scalar: }&ab'+ba'+\bX\dotprod\bY'+\bY\dotprod\bX',\\
 \text{real vector: }&a\bX'-b\bY'+a'\bX-b'\bY
                    -\bX\times\bY'-\bY\times\bX',\\
 \text{imaginary vector: }&a\bY'+b\bX'+a'\bY+b'\bX
                    +\bX\times\bX'-\bY\times\bY'.
\end{align*}
Specializing \eqref{eq:para-product} gives the quadratic products:
\begin{align}
 \pZ^2&=(a^2-b^2+\bX^2-\bY^2)
 +2\ii(ab+\bX\dotprod\bY) \notag\\
 &\quad+2(a\bX-b\bY)\dotprod\bsig
 +2\ii(a\bY+b\bX)\dotprod\bsig, \notag\\
 \pZ \pZ^*&=(a^2+b^2-\bX^2-\bY^2)
 -2(\bX\times\bY)\dotprod\bsig
 +2\ii(a\bY-b\bX)\dotprod\bsig,
 \label{eq:star-product-left}\\
 \pZ^*\pZ&=(a^2+b^2-\bX^2-\bY^2)
 +2(\bX\times\bY)\dotprod\bsig
 +2\ii(a\bY-b\bX)\dotprod\bsig,
 \label{eq:star-product-right}\\
 \pZ \pZ^{\mathsf H}&=a^2+b^2+\bX^2+\bY^2
 +2(a\bX+b\bY+\bX\times\bY)\dotprod\bsig,
 \label{eq:hermitian-product-left}\\
 \pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ&=a^2+b^2+\bX^2+\bY^2
 +2(a\bX+b\bY-\bX\times\bY)\dotprod\bsig,
 \label{eq:hermitian-product-right}\\
 \pZ \pZ^{*\mathsf H}&=\det \pZ
 =a^2-b^2-\bX^2+\bY^2
 +2\ii(ab-\bX\dotprod\bY). \notag
\end{align}
Equations~\eqref{eq:star-product-left} and \eqref{eq:star-product-right}
have values in $\R\oplus\C^3$ and show $\pZ \pZ^*=\pZ^*\pZ$ exactly when
$\bX\times\bY=0$.  Equations
\eqref{eq:hermitian-product-left} and \eqref{eq:hermitian-product-right}
take values in $\R\oplus\R^3$ and give the same condition for
$\pZ \pZ^{\mathsf H}=\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ$.

\subsection{Norm and determinant}
For the complex coefficients $c=a+b\ii$ and $\bZ=\bX+\ii\bY$, the squared norms
defined above are
\begin{align*}
 \lVert c\rVert^2&=a^2+b^2,\\
 \lVert\bZ\rVert^2&=\bX^2+\bY^2.
\end{align*}
For the squared norm~\eqref{eq:coefficient-norm}, the quadratic products
and cyclicity give the Hermitian form~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(46)}{page=11}
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \lVert \pZ\rVert^2&=\scpart{\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ}
 =\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}(\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ)
 =\scpart{\pZ \pZ^{\mathsf H}}\\
 &=\scpart{\pZ^*\pZ^{*\mathsf H}}
 =\scpart{\pZ^{*\mathsf H}\pZ^*}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:hermitian-norm}
\end{equation}
For complex coefficients, $c^2$ and $\bZ^2$ generally differ from these
squared norms.  Also $\det(\pZ)$ may be complex.  For a complex scalar
$z=r e^{\ii\theta}$, with real $r\ge0$ and $\theta$, scalar scaling gives
\begin{align}
 \det(z\pZ)&=r^2e^{2\ii\theta}\det(\pZ), \notag\\
 \lVert z\pZ\rVert^2&=\lVert z\rVert^2\lVert \pZ\rVert^2
 =r^2\lVert \pZ\rVert^2.
 \label{eq:norm-scalar-scaling}
\end{align}
The determinant retains the scalar phase by \eqref{eq:det-scalar-scaling};
the norm loses it by \eqref{eq:norm-scalar-scaling}.

\section{Projectors and spectral functions}
\label{sec:spectral}

\subsection{Quadratic identity and eigenvalues}

Notation: $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$, with $c\in\C$ and $\bZ\in\C^3$.
Since $\pZ-c=\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ and
$(\bZ\dotprod\bsig)^2=\bZ^2$,
\begin{align*}
 (\pZ-c)^2&=\bZ^2,\\
 \pZ^2-2c\pZ+(c^2-\bZ^2)&=0.
\end{align*}
Using $\operatorname{tr}(\pZ)=2c$ and
$\det \pZ=c^2-\bZ^2$ gives the trace--determinant identity
\begin{equation}
 \pZ^2-\operatorname{tr}(\pZ)\pZ+\det \pZ=0.
  \label{eq:minimal}
\end{equation}
An eigenvalue is a scalar $\lambda\in\C$ for which $\pZ-\lambda$ has no
inverse.  Thus $\det(\pZ-\lambda)=\lambda^2-2c\lambda+\det \pZ=0$,
the scalar quadratic corresponding to \eqref{eq:minimal}.  Its roots are
\begin{equation*}
 \lambda_\pm
 =\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\pZ)
 \pm\sqrt{\operatorname{tr}(\pZ)^2-4\det(\pZ)}}{2}
 =c\pm\sqrt{\bZ^2}.
\end{equation*}
Because $\bZ$ is generally complex, $\bZ^2=\bZ\dotprod\bZ$ and its square
root may also be complex.
The quadratic identity therefore factors as
\begin{equation*}
 (\pZ-\lambda_-)(\pZ-\lambda_+)=0.
\end{equation*}

\subsection{Projection operators}
\label{sec:algebra-projectors}

For a \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$, the
paravector $\bu\dotprod\bsig\in\{0\}\oplus\R^3$ is Hermitian and
\hyperref[eq:involutory-paravector]{self-inverse}.  Its two orthogonal projectors are defined by~\citeat{baylis-applications}{p.~121}{page=132}
\begin{equation}
 \pvPi_\pm:=\tfrac12(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bsig).
 \label{eq:projectors}
\end{equation}

Here $\pvPi_\pm\in\R\oplus\R^3$ with scalar part $1/2$.
Since $(\bu\dotprod\bsig)^2=1$,
\begin{align*}
 \pvPi_\pm^2&=\pvPi_\pm,\\
 \pvPi_+\pvPi_-&=0,\\
 \pvPi_++\pvPi_-&=1,\\
 \pvPi_\pm^{\mathsf H}&=\pvPi_\pm,\\
 \pvPi_\pm^*&=\pvPi_\mp,\\
 (\bu\dotprod\bsig)\pvPi_\pm&=\pm \pvPi_\pm.
\end{align*}
Thus $\bu\dotprod\bsig=\pvPi_+-\pvPi_-$.  For a second
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu'$,
\begin{align*}
 (\bu\dotprod\bsig)(\bu'\dotprod\bsig)
 &=\bu\dotprod\bu'+\ii(\bu\times\bu')\dotprod\bsig,\\
 [\bu\dotprod\bsig,\bu'\dotprod\bsig]
 &=2\ii(\bu\times\bu')\dotprod\bsig,\\
 (\bu\dotprod\bsig)(\bu'\dotprod\bsig)(\bu\dotprod\bsig)
 &=\left(2(\bu\dotprod\bu')\bu-\bu'\right)\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
The projections $\bu'_{\parallel}=(\bu'\dotprod\bu)\bu$ and
$\bu'_{\perp}=\bu'-\bu'_{\parallel}$ are defined in $\R^3$.  Then
\begin{align*}
 \pvPi_\pm(\bu'\dotprod\bsig)\pvPi_\pm
 &=\pm(\bu\dotprod\bu')\pvPi_\pm,\\
 \pvPi_\pm(\bu'\dotprod\bsig)\pvPi_\mp
 &=\tfrac12(\bu'_{\perp}\pm\ii(\bu\times\bu'))\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
For $\pvPi_\pm'=(1\pm\bu'\dotprod\bsig)/2\in\R\oplus\R^3$, all four products lie in $\R\oplus\C^3$:
\begin{align*}
 \pvPi_\pm\pvPi_\pm'&=\tfrac14(1+\bu\dotprod\bu')
 +\tfrac14(\pm\bu\pm\bu'+\ii\bu\times\bu')\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \pvPi_\pm\pvPi_\mp'&=\tfrac14(1-\bu\dotprod\bu')
 +\tfrac14(\pm\bu\mp\bu'-\ii\bu\times\bu')\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}

\subsection{Spectral rule}
The decomposition $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ is used, with $c\in\C$, $\bZ\in\C^3$, and
$\bZ^2\ne0$.  The complex scalars $z,\lambda_\pm$, complex vector $\bu$, and
projectors $\pvPi_\pm\in\R\oplus\C^3$ are defined by
\begin{align*}
 z&:=+\sqrt{\bZ^2},\\
 \bu&:=\bZ/z,& \pZ&=c+z\bu\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \lambda_\pm&:=c\pm z,& \pvPi_\pm&:=\tfrac12(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bsig).
\end{align*}
The root is the principal scalar root.  Thus $\bu$ is a
\hyperref[eq:bilinear-unit-vector]{vector of square one};
$\bu\dotprod\bsig$ is \hyperref[eq:involutory-paravector]{self-inverse}.
By squaring,
\begin{align*}
 \pvPi_\pm^2&=\tfrac14(1\pm2\bu\dotprod\bsig+1)=\pvPi_\pm,\\
 \pvPi_+\pvPi_-&=\tfrac14(1-(\bu\dotprod\bsig)^2)=0,\\
 \pvPi_++\pvPi_-&=1,\\
 \pZ&=c(\pvPi_++\pvPi_-)+z(\pvPi_+-\pvPi_-)
   =\lambda_+\pvPi_++\lambda_-\pvPi_-.
\end{align*}
Here $z,\lambda_\pm\in\C$, $\bu\in\C^3$, and
$\pZ,f(\pZ)\in\C\oplus\C^3$ for the analytic functions below.
These projectors are Hermitian when $\bu$ is real; in general they need
not be. The same formulas apply directly to any given
$\pZ=c+z\bu\dotprod\bsig$, with $c,z\in\C$ and $\bu^2=1$.
If $z=0$, $\pZ=c$ is scalar and $f(\pZ)=f(c)$.
The complementary-projector
identities give, for every
nonnegative integer $n$,
\[
 \pZ^n=\lambda_+^n\pvPi_++\lambda_-^n\pvPi_-
 =\tfrac12(\lambda_+^n+\lambda_-^n)
 +\tfrac12(\lambda_+^n-\lambda_-^n)\bu\dotprod\bsig.
\]
For a scalar power series $f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^\infty c_nz^n$ converging at
both eigenvalues, with $c_n\in\C$, inserting these powers into $f(\pZ)=\sum_n c_n\pZ^n$
gives the spectral rule~\citeat{higham-functions}{p.~3}{page=4}
\begin{equation}
 f(\pZ)=f(\lambda_+)\pvPi_++f(\lambda_-)\pvPi_-.
 \label{eq:complex-function-projectors}
\end{equation}
The same rule defines chosen analytic branches at the two eigenvalues.
Only the complementary projector identities are used in the proof, so
the result is valid for any decomposition with these identities.

Adjugation exchanges the eigenvalues, so
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{adj} \pZ&=\lambda_-\pvPi_++\lambda_+\pvPi_-,\\
 \det \pZ&=\lambda_+\lambda_-,\\
 \pZ^{-1}&=\frac{\adj {\pZ}}{\det(\pZ)}
       =\lambda_+^{-1}\pvPi_++\lambda_-^{-1}\pvPi_-.
\end{align*}
The inverse requires both eigenvalues to be nonzero.  Independently choosing
the signs of their principal square roots gives
\begin{equation*}
 \sqrt {\pZ}=\pm\sqrt{\lambda_+}\pvPi_+\pm\sqrt{\lambda_-}\pvPi_-.
\end{equation*}
There are four distinct roots when both eigenvalues are nonzero, and two
when one vanishes.  Changing $z$ to $-z$ exchanges the eigenvalue--projector
pairs and leaves the function unchanged when its branch choices are
exchanged with them.

\subsection{Square roots and repeated eigenvalues}

\subsubsection{Real paravectors and direct square roots}
For real $\pX=a+\bX\dotprod\bsig$, with $a\in\R$, $\bX\in\R^3$, and
$\bX\ne0$, the spectral quantities above reduce to
\[
 z=\lVert\bX\rVert,\qquad
 \bu=\bX/\lVert\bX\rVert,\qquad
 \lambda_\pm=a\pm\lVert\bX\rVert.
\]
Here $\pX,\pvPi_\pm\in\R\oplus\R^3$, $z,\lambda_\pm\in\R$,
and $\bu\in\R^3$. The projectors are now Hermitian; complex-valued
functions or square roots may still give $f(\pX)\in\C\oplus\C^3$.  In particular, for a
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$,
\begin{align*}
 f(\bu\dotprod\bsig)
 &=f(1)\pvPi_++f(-1)\pvPi_-\\
 &=\tfrac12(f(1)+f(-1))
  +\tfrac12(f(1)-f(-1))\bu\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \sqrt{\bu\dotprod\bsig}&=\pm\pvPi_+\pm\ii\pvPi_-.
\end{align*}
The two root signs are independent.  For a fixed analytic branch,
$f(\pX)\to f(a)$ as $\bX\to0$; independent root choices require the
scalar case below.

A direct component derivation also determines all \emph{real} roots.
$\pY=b+\bY\dotprod\bsig$ is taken to be real, with $b\in\R$, $\bY\in\R^3$,
and real roots $\pX=a+\bX\dotprod\bsig$ are sought such that $\pX^2=\pY$.  By equating parts,
\begin{align*}
 a^2+\bX^2&=b,\\
 2a\bX&=\bY.
\end{align*}
If $\bY\ne0$, then $a\ne0$ and $\bX=\bY/(2a)$.  By substitution,
\begin{align}
 a^4-ba^2+\tfrac14\bY^2&=0, \notag\\
 a^2&=\tfrac12\left(b\pm\sqrt{b^2-\bY^2}\right).
 \label{eq:real-square-root-scalar}
\end{align}
For each nonnegative $a^2$ in \eqref{eq:real-square-root-scalar}, both
scalar roots $a$ are included, with
$\bX=\bY/(2a)$; then $\bX^2=b-a^2$.  There are four real roots when
$b>\lVert\bY\rVert$, two when $b=\lVert\bY\rVert>0$, and none when
$b<\lVert\bY\rVert$.

If $\bY=0$, the equations instead give the scalar roots
$\pX=\pm\sqrt b$ and the pure-vector roots $\pX=\bX\dotprod\bsig$ with
$\bX^2=b$ for $b>0$.  For $b=0$ only $\pX=0$ is real; for $b<0$ there
is no real root.

\subsubsection{Coalescing eigenvalues}
For complex $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ with $\bZ^2=0$,
$(\bZ\dotprod\bsig)^2=0$. A scalar function analytic at $c$
has the terminating Taylor expansion~\citeat{higham-functions}{p.~4}{page=5}
\begin{equation}
 f(\pZ)=f(c)+f'(c)\bZ\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:coalescing-function}
\end{equation}
For $c\ne0$, the chosen square root and logarithm are
\begin{align*}
 \sqrt {\pZ}&=r+\frac{\bZ\dotprod\bsig}{2r},
 \\
 \log \pZ&=\log c+\frac{\bZ\dotprod\bsig}{c}.
\end{align*}
Here $r\in\C$ is a chosen scalar root of $c$ ($r^2=c$); a consistent
logarithm branch is used.  A nonzero $\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ with $c=0$ has no square
root: such a root would have only zero eigenvalues, and its quadratic
identity would force its square to vanish.  It has no logarithm because
it is not invertible.  If $\bZ\dotprod\bsig=0$, a fixed analytic
branch gives $f(c)$; non-scalar square roots of a scalar must instead be
found from the component equations $a^2+\bX^2=c$, $2a\bX=0$, now allowing
$a\in\C$ and $\bX\in\C^3$.  Independent branch choices need not remain
finite as distinct eigenvalues coalesce.

\section{Exponentials and elementary functions}
\label{sec:elementary-functions}

Explicit functions and the factors used for rotations and boosts are
obtained from the spectral rule. Algebraic functions are connected to
geometric transformations through composition and logarithms.

\subsection{Exponential: projection and series derivations}
For complex $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ with $\bZ^2\ne0$, the following notation is retained:
$z=+\sqrt{\bZ^2}$, the \hyperref[eq:bilinear-unit-vector]{vector of square one}
$\bu=\bZ/z$, and $\lambda_\pm=c\pm z$.
The spectral rule~\eqref{eq:complex-function-projectors} gives
\begin{align*}
 \exp(\pZ)
 &=\tfrac12e^{c+z}(1+\bu\dotprod\bsig)
  +\tfrac12e^{c-z}(1-\bu\dotprod\bsig)\\
 &=e^c\left(\frac{e^z+e^{-z}}2
      +\frac{e^z-e^{-z}}2\bu\dotprod\bsig\right).
\end{align*}
Independently, $c$ commutes with the vector part and
$(z\bu\dotprod\bsig)^{2n}=z^{2n}$,
$(z\bu\dotprod\bsig)^{2n+1}=z^{2n+1}\bu\dotprod\bsig$.  Separating the
defining series therefore gives
\begin{align*}
 \exp(\pZ)&=e^c\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
      \frac{(z\bu\dotprod\bsig)^n}{n!}\\
 &=e^c\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{2n}}{(2n)!}
       +\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)!}\bu\dotprod\bsig\right).
\end{align*}
Both derivations give~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(52)}{page=12}
\begin{equation}
 \exp(\pZ)=e^c(\cosh z+\sinh z\,\bu\dotprod\bsig).
 \label{eq:paravector-exponential}
\end{equation}
This is independent of the choice $z$ or $-z$: $\cosh$ is even, while
both $\sinh z$ and $\bu$ change sign.  When $\bZ^2=0$,
\eqref{eq:coalescing-function} instead gives
\[
 \exp(c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig)=e^c(1+\bZ\dotprod\bsig).
\]
The defining series also gives
\begin{align*}
 \exp(\pZ^*)&=\exp(\pZ)^*,\\
 \exp(\pZ^{\mathsf H})&=\exp(\pZ)^{\mathsf H}.
\end{align*}

For the real components $a,b,\bX,\bY$ of
$\pZ=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\ii\bY)\dotprod\bsig$, the following form is used:
\[
 z=+\sqrt{\bX^2-\bY^2+2\ii\bX\dotprod\bY}.
\]
Substituting $\bu=(\bX+\ii\bY)/z$ into
\eqref{eq:paravector-exponential} gives the component form
\begin{equation*}
 \exp(\pZ)=e^a(\cos b+\ii\sin b)
 \left(\cosh z+\frac{\sinh z}{z}(\bX+\ii\bY)\dotprod\bsig\right).
\end{equation*}
Its real and imaginary specializations are
\begin{align}
 \exp(a+\bX\dotprod\bsig)
 &=e^a\left(\cosh\lVert\bX\rVert
  +\frac{\sinh\lVert\bX\rVert}{\lVert\bX\rVert}\bX\dotprod\bsig\right),
 \label{eq:real-exponential}\\
 \exp\!\left(\ii(b+\bY\dotprod\bsig)\right)
 &=(\cos b+\ii\sin b)\left(\cos\lVert\bY\rVert
  +\ii\frac{\sin\lVert\bY\rVert}{\lVert\bY\rVert}\bY\dotprod\bsig\right).
 \label{eq:imaginary-exponential}
\end{align}
Setting $a=0$ in \eqref{eq:real-exponential} or $b=0$ in
\eqref{eq:imaginary-exponential} gives the pure-vector exponentials.
Every quotient uses its analytic limit at a zero argument.

\subsection{Rotation and boost factors}
\label{sec:algebra-rotation-boost}
For a general real exponent $a+\bX\dotprod\bsig$, where $a\in\R$ and
$\bX\in\R^3$, \eqref{eq:real-exponential} gives
\begin{equation}
 \det\!\left(\exp(a+\bX\dotprod\bsig)\right)=e^{2a}.
 \label{eq:exponential-determinant}
\end{equation}
Thus a real exponential has determinant one exactly when its scalar
exponent is zero.  Its value still has a nonzero scalar part, supplied
by the hyperbolic cosine.

A real parameter $\theta\in\R$ is used, and $\bu\in\R^3$ is taken to be a
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector},
$\bu^2=1$.  The two special exponentials just derived define the complex
rotation factor $\pR\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3$ and the real boost factor
$\pL\in\R\oplus\R^3$~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(6)}{page=4}\,\citeat{baylis-intro}{p.~23}{page=23}:
\begin{equation}
 \pR(\theta,\bu):=\exp\!\left(-\ii\frac\theta2\bu\dotprod\bsig\right)
 =\cos\frac\theta2-\ii\sin\frac\theta2\bu\dotprod\bsig,
 \label{eq:rotation-exponential}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 \pL(\theta,\bu):=\exp\!\left(-\frac\theta2\bu\dotprod\bsig\right)
 =\cosh\frac\theta2-\sinh\frac\theta2\bu\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:boost-exponential}
\end{equation}
In \eqref{eq:rotation-exponential} and \eqref{eq:boost-exponential},
the parameter is a rotation angle for $\pR$ and a rapidity for $\pL$; both are
real dimensionless numbers.  Here these names specify algebraic objects.
Their actions on a general paravector are derived in
Section~\ref{sec:preserved-quantities}; their physical applications follow in
Sections~\ref{sec:inertial-frames} and~\ref{sec:transformations}.

Because $(\bu\dotprod\bsig)^2=1$, reversing the parameter gives the
inverses
\begin{align}
 \pR^{-1}&=\pR(-\theta,\bu)
 =\cos\frac\theta2+\ii\sin\frac\theta2\bu\dotprod\bsig,
 \notag\\
 \pL^{-1}&=\pL(-\theta,\bu)
 =\cosh\frac\theta2+\sinh\frac\theta2\bu\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:boost-inverse}
\end{align}
Sigma conjugation changes both $\ii$ and $\bsig$ in sign; Hermitian
conjugation changes $\ii$ but fixes a real vector.  Hence
\begin{align}
 \pR^*&=\pR, \notag\\
 \pR^{\mathsf H}&=\pR^{-1}, \label{eq:rotation-H}\\
 \pL^*&=\pL^{-1}, \notag\\
 \pL^{\mathsf H}&=\pL. \label{eq:boost-H}
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{eq:rotation-H} makes $\pR$
\hyperref[eq:unitary-transform]{unitary}; \eqref{eq:boost-H}
makes $\pL$ Hermitian, so $\pL^{\mathsf H}\pL=\pL^2$.  Their determinants and
squared norms follow from the circular and hyperbolic identities:
\begin{align}
 \det \pR&=\cos^2(\theta/2)+\sin^2(\theta/2)=1,
 \label{eq:rotation-determinant}\\
 \det \pL&=\cosh^2(\theta/2)-\sinh^2(\theta/2)=1,
 \label{eq:boost-determinant}\\
 \lVert \pR\rVert^2&=1, \label{eq:rotation-norm}\\
 \lVert \pL\rVert^2&=\cosh\theta. \label{eq:boost-norm}
\end{align}
Equations~\eqref{eq:rotation-determinant} and \eqref{eq:boost-determinant}
make both \hyperref[eq:unit-determinant-paravector]{paravectors of determinant one},
so their adjugates equal their inverses.  Equations~\eqref{eq:rotation-norm}
and \eqref{eq:boost-norm} distinguish their squared norms; only $\pR$ is
unitary for nonzero $\theta$.  The half-angle gives the sign and period:
\begin{align}
 \pR(\theta+2\pi,\bu)&=-\pR(\theta,\bu),
 \label{eq:rotation-two-pi}\\
 \pR(\theta+4\pi,\bu)&=+\pR(\theta,\bu).
 \notag
\end{align}

\paragraph{Gamma form.}
Notation: $\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$ from
\eqref{eq:boost-exponential}.  Its real velocity parameter and
Lorentz factor are defined by
\begin{align*}
 \beta&:=\tanh\theta,
 \\
 \gamma&:=\cosh\theta=\frac1{\sqrt{1-\beta^2}}.
\end{align*}
Here $-1<\beta<1$, $\gamma\ge1$, and $\gamma\beta=\sinh\theta$.
By the half-angle identities,
\begin{align*}
 \cosh\frac\theta2&=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma+1}{2}},\\
 \sinh\frac\theta2&=\frac{\gamma\beta}{\sqrt{2(\gamma+1)}}.
\end{align*}
Hence the boost factor itself is
\begin{equation}
 \pL=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma+1}{2}}
 \left(1-\frac{\gamma\beta}{\gamma+1}\bu\dotprod\bsig\right).
 \label{eq:boost-gamma-form}
\end{equation}
Squaring doubles the exponent, so the full-rapidity paravector is
\begin{equation}
 \pL^2=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig)
 =\gamma(1-\beta\bu\dotprod\bsig).
 \label{eq:boost-square}
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:boost-square} has prefactor $\gamma$; $\pL$ has the half-angle
prefactor in \eqref{eq:boost-gamma-form}.  Equivalently, for any real vector
$\bX\ne0$, $\bv=\tanh\lVert\bX\rVert\,\bX/\lVert\bX\rVert$ and
$\gamma=\cosh\lVert\bX\rVert$ are defined.  Then
\[
 \exp(\bX\dotprod\bsig)=\gamma(1+\bv\dotprod\bsig).
\]
The zero-vector case follows by continuity.  This last real paravector has
\hyperref[eq:unit-determinant-paravector]{determinant one} and represents four-velocity in
Section~\ref{sec:kinematics}.

\subsection{Cayley transform}
$\pY=b+\bY\dotprod\bsig$ is taken to be real, with $b\in\R$, $\bY\in\R^3$, and
$0<\lVert\bY\rVert<b$.  The \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector}
$\bu=\bY/\lVert\bY\rVert$ is used, with
$\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bsig)/2$.  Its sigma conjugate
$\pY^*=b-\bY\dotprod\bsig=\adj {\pY}$ commutes with $\pY$, so
\[
 \frac{\pY}{\pY^*}=\frac{\pY^2}{\det(\pY)}
 =\frac{b^2+\bY^2+2b\bY\dotprod\bsig}{b^2-\bY^2}.
\]
The real parameter $\theta=2\tanh^{-1}(\lVert\bY\rVert/b)$ is defined.
By the double-angle identities,
\begin{align*}
 \cosh\theta&=\frac{b^2+\bY^2}{b^2-\bY^2},\\
 \sinh\theta&=\frac{2b\lVert\bY\rVert}{b^2-\bY^2}.
\end{align*}
Thus the quotient is the full-rapidity exponential
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{b+\bY\dotprod\bsig}{b-\bY\dotprod\bsig}
 &=\cosh\theta+\sinh\theta\,\bu\dotprod\bsig\\
 &=\exp(\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig)
 =e^\theta\pvPi_++e^{-\theta}\pvPi_-.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:cayley-exponential}
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:cayley-exponential} is $\pL(\theta,\bu)^{-2}$ from
\eqref{eq:boost-square}. Its real exponent is written using
$\bX=\theta\bu$, while the Cayley vector is determined by
\begin{equation*}
 \bY=b\tanh\!\left(\frac{\lVert\bX\rVert}{2}\right)
       \frac{\bX}{\lVert\bX\rVert}.
\end{equation*}
Thus $\lVert\bY\rVert/b=\tanh(\theta/2)$, the half-rapidity
parameter.  The corresponding real Lorentz factor and velocity vector are
\begin{align*}
 \gamma&=\frac{b^2+\bY^2}{b^2-\bY^2},\\
 \bv&=\frac{2b\bY}{b^2+\bY^2}.
\end{align*}
They give the equivalent forms
\[
 \exp(\bX\dotprod\bsig)=\gamma(1+\bv\dotprod\bsig)
 =\frac{b+\bY\dotprod\bsig}{b-\bY\dotprod\bsig}=\frac{\pY}{\pY^*}.
\]
Choosing $b=1$ normalizes the scalar part of $\pY$ to one; $\bY=0$ follows by
continuity.

\subsection{Composing the factors}
Notation: $\pL(\theta,\bu)=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$ and
$\pR(\theta,\bu)=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$ from
\eqref{eq:boost-exponential} and \eqref{eq:rotation-exponential}.
For a fixed \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit direction} $\bu$
and real parameters $\theta,\theta'$, the parameters can be added
because the exponents commute:
\begin{equation}
 \pL(\theta,\bu)\pL(\theta',\bu)=\pL(\theta+\theta',\bu),
 \label{eq:boost-composition}
\end{equation}
\begin{align*}
 \pR(\theta,\bu)\pR(\theta',\bu)&=\pR(\theta+\theta',\bu),\\
 \pL(\theta,\bu)\pR(\theta',\bu)&=\pR(\theta',\bu)\pL(\theta,\bu).
\end{align*}
For a common axis, the following complex parameter is defined:
$z=-(\theta+\ii\theta')/2\in\C$.  Its exponential is
\[
 \exp(z\bu\dotprod\bsig)
 =\pL(\theta,\bu)\pR(\theta',\bu)
 =\cosh z+\sinh z\,\bu\dotprod\bsig.
\]
By expansion of the complex scalar coefficients,
\begin{align*}
 \cosh z&=+\cosh\frac\theta2\cos\frac{\theta'}2
 +\ii\sinh\frac\theta2\sin\frac{\theta'}2,\\
 \sinh z&=-\sinh\frac\theta2\cos\frac{\theta'}2
 -\ii\cosh\frac\theta2\sin\frac{\theta'}2.
\end{align*}
This factorization is valid when the real and imaginary vector parts of
the exponent are parallel.  For different \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit directions}
$\bu,\bv$, the vector product
instead gives
\begin{equation}
 \left[-\tfrac12\bu\dotprod\bsig,-\tfrac12\bv\dotprod\bsig\right]
 =\tfrac\ii2(\bu\times\bv)\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:boost-generator-commutator}
\end{equation}
This commutator has the imaginary-vector form used in the exponent of a
rotation; boosts along nonparallel directions cannot be interchanged.

\subsection{Logarithm}
For complex $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ with $\bZ^2\ne0$, the following notation is retained:
$z=+\sqrt{\bZ^2}$, the \hyperref[eq:bilinear-unit-vector]{vector of square one}
$\bu=\bZ/z$, $\lambda_\pm=c\pm z$, and
$\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bsig)/2$.  Both eigenvalues are assumed to be nonzero.
For a nonzero complex scalar $\lambda$ and any integer $n$, the scalar branches are
\[
 \log\lambda=\log\lVert\lambda\rVert+\ii(\arg\lambda+2n\pi).
\]
One branch is chosen for each eigenvalue. By the spectral rule,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \log \pZ&=\log(\lambda_+)\pvPi_++\log(\lambda_-)\pvPi_-\\
 &=\tfrac12\bigl(\log\lambda_++\log\lambda_-\bigr)
 +\tfrac12\bigl(\log\lambda_+-\log\lambda_-\bigr)\bu\dotprod\bsig.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:paravector-logarithm}
\end{equation}
Exchanging $z$ and $-z$ exchanges both eigenvalue--projector pairs and
preserves the same chosen logarithm.  Independent scalar branches give
the different logarithms.

For a \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$,
\eqref{eq:paravector-logarithm} uses
eigenvalues $1$ and $-1$, giving, for integers $n,m$,
\begin{equation*}
 \log(\bu\dotprod\bsig)
 =2n\pi\ii\pvPi_++(2m+1)\pi\ii\pvPi_-.
\end{equation*}
For real $\pY=b+\bY\dotprod\bsig$, with $b\in\R$, $\bY\in\R^3$, and
$b>\lVert\bY\rVert$, both eigenvalues are positive.  Combining their real
logarithms yields
\begin{equation*}
 \log \pY=\tfrac12\log(b^2-\bY^2)
 +\tanh^{-1}\!\left(\frac{\lVert\bY\rVert}{b}\right)
       \frac{\bY}{\lVert\bY\rVert}\dotprod\bsig.
\end{equation*}
The vector term vanishes at $\bY=0$; the general coalescing case
was treated above.

\section{Transformations and invariants}
\label{sec:preserved-quantities}

Here $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ is a complex paravector: $c\in\C$ and
$\bZ\in\C^3$, hence $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$.
Transformation factors $\pT,\pT'\in\C\oplus\C^3$ are constant.
A distinction is made between the scalar part $\scpart {\pZ}=c=\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}\pZ$
from \eqref{eq:trace-scalar}, the complex determinant $c^2-\bZ^2$ from
\eqref{eq:det}, and the squared norm $\scpart{\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ}$ from
\eqref{eq:hermitian-norm}.  Scalar part and trace carry exactly the same
information; $\operatorname{sc}$ is used below.

\subsection{Similarity, determinant, and norm}

For a complex \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} $\pT$,
similarity means $\pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{-1}$.
By scalar-part cyclicity,
\begin{equation*}
 \scpart{\pZ'}=\scpart{\pT \pZ \pT^{-1}}
 =\scpart{\pZ \pT^{-1}\pT}=\scpart {\pZ}.
\end{equation*}
Thus trace is also preserved.  By multiplicativity,
\begin{equation}
 \det \pZ'=\det(\pT)\det(\pZ)\det \pT^{-1}=\det(\pZ).
 \label{eq:det-similarity}
\end{equation}
The opposite similarity $\pZ'=\pT^{-1}\pZ \pT$ has the same invariants.
For independent complex factors $\pT,\pT'$, the law $\pZ'=\pT'\pZ \pT$ instead gives
\begin{equation*}
 \det \pZ'=\det(\pT')\det(\pT)\det(\pZ).
\end{equation*}
This preserves the determinant for
every $\pZ$ exactly when $\det(\pT')\det \pT=1$.  This alone does not preserve
scalar part, norm, or the real paravector subspace.

If the factor $\pT$ is unitary, then $\pT^{\mathsf H}=\pT^{-1}$ by
\eqref{eq:unitary-transform}.  For $\pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}$, cancellation and
scalar-part cyclicity give
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \lVert \pZ'\rVert^2
 &=\scpart{\pT \pZ^{\mathsf H}\pT^{\mathsf H}\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}}\\
 &=\scpart{\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}\pT}=\lVert \pZ\rVert^2.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
The norm is also preserved by $\pZ'=\pT \pZ$ and $\pZ'=\pZ \pT$.
The congruence additionally preserves scalar part and determinant;
a one-sided unitary product need not.

\subsubsection{Determinant one is not Hermitian unitarity}
\label{sec:determinant-versus-unitarity}

Invertibility, determinant one, and Hermitian unitarity are distinct
conditions. Invertibility means that a two-sided inverse exists, as in
\eqref{eq:unit-paravector}; determinant one means $\det\pZ=1$; and
Hermitian unitarity means $\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ=1$, as in
\eqref{eq:unitary-transform}. The phrase \emph{unit determinant} means
only that the determinant equals one. It does not mean unit norm or
Hermitian unitarity.

Determinant one implies invertibility, but does not imply Hermitian unitarity.
For comparison, a real paravector $\pX=a+\bX\dotprod\bsig$
with $a\in\R$, $\bX\in\R^3$, and $a^2-\bX^2=1$ is considered.  Then
\[
 \pX^*\pX=\det\pX=1,\qquad
 \pX^{-1}=\pX^*=a-\bX\dotprod\bsig.
\]
Thus determinant one implies invertibility.  Hermitian conjugation fixes
$\pX$, whereas sigma conjugation reverses its vector part, so
\begin{align*}
 \pX^{\mathsf H}\pX&=\pX^2=a^2+\bX^2+2a\bX\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \lVert\pX\rVert^2&=a^2+\bX^2=1+2\bX^2.
\end{align*}
Both equal $1$ only when $\bX=0$, in which case $\pX=\pm1$.
Choosing any nonzero real $\bX$ and $a=\sqrt{1+\bX^2}$ therefore gives
an invertible paravector of determinant one that is not unitary.
These properties follow from the defining equations, not from the
letter used for the paravector.

\subsection{Real paravectors and the Lorentz metric}

$\pX=a+\bX\dotprod\bsig$ is taken to be real, with $a\in\R$ and
$\bX\in\R^3$.  The determinant and squared norm give different quadratic forms:
\begin{align*}
 \det \pX&=a^2-\bX^2,\\
 \lVert \pX\rVert^2&=a^2+\bX^2.
\end{align*}
The first is the Lorentz quadratic form of signature $(+,-,-,-)$;
the second is the positive sum of squared coefficients.  A real-linear
isometry of the first form must preserve the real subspace and its
determinant.  For a constant complex
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} $\pT$ with $\det \pT=1$,
the following Hermitian congruence is used~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(17)}{page=6}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pX'=\pT \pX \pT^{\mathsf H}.}
 \label{eq:general-lorentz-congruence}
\end{equation}
Hermitian conjugation and determinant multiplication give
\begin{align*}
 \pX'^{\mathsf H}&=\pT \pX^{\mathsf H}\pT^{\mathsf H}=\pX',\\
 \det \pX'&=\det(\pT)\det(\pX)\det \pT^{\mathsf H}=\det(\pX).
\end{align*}
For a second real paravector $\pY=b+\bY\dotprod\bsig$, with $b\in\R$
and $\bY\in\R^3$, the associated metric pairing is~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(15)}{page=6}
\begin{equation}
 \scpart{\pX^*\pY}=ab-\bX\dotprod\bY
 =\tfrac12\bigl(\det(\pX+\pY)-\det(\pX)-\det(\pY)\bigr).
 \label{eq:lorentz-pairing}
\end{equation}
Linearity and determinant invariance therefore give
$\scpart{\pX'^*\pY'}=\scpart{\pX^*\pY}$: the full metric is preserved.
These maps preserve the positive cone as well.  For
$a\geq\lVert\bX\rVert$, the eigenvalues
$a\pm\lVert\bX\rVert$ are nonnegative, so $\pX$ has a real square root and
\[
 \scpart{\pX'}=\lVert\sqrt {\pX}\,\pT^{\mathsf H}\rVert^2\geq0.
\]
Together with determinant invariance, this keeps $\pX'$ in the cone
$a\geq\lVert\bX\rVert$.  These are the proper Lorentz transformations
preserving that cone; $\pT$ and $-\pT$ give the same map.

\paragraph{Why the scalar exponent vanishes.}
More generally, the congruence multiplies $\det(\pX)$ by
$\lVert\det(\pT)\rVert^2$.  For real $\pT=\exp(a+\bX\dotprod\bsig)$,
\eqref{eq:exponential-determinant} therefore gives
\[
 \det(\pT \pX \pT^{\mathsf H})=e^{4a}\det(\pX).
\]
A nonzero real scalar exponent dilates the metric; it is excluded by
determinant invariance.  An imaginary scalar exponent contributes a phase
$e^{\ii b}$, $b\in\R$, which cancels between $\pT$ and $\pT^{\mathsf H}$.
Requiring $\det \pT=1$ removes this continuous phase freedom.
Thus a pure boost needs only a real vector exponent.

\paragraph{Why the squared norm is different.}
For $\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$, with real $\theta$ and real unit
$\bu$, applying \eqref{eq:general-lorentz-congruence} to $\pX=1$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \pX'&=\pL^2=\cosh\theta-\sinh\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \det \pX'&=1,\\
 \scpart{\pX'}&=\cosh\theta,\\
 \lVert \pX'\rVert^2&=\cosh(2\theta).
\end{align*}
A nonzero boost preserves the determinant while changing the scalar part
and squared norm.  In fact, a determinant-one congruence preserves the
squared norm for every real $\pX$ exactly when $\pT$ is
\hyperref[eq:unitary-transform]{unitary}.  Necessity follows by taking
$\pX=1$: the real, positive $\pT \pT^{\mathsf H}$ can have both determinant and
norm one only if it is $1$, which makes $\pT$ unitary.  Sufficiency was proved above.
Normalizing $\pL$ to $\lVert \pL\rVert=1$ would change its determinant
and lose the Lorentz metric.

\paragraph{Every factor separates into a boost and a rotation.}
For any complex $\pT$, the Hermitian product rule gives
\[
 (\pT\pT^{\mathsf H})^{\mathsf H}
 =(\pT^{\mathsf H})^{\mathsf H}\pT^{\mathsf H}
 =\pT\pT^{\mathsf H}.
\]
By \eqref{eq:algebra-H}, a paravector equals its Hermitian conjugate
exactly when its scalar and vector coefficients are real. Thus
$\pT\pT^{\mathsf H}\in\R\oplus\R^3$, without any determinant constraint.
For a determinant-one complex $\pT$, i.e., $\det(\pT)=1$,
this real paravector also has determinant one and positive scalar part
$\lVert\pT\rVert^2$. Both eigenvalues are therefore positive.
Using the positive real square root,
\[
 \pL=\sqrt{\pT\pT^{\mathsf H}},\qquad
 \pR=\pL^{-1}\pT,\qquad \pT=\pL\pR.
\]
The factors satisfy
\[
 \det\pL=\det\pR=1,\qquad
 \pL^{\mathsf H}=\pL,\qquad
 \pR^{\mathsf H}=\pR^{-1},
\]
with $\pL\in\R\oplus\R^3$ and $\pR\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3$.
The real logarithm of $\pL$ has zero scalar part by
\eqref{eq:exponential-determinant}, so $\pL$ is a real vector exponential.
For $\pR$, the identity $\pR^{\mathsf H}=\pR^{-1}=\adj {\pR}$ makes its scalar
coefficient real and its vector coefficient imaginary; determinant one
then gives $\pR=\cos(\theta/2)-\ii\sin(\theta/2)\bu\dotprod\bsig$
for some real $\theta$ and real unit $\bu$.
Consequently,
\[
 \pX'=\pT\pX\pT^{\mathsf H}
 =\pL\pR\pX\pR^{\mathsf H}\pL,\qquad
 \det\pX'=\det\pX.
\]
The rotation is followed by the boost. Their component actions follow next.

\subsection{Rotation action and Rodrigues' formula}
\label{sec:algebra-rotation-action}

Notation: the unitary factor
$\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$ from
\eqref{eq:rotation-exponential}, with real angle $\theta$ and
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit axis} $\bu$.
For the complex paravector $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$, the following Hermitian congruence is used~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(5)}{page=4}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pZ'=\pR \pZ \pR^{\mathsf H}.}
 \label{eq:rotation-action}
\end{equation}
Since $\pR^{\mathsf H}=\pR^{-1}$ by \eqref{eq:rotation-H}, this keeps $c'=c$.
The vector product
\eqref{eq:vector-product} gives
\begin{align*}
 (\bu\dotprod\bsig)(\bZ\dotprod\bsig)(\bu\dotprod\bsig)
 &=2(\bu\dotprod\bZ)\bu\dotprod\bsig-\bZ\dotprod\bsig,\\
 [\bZ\dotprod\bsig,\bu\dotprod\bsig]
 &=-2\ii(\bu\times\bZ)\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
Inserting the sine and cosine forms of $\pR$ and $\pR^{\mathsf H}$ therefore yields
\begin{align*}
 \bZ'\dotprod\bsig
 &={}\cos^2\frac\theta2\,\bZ\dotprod\bsig
 +2\sin\frac\theta2\cos\frac\theta2
       (\bu\times\bZ)\dotprod\bsig\\
 &\quad+\sin^2\frac\theta2
 \left(2(\bu\dotprod\bZ)\bu\dotprod\bsig-\bZ\dotprod\bsig\right).
\end{align*}
Rodrigues' formula is obtained by collecting terms~\citeat{baylis-intro}{p.~14}{page=14}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bZ'=\bZ\cos\theta+(\bu\times\bZ)\sin\theta
 +(\bu\dotprod\bZ)(1-\cos\theta)\bu.}
 \label{eq:rodrigues}
\end{equation}
This is the active right-handed rotation through $+\theta$ about $\bu$.
Rotating the coordinate axes through $+\theta$ instead uses the inverse map.
It preserves $\operatorname{sc}$, determinant, norm, and the real
paravector subspace. For complex $\bZ$, it rotates its real and imaginary
parts by the same real rotation.  The sign change in
\eqref{eq:rotation-two-pi} affects both factors in
\eqref{eq:rotation-action}, so $\pZ'$ has period $2\pi$.

\subsection{Quaternions as paravectors}
\label{sec:quaternions}

The quaternion multiplication rules are satisfied by $(-\ii\pvsigma_k)$:
\begin{gather*}
 (-\ii\pvsigma_1)^2=(-\ii\pvsigma_2)^2=(-\ii\pvsigma_3)^2
 =(-\ii\pvsigma_1)(-\ii\pvsigma_2)(-\ii\pvsigma_3)=-1,\\[1em]
 (-\ii\pvsigma_1)(-\ii\pvsigma_2)=(-\ii\pvsigma_3),\\
 (-\ii\pvsigma_2)(-\ii\pvsigma_3)=(-\ii\pvsigma_1),\\
 (-\ii\pvsigma_3)(-\ii\pvsigma_1)=(-\ii\pvsigma_2).
\end{gather*}
The sign is reversed when two distinct factors are exchanged.
Here $\ii$ remains the commuting complex scalar.

For $a\in\R$ and $\bQ\in\R^3$, the quaternion representation is~\citeat{baylis-applications}{p.~92}{page=103}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pQ:=a+\bQ\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)=a-\ii\bQ\dotprod\bsig.}
 \label{eq:quaternion-paravector}
\end{equation}
Here $\bQ\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)=Q_1(-\ii\pvsigma_1)+Q_2(-\ii\pvsigma_2)+Q_3(-\ii\pvsigma_3)$.
In the decomposition $\pZ=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\ii\bY)\dotprod\bsig$,
$b=0$, $\bX=0$, and $\bY=-\bQ$ are selected.
Thus $\pQ,\pQ'\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3$: a real scalar coefficient and purely
imaginary vector coefficients. This four-real-component form is
preserved under addition and multiplication.
Indeed, for $\pQ'=a'+\bQ'\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)$, the paravector product becomes~\citeat{lounesto-intro}{p.~9}{page=22}
\begin{equation}
 \pQ \pQ'=(aa'-\bQ\dotprod\bQ')
 +(a\bQ'+a'\bQ+\bQ\times\bQ')\dotprod(-\ii\bsig).
 \label{eq:quaternion-product}
\end{equation}

Since $(-\ii\pvsigma_k)^{\mathsf H}=-(-\ii\pvsigma_k)$ and
$(-\ii\pvsigma_k)^*=(-\ii\pvsigma_k)$, quaternion conjugation
is the existing Hermitian conjugation:
\begin{align}
 \pQ^{\mathsf H}&=a-\bQ\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)
 =a+\ii\bQ\dotprod\bsig,\notag\\
 \pQ^*&=\pQ,\qquad \operatorname{adj} \pQ=\pQ^{\mathsf H},\notag\\
 \pQ \pQ^{\mathsf H}&=\pQ^{\mathsf H}\pQ=a^2+\bQ^2
 =\det \pQ=\lVert \pQ\rVert^2.
 \label{eq:quaternion-norm}
\end{align}
For nonzero $\pQ$, \eqref{eq:quaternion-norm} gives
$\pQ^{-1}=\pQ^{\mathsf H}/(a^2+\bQ^2)$.
Nonzero quaternions are normalized with
\[
 \pQ\leftarrow\frac{\pQ}{\sqrt{\pQ \pQ^{\mathsf H}}},
\]
which, with $\lVert\bQ\rVert^2=\bQ^2$ for real $\bQ$, gives
\[
 \pQ \pQ^{\mathsf H}=a^2+\lVert\bQ\rVert^2=1.
\]
Thus a unit quaternion has $\pQ^{-1}=\pQ^{\mathsf H}$.

For rotation, $\br\in\R^3$ is represented by the pure quaternion
$\br\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)=-\ii\br\dotprod\bsig$.
For a unit quaternion $\pQ=a+\bQ\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)$, the quaternion sandwich is used:
\begin{equation}
 \br'\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)=\pQ(\br\dotprod(-\ii\bsig))\pQ^{\mathsf H},
 \qquad
 \br'\dotprod\bsig=\pQ(\br\dotprod\bsig)\pQ^{\mathsf H}.
 \label{eq:quaternion-rotation}
\end{equation}
The two forms agree because $-\ii$ commutes with every paravector.
Applying \eqref{eq:quaternion-product} twice gives
\begin{align*}
 \pQ(\br\dotprod(-\ii\bsig))
 &=-\bQ\dotprod\br+(a\br+\bQ\times\br)\dotprod(-\ii\bsig),\\
 \pQ(\br\dotprod(-\ii\bsig))\pQ^{\mathsf H}
 &=\bigl((a^2-\bQ^2)\br+2(\bQ\dotprod\br)\bQ
          +2a\bQ\times\br\bigr)\dotprod(-\ii\bsig).
\end{align*}
The scalar part cancels. For a unit quaternion with $\bQ\ne0$, its
real unit axis $\bu$ and rotation angle $\theta$ satisfy
\[
 \bu=\frac{\bQ}{\lVert\bQ\rVert},\qquad
 a=\cos\frac\theta2,\qquad \bQ=\bu\sin\frac\theta2.
\]
Thus the quaternion is the rotation factor
\[
 \pQ=\cos\frac\theta2-\ii\bu\dotprod\bsig\sin\frac\theta2=\pR.
\]
Then $a^2-\bQ^2=\cos\theta$,
$2a\bQ=\bu\sin\theta$, and
$2(\bQ\dotprod\br)\bQ=(1-\cos\theta)(\bu\dotprod\br)\bu$.
Consequently \eqref{eq:quaternion-rotation} recovers
\[
 \boxed{\br'=\br\cos\theta+(\bu\times\br)\sin\theta
       +(\bu\dotprod\br)(1-\cos\theta)\bu.}
\]
This is precisely Rodrigues' formula~\eqref{eq:rodrigues} for
$\bZ=\br$.  Thus $\pR=\pQ$, and the quaternion sandwich is exactly
$\pR(\br\dotprod\bsig)\pR^{\mathsf H}$ from \eqref{eq:rotation-action}.
The reversed sandwich $\pQ^{\mathsf H}(\br\dotprod\bsig)\pQ$ gives the inverse rotation.

\subsection{Boost action and its sign convention}
\label{sec:algebra-boost-action}

Notation: $\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$ from
\eqref{eq:boost-exponential}; $\theta\in\R$, $\bu\in\R^3$, $\bu^2=1$,
$\beta=\tanh\theta$, and $\gamma=\cosh\theta$.
The same congruence is used with $\pT=\pL$ and $\pL^{\mathsf H}=\pL$ from \eqref{eq:boost-H}~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(24)}{page=20}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pZ'=\pL \pZ \pL.}
 \label{eq:boost-action}
\end{equation}
The paravector $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig\in\C\oplus\C^3$ may be complex;
the congruence also preserves its real subspace.
$\bZ\in\C^3$ is resolved into parallel and perpendicular parts in $\C^3$:
\begin{align*}
 \bZ_{\parallel}&:=(\bu\dotprod\bZ)\bu,\\
 \bZ_{\perp}&:=\bZ-\bZ_{\parallel}.
\end{align*}
The parallel term commutes with $\bu\dotprod\bsig$; the perpendicular term
anticommutes with it.  Consequently,
\begin{align*}
 \pL(c+\bZ_{\parallel}\dotprod\bsig)\pL
 &=(c+\bZ_{\parallel}\dotprod\bsig)\pL^2,\\
 \pL(\bZ_{\perp}\dotprod\bsig)\pL
 &=\bZ_{\perp}\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
\Needspace{7\baselineskip}
Using $\pL^2=\gamma(1-\beta\bu\dotprod\bsig)$ from
\eqref{eq:boost-square}, multiplication gives~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.1)}{page=104}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{c'=\gamma(c-\beta\bu\dotprod\bZ),}
 \label{eq:boost-scalar-component}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bZ'=\bZ_{\perp}+\gamma(\bZ_{\parallel}-\beta c\bu).}
 \label{eq:boost-vector-component}
\end{equation}
The equivalent longitudinal combinations expose the invariant directly:
\begin{equation}
 c'\pm\bu\dotprod\bZ'=e^{\mp\theta}(c\pm\bu\dotprod\bZ).
 \label{eq:boost-null-components}
\end{equation}
Their product is $c^2-(\bu\dotprod\bZ)^2$; the transverse part is unchanged.
Thus \eqref{eq:boost-null-components} preserves $c^2-\bZ^2$ and, for real
$\pZ$, the null cone $c^2-\bZ^2=0$.

The inverse map is $\pZ=\pL^{-1}\pZ'\pL^{-1}$, obtained by reversing $\theta$.
Generally $\pL \pZ\ne \pZ \pL$: the scalar and parallel parts commute with $\pL$,
while a nonzero perpendicular part does not for a nonzero boost.
For any transverse vector $\bZ_\perp$ with $\bu\dotprod\bZ_\perp=0$,
\[
 \pL(\bZ_\perp\dotprod\bsig)
 =(\bZ_\perp\dotprod\bsig)\pL^{-1},\qquad
 \pL(\bZ_\perp\dotprod\bsig)\pL=\bZ_\perp\dotprod\bsig.
\]
Thus the two-sided action leaves every transverse component unchanged.
For real inputs, the right factor cancels the imaginary transverse term
introduced by the left factor. A single left factor, even at full rapidity,
cannot represent the boost of every real paravector.

\paragraph{Similarity action of the boost factor.}
For $\pZ'=\pL \pZ \pL^{-1}$, the same commutation rules give $c'=c$ and
\begin{equation}
 \bZ'=\bZ_{\parallel}
 +\gamma\bigl(\bZ_{\perp}-\ii\beta\bu\times\bZ\bigr).
 \label{eq:boost-similarity-vector}
\end{equation}
This preserves scalar part and determinant, but fixes $\pZ=1$ and generally
makes a real vector complex.  It does not mix scalar and vector components
as the congruence does in
\eqref{eq:boost-scalar-component}--\eqref{eq:boost-vector-component}.
In particular, similarity preserves the scalar-free subspace;
a nonzero boost congruence gives a nonzero scalar part to
$\pZ=z\bu\dotprod\bsig$ for any nonzero $z\in\C$.

\section{Space--time kinematics}
\label{sec:kinematics}

In an inertial frame, an event is $\pX=t+\br\dotprod\bsig\in\R\oplus\R^3$.
A massive particle is described by a future-directed time-like path $\br(t)$,
with coordinate velocity $\bv=\dot\br$ and $\bv^2<1$.
Coordinate time is denoted by $t$; the carried clock's proper time is
represented by $s$ in the natural units used here.
Dots denote total $t$ derivatives of scalars, vectors, and paravectors,
even when a path is parametrized by $s$.

\subsection{Intervals, light cones, and proper time}
\label{sec:proper-time}

The displacement and its Minkowski interval are~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(7)}{page=8}
\begin{align}
 \dd\pX&=\dd t+\dd\br\dotprod\bsig,\notag\\
 \det(\dd\pX)&=\dd\pX\,\dd\pX^*=\dd t^2-\dd\br^2.
 \label{eq:interval}
\end{align}
The interval is positive, zero, or negative for time-like, null, or
space-like displacements. Along a massive particle's path it equals
$\dd s^2$; a null path has no elapsed proper time.
By \eqref{eq:general-lorentz-congruence}, a constant complex
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} $\pT$ with
\hyperref[eq:unit-determinant-paravector]{$\det \pT=1$} gives
\[
 \dd \pX'=\pT\dd \pX \pT^{\mathsf H},
 \qquad \det(\dd \pX')=\det(\dd\pX).
\]
For a rotation, $\pX'=\pR\pX\pR^{\mathsf H}$ leaves $t'=t$ and
acts on $\br$ by Rodrigues' formula~\eqref{eq:rodrigues}.
For real displacements, the product also gives
\begin{align*}
 [\dd \pX,\dd \pX']&=2\ii(\dd\br\times\dd\br')\dotprod\bsig,\\
 (\dd \pX)^{-1}&=\frac{\dd \pX^*}{\det(\dd\pX)},\quad\mathrm{if}\quad\det(\dd\pX)\ne0.
\end{align*}
A null interval has no inverse.

\paragraph{Light cones.}
For a finite real event separation $\Delta \pX=\Delta t+\Delta\br\dotprod\bsig$,
the eigenvalues are $\Delta t\pm\lVert\Delta\br\rVert$.
Both are nonnegative precisely in the future causal cone:
\begin{equation}
 \Delta t\ge\lVert\Delta\br\rVert.
 \label{eq:future-light-cone}
\end{equation}
The future null boundary has $\det(\Delta \pX)=0$ and $\Delta t\ge0$; for a
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$, its nonzero future rays are
\[
 \Delta \pX=\Delta t(1+\bu\dotprod\bsig),
 \qquad \Delta t>0,\quad\bu^2=1.
\]
The Hermitian congruence~\eqref{eq:general-lorentz-congruence} preserves
nonnegativity of the eigenvalues and the determinant, hence the cone and its
boundary.  For a future time-like or null path,
\[
 \lVert\Delta\br\rVert
 =\left\lVert\int\bv\dd t\right\rVert
 \le\int\lVert\bv\rVert\dd t\le\Delta t.
\]
Its endpoints therefore satisfy \eqref{eq:future-light-cone}; a space-like
separation cannot carry a causal signal
\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~118}{page=124}.

\paragraph{Coordinate time and proper time.}
The coordinate $t$ is read from synchronized clocks of the chosen inertial
frame. Along a future-directed time-like path, $\dd t>0$ and the positive
spacetime arc-length increment $\dd s=\sqrt{\det(\dd\pX)}$ are chosen.
Proper time is commonly denoted by $\tau$.

\begin{SIunits}{Proper time}
Reverting to SI units, with
$s$ measured in metres and $\tau$ in seconds,
\[
 \dd s=c\,\dd\tau.
\]
\end{SIunits}

With $c=1$, the same elapsed quantity is represented by $s$ and $\tau$;
only $s$ is used for proper time below. This interpretation is fixed by
the positive interval normalization, not by an arbitrary choice of path
parameter. Null paths have $\dd s=0$ and no proper-time parametrization;
space-like paths have no real proper-time increment
\citeat{tong-relativity}{pp.~133--134}{page=139}.

Dividing by $\dd t$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \dot{s}=\frac{\dd s}{\dd t}
 &=\frac{\sqrt{\dd t^2-\dd\br^2}}{\dd t}\\
 &=\sqrt{\frac{\dd t^2-\dd\br^2}{\dd t^2}}\\
 &=\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{\dd\br}{\dd t}\right)^2}\\
 &=\sqrt{1-\dot{\br}^2}=\sqrt{1-\bv^2}.
\end{align*}
The Lorentz factor and the derivative conversion are therefore~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.18)}{page=139}
\begin{align}
 \gamma&:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\bv^2}}=\frac{\dd t}{\dd s},
 \label{eq:lorentz-factor}\\
 \frac{\dd}{\dd s}&=\gamma\frac{\dd}{\dd t}.
 \label{eq:proper-time-derivative}
\end{align}
The coordinate-time and proper-time rates coincide only at rest in the
chosen frame.

\paragraph{Elapsed time and clock comparison.}
Between coordinate times $t_0$ and $t$ the clock records~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.19)}{page=140}
\begin{equation}
 s(t)-s(t_0)=\int_{t_0}^{t}\frac{\dd t'}{\gamma(t')}
 =\int_{t_0}^{t}\sqrt{1-\bv(t')^2}\dd t'.
 \label{eq:elapsed-proper-time}
\end{equation}
Fixed, time-like separated events are denoted by $\pX_0=\pX(s_0)$ and $\pX_1=\pX(s_1)$, with $\pX_1$ to the future of $\pX_0$.
The decomposition $\pX_k=t_k+\br_k\dotprod\bsig$ is used for $k=0,1$.
In their separation's rest frame, $\br_1=\br_0$ and
$t_1-t_0=\sqrt{\det(\pX_1-\pX_0)}$.  Since $\sqrt{1-\bv^2}\le1$,
\eqref{eq:elapsed-proper-time} gives~\citeat{tong-em}{p.~122}{page=130}
\begin{equation}
 s_1-s_0\le\sqrt{\det(\pX_1-\pX_0)}.
 \label{eq:maximum-proper-time}
\end{equation}
Equality requires the straight inertial path.  The determinant of a finite
displacement therefore does not generally equal the squared elapsed
proper time of an accelerated clock.

For a return trip lasting real coordinate time $T>0$, velocities
$+\beta\bu$ and $-\beta\bu$ are maintained for $T/2$ each, with
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$ and
$0<\beta<1$.  The ideal instantaneous reversal is the limit of a short
smooth turn.  By the traveling clock, the following interval is recorded:
\[
 s(T)-s(0)=T\sqrt{1-\beta^2},
\]
while $T$ is recorded by the clock at rest.  This is the twin-clock comparison
\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~121}{page=127}.

\subsection{Four-velocity and worldlines}
\label{sec:worldlines}

The real four-velocity is the proper-time tangent~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(8)}{page=8}:
\begin{equation}
 \pU:=\frac{\dd\pX}{\dd s}
 =\frac{\dd t}{\dd s}\left(1+\dot{\br}\dotprod\bsig\right)
 =\gamma\left(1+\bv\dotprod\bsig\right).
 \label{eq:proper-velocity}
\end{equation}
Its normalization and conjugates are
\begin{equation}
 \pU^*\pU=\det\pU=\frac{\det(\dd\pX)}{\dd s^2}=1,
 \qquad \pU^{\mathsf H}=\pU,\qquad
 \pU^{-1}=\pU^*=\gamma\left(1-\bv\dotprod\bsig\right).
 \label{eq:proper-velocity-star-normalization}
\end{equation}
Determinant one is distinct from Hermitian unitarity:
\[
 \pU^{\mathsf H}\pU=\pU^2
 =2\gamma^2-1+2\gamma^2\bv\dotprod\bsig,\qquad
 \lVert\pU\rVert^2=2\gamma^2-1.
\]
Both equal $1$ only at rest. Thus four-velocity has determinant one in
every inertial frame but is Hermitian unitary only in its rest frame.
For a \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit direction} $\bu$, $\bv=\beta\bu$ and
$\theta=\tanh^{-1}\beta$ are used.  Equation~\eqref{eq:boost-square} gives $\pU=\pL^{-2}$ for
$\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$: the direct boost $\pL \pU \pL=1$
takes this tangent to rest.

\paragraph{Integrating four-velocity.}
Integrating $\pU=\dd\pX/\dd s$ between $s_0<s_1$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \pX(s_1)-\pX(s_0)=\int_{s_0}^{s_1}\pU(s)\dd s.
 \label{eq:worldline-integral}
\end{equation}
Its real components are
\begin{align*}
 t(s_1)-t(s_0)&=\int_{s_0}^{s_1}\gamma(s)\dd s,\\
 \br(s_1)-\br(s_0)&=\int_{s_0}^{s_1}\gamma(s)\bv(s)\dd s.
\end{align*}
The path is determined by the velocity history; an endpoint boost alone is insufficient.

\subsection{Fixed-axis motion and rapidity}
\label{sec:fixed-axis}
For a fixed real unit vector $\bu$, the signed velocity along the axis
is $\beta=\tanh\theta$, with $\bv=\beta\bu$ and $\gamma=\cosh\theta$.
With the projectors $\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bsig)/2$
from \eqref{eq:projectors}, the four-velocity and longitudinal rest
direction have equivalent hyperbolic, exponential, and projected forms:
\begin{align*}
 \pU&=\cosh\theta+\sinh\theta\,\bu\dotprod\bsig\\
 &=\exp(\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig)
 =e^\theta\pvPi_++e^{-\theta}\pvPi_-,\\
 \pN&=\sinh\theta+\cosh\theta\,\bu\dotprod\bsig\\
 &=(\bu\dotprod\bsig)\exp(\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig)
 =e^\theta\pvPi_+-e^{-\theta}\pvPi_-.
\end{align*}
Here $\det\pU=1$, $\det\pN=-1$, and $\scpart{\pU^*\pN}=0$.
The pair is an orthonormal time and spatial basis in the plane of motion.
Although rapidity is given as $\theta(s)$, its coordinate-time derivatives
are those of the composition $\theta(s(t))$.

\subsection{Constant velocity}
\label{sec:constant-velocity}
Subscript $0$ denotes the initial event $t=s=0$.
For $\bv(t)=\bv_0$, $\bv_0^2<1$, and $\br(0)=\br_0$,
\[
 \gamma_0=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\bv_0^2}},\qquad
 \pX(t)=t+\left(\br_0+\bv_0t\right)\dotprod\bsig.
\]
With $s(0)=0$, the clock integral gives $s=t/\gamma_0$. Hence
\begin{equation}
 \pX(s)=\br_0\dotprod\bsig+s\pU_0,\qquad
 \pU_0=\gamma_0\left(1+\bv_0\dotprod\bsig\right).
 \label{eq:constant-velocity-worldline}
\end{equation}
This straight time-like worldline has $\dot{\bv}=0$ and
$\dot{\pU}=\dd\pU/\dd s=0$. For any two events on this
inertial path, $\Delta\pX=\Delta s\,\pU_0$ and $\det\pU_0=1$ give
\[
 \det(\Delta\pX)=(\Delta s)^2
 =(\Delta t)^2(1-\bv_0^2).
\]
In the particle's rest frame, $\bv_0=0$, so $\pU_0=1$ and $s=t$.

\section{Inertial frames and particle dynamics}
\label{sec:inertial-frames}

Constant Lorentz transformations are applied to coordinates, clocks, and
particle quantities. The proper time $s$ defined in
Section~\ref{sec:proper-time} is invariant; $t,t'$ are frame-dependent
coordinate times. Dots continue to denote total $t$ derivatives.

\subsection{Coordinates, intervals, and light cones}
\label{sec:boosts}

Notation: $\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$, with real rapidity $\theta$,
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$, $\beta=\tanh\theta$, and $\gamma=\cosh\theta$.
Here $\gamma$ is the factor for frame velocity $\beta\bu$.
The gamma form~\eqref{eq:boost-gamma-form} and its square
\eqref{eq:boost-square} are the same exponential at half and full rapidity.
For real $\pX=t+\br\dotprod\bsig$, coincident inertial origins are chosen at
$t=t'=0$.  Equation~\eqref{eq:general-lorentz-congruence} with $\pT=\pL$
gives $\pX'=\pL \pX \pL$: the primed frame moves at $+\beta\bu$.
Equations~\eqref{eq:boost-scalar-component}--\eqref{eq:boost-vector-component}
become~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.1)}{page=104}
\begin{align}
 t'&=\gamma(t-\beta\bu\dotprod\br), \label{eq:lorentz-time}\\
 \br'&=\br_{\perp}+\gamma(\br_{\parallel}-\beta t\bu),
 \label{eq:lorentz-position}
\end{align}
where $\br_{\parallel}=(\bu\dotprod\br)\bu$ and
$\br_{\perp}=\br-\br_{\parallel}$.
In particular, $\br=\beta t\bu$ maps to $\br'=0$, fixing the physical
meaning of the sign.  Reversing $\theta$ as in \eqref{eq:boost-inverse}
gives $\pX=\pL^{-1}\pX'\pL^{-1}$.

Specializing \eqref{eq:boost-null-components} to the real event $\pX$ gives~\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~154}{page=160}
\begin{equation}
 t'\pm\bu\dotprod\br'=e^{\mp\theta}(t\pm\bu\dotprod\br).
 \label{eq:boost-null-coordinates}
\end{equation}
Multiplying the two signs in \eqref{eq:boost-null-coordinates} and using
$\br'_{\perp}=\br_{\perp}$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 t'^2-\br'^2
 =(t'+\bu\dotprod\br')(t'-\bu\dotprod\br')-\br_{\perp}'^2
 =t^2-\br^2.
\end{equation*}
Thus the exponential stretches one light-cone coordinate and compresses
the other reciprocally.  The same identity holds for event differences;
in particular,
\begin{equation}
 \dd t'^2-\dd\br'^2=\det(\dd \pX')=\det(\dd \pX)
 =\dd t^2-\dd\br^2.
 \label{eq:boost-interval}
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:boost-interval} preserves the interval~\eqref{eq:interval}
and light cones in every direction.  Future causal increments stay future
because $\dd t'=\gamma(\dd t-\beta\bu\dotprod\dd\br)>0$ when
$\dd t>0$ and $\dd t\geq\lVert\dd\br\rVert$
\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~118}{page=124}.

Along a future-directed time-like path,
$\dd s^2=\dd t'^2-\dd\br'^2$. With
$\bv'=\dd\br'/\dd t'$ and particle Lorentz factor
$\gamma'=\dd t'/\dd s=(1-\bv'^2)^{-1/2}$, the squared four-velocity norm is
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert\frac{\dd\pX'}{\dd s}\right\rVert^2
 &=\left(\frac{\dd t'}{\dd s}\right)^2
   +\left(\frac{\dd\br'}{\dd s}\right)^2\\
 &=2\left(\frac{\dd t'}{\dd s}\right)^2-1\\
 &=2\gamma'^2-1
 =\frac{1+\bv'^2}{1-\bv'^2}.
\end{align*}
Likewise, in the unprimed frame,
\[
 \left\lVert\frac{\dd\pX}{\dd s}\right\rVert^2
 =2\left(\frac{\dd t}{\dd s}\right)^2-1
 =\frac{1+\bv^2}{1-\bv^2}.
\]
Each norm equals $1$ only at rest in its corresponding frame, whereas
$\det(\dd\pX'/\dd s)=1$ in every inertial frame.
Proper time and causality are determined by the determinant; these
Lorentz transformations would not be obtained by preserving the squared norm.

\subsection{Spacetime increments and velocity}
Notation: the real four-velocity $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$, an
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$,
along a real world line $\pX(s)$.  For constant $\pL$, differentiation gives
\begin{equation}
 \dd \pX'=\pL\dd \pX \pL.
 \label{eq:boost-worldline-step}
\end{equation}
Because $\dd s$ is invariant, $\pU'=\pL \pU \pL$.  At a point where
$\pU=\pL^{-2}=\gamma(1+\beta\bu\dotprod\bsig)$, this gives $\pU'=1$ and
$\dd \pX'=\dd s$: the boost takes the particle's instantaneous tangent
to rest.  For varying velocity, a different $\pL$ is needed at each point;
Section~\ref{sec:varying-transformations} supplies its derivative terms.

The particle velocity $\bv=\dot{\br}$ is resolved along
$\bu$.  Dividing the spatial increment by
$\dd t'=\gamma(1-\beta\bu\dotprod\bv)\dd t$ gives~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.22)}{page=141}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bv'=\frac{\bv_{\perp}/\gamma+\bv_{\parallel}-\beta\bu}
                       {1-\beta\bu\dotprod\bv}.}
 \label{eq:velocity-boost}
\end{equation}
By interval invariance,
\begin{equation}
 1-\bv'^2=\frac{1-\bv^2}{\gamma^2(1-\beta\bu\dotprod\bv)^2}.
 \label{eq:velocity-causal-bound}
\end{equation}
Thus \eqref{eq:velocity-boost} preserves subluminal motion and light speed.
Since $\pL$ is constant, the same transformation applies to the integrated
worldline in \eqref{eq:worldline-integral}.

\subsection{Clock rates and measured lengths}
For a clock at rest in the primed frame, \eqref{eq:lorentz-time} uses
$\dd\br=\beta\bu\dd t$, hence~\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~119}{page=125}
\begin{equation}
 \dd t'=\gamma(1-\beta^2)\dd t=\dd t/\gamma.
 \label{eq:time-dilation}
\end{equation}
Here $\dd t'=\dd s$; finite elapsed time follows from
\eqref{eq:elapsed-proper-time}.

For a rod at rest in the unprimed frame, the fixed endpoint separation is
denoted by $\Delta\br\in\R^3$.  A simultaneous measurement in the primed
frame requires $\Delta t'=0$, so
$\Delta t=\beta\bu\dotprod\Delta\br$.  Substituting in
\eqref{eq:lorentz-position} gives~\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~123}{page=129}
\begin{equation}
 \Delta\br'_{\parallel}=\Delta\br_{\parallel}/\gamma.
 \label{eq:length-contraction}
\end{equation}
The transverse separation stays unchanged; thus a rod of any orientation has
\begin{equation*}
 \lVert\Delta\br'\rVert^2
 =\lVert\Delta\br_{\perp}\rVert^2
 +\lVert\Delta\br_{\parallel}\rVert^2/\gamma^2.
\end{equation*}
Longitudinal contraction~\eqref{eq:length-contraction} therefore compares
endpoint events at equal primed times.
Equal unprimed times instead give
$\Delta t'=-\gamma\beta\bu\dotprod\Delta\br$; simultaneity is frame-dependent.
The reciprocal statement follows by exchanging primes and reversing $\beta$.

\subsection{Composing frames}
For constant determinant-one factors $\pT,\pT'$, applying the frame change
$\pT$ and then $\pT'$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 \pZ''=\pT'\bigl(\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}\bigr)\pT'^{\mathsf H}
 =(\pT'\pT)\pZ(\pT'\pT)^{\mathsf H}.
\end{equation*}
Thus the combined factor is $\pT'\pT$: the rightmost factor acts first.

Collinear boosts add rapidities by \eqref{eq:boost-composition}.
For a second real rapidity $\theta'$, the parameter $\beta'=\tanh\theta'$ is defined, and
$\gamma'=\cosh\theta'$.  Their combined parameters are
\begin{align*}
 \beta''&=\tanh(\theta+\theta')
 =\frac{\beta+\beta'}{1+\beta\beta'}, \\
 \gamma''&=\gamma\gamma'(1+\beta\beta').
\end{align*}
These are the collinear velocity and clock-factor composition laws.
Noncollinear boosts instead have the rotation commutator
\eqref{eq:boost-generator-commutator}, producing the Thomas--Wigner rotation.

\subsection{Four-momentum and the mass shell}
\label{sec:four-momentum}

For constant invariant mass $m>0$, the real four-momentum $\pP\in\R\oplus\R^3$ is defined by~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.24)}{page=142}
\begin{equation}
 \pP:=m\pU=E+\bp\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:four-momentum}
\end{equation}
Energy and momentum are
\begin{align*}
 E&=\scpart {\pP}=m\frac{\dd t}{\dd s}=\frac{m}{\sqrt{1-\bv^2}},\\
 \bp&=\vecpart {\pP}=E\bv.
\end{align*}
Since $\det\pU=1$, scalar scaling gives the mass shell~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.30)}{page=143}:
\begin{equation}
 E^2-\bp^2=\det\pP=m^2,\qquad E>0.
 \label{eq:mass-shell}
\end{equation}
\begin{SIunits}{Momentum}
With dimensionless four-velocity $\pU$, mass $m$ in kilograms, energy $E$
in joules, and momentum $\bp$ in $\mathrm{kg\,m\,s^{-1}}$,
\begin{gather*}
 \pP=mc\pU=\frac{E}{c}+\bp\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \det\pP=m^2c^2,\qquad
 E^2=m^2c^4+c^2\bp^2.
\end{gather*}
The scalar and vector coefficients of $\pP$ now both have momentum units.
\end{SIunits}

Returning to natural units, the inverse and speed follow directly:
\[
 \pP^{-1}=\frac{E-\bp\dotprod\bsig}{m^2},\qquad
 \bv^2=\frac{\bp^2}{E^2}=1-\frac{m^2}{E^2}<1.
\]
For fixed $m>0$, approaching light speed requires unbounded energy.
A massless particle has no proper-time four-velocity. Its momentum is
defined directly by $\pP=E+\bp\dotprod\bsig$, with $E>0$ and $E^2=\bp^2$.

For the real momentum $\pP=E+\bp\dotprod\bsig$, the same congruence gives~\citeat{feynman-spacetime}{Eq.~(17.12)}{Eq:I:17:12}
\begin{align}
 E'&=\gamma(E-\beta\bu\dotprod\bp),
 \label{eq:energy-boost}\\
 \bp'&=\bp_{\perp}+\gamma(\bp_{\parallel}-\beta E\bu).
 \label{eq:momentum-boost}
\end{align}
Here the projections are along $\bu$.
The factor $\gamma=\cosh\theta$ belongs to the frame boost.
Since $\bp=E\bv$, energy and coordinate-time increments acquire the same
factor along a massive particle's path:
\begin{equation}
 \frac{E'}{E}=\frac{\dd t'}{\dd t}
 =\gamma(1-\beta\bu\dotprod\bv).
 \label{eq:energy-clock-factor}
\end{equation}
This is also the time factor used to obtain the velocity transformation.
Determinant invariance gives $E'^2-\bp'^2=E^2-\bp^2=m^2$ for a
particle of rest mass $m>0$.  The energy increase is obtained directly
from $\pP'=m$ in the rest frame by inversion of the boost:
\begin{equation*}
 \pP=\pL^{-1}m\pL^{-1}=m\gamma(1+\beta\bu\dotprod\bsig).
\end{equation*}
Thus $E=m\gamma$ and $\bp=m\gamma\beta\bu$, while $m$ remains unchanged
\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eqs.~(7.25), (7.28)}{page=142}.

\subsection{Free-particle action}

Notation: real $m>0$, $\bv=\dot{\br}$, particle factor
$\gamma=(1-\bv^2)^{-1/2}$, and $\dd s=\dd t/\gamma$.
Here $L:\R\times\R^3\times\{\bv\in\R^3:\bv^2<1\}\to\R$
is the particle Lagrangian; the boost factor is $\pL\in\R\oplus\R^3$.
