$\partial_{\bv}$ denotes its vector derivative with respect to the three
independent velocity components, while $\pdr$ denotes the spatial gradient.
The free action is the physical choice~\citeat{tong-em}{Eqs.~(5.38)--(5.39)}{page=130}
\begin{equation}
 \int L\dd t=-m\int\dd s.
 \label{eq:free-particle-action}
\end{equation}
Using $\dd s=\dd t/\gamma$ gives the Lagrangian
\begin{equation*}
 L=-m\sqrt{1-\bv^2}=-m/\gamma.
\end{equation*}
For endpoint-fixed real path variations $\delta\br(t)$, varying the
Lagrangian and integrating by parts gives
\[
 \delta\int L\dd t
 =\int\left(\pdr L-\frac{\dd\left(\partial_{\bv}L\right)}{\dd t}\right)
 \dotprod\delta\br\dd t.
\]
The Euler--Lagrange equation is obtained from stationarity for every variation.
With Hamiltonian $H:=\bv\dotprod\partial_{\bv}L-L\in\R$, the chain rule gives
\begin{align}
 \frac{\dd\left(\partial_{\bv}L\right)}{\dd t}&=\pdr L, \label{eq:euler-lagrange}\\
 \dot{H}
 &=\bv\dotprod\left(\frac{\dd\left(\partial_{\bv}L\right)}{\dd t}-\pdr L\right)
   -\partial_tL=-\partial_tL. \label{eq:hamiltonian-energy-balance}
\end{align}
By \eqref{eq:euler-lagrange} and \eqref{eq:hamiltonian-energy-balance},
spatial and temporal translation invariance conserve canonical
momentum and energy.  For the free action,
\begin{align*}
 \partial_{\bv}L&=m\gamma\bv=\bp,\\
 H&=m\gamma\bv^2+m/\gamma=m\gamma=E.
\end{align*}
The notation $v=\lVert\bv\rVert\ll1$ is used.  Here $O(v^n)$ denotes terms bounded
by a constant times $v^n$ as $v\to0$.  The binomial series gives
\begin{align*}
 \sqrt{1-v^2}&=1-\tfrac12v^2+O(v^4),\\
 \gamma&=1+\tfrac12v^2+O(v^4),\\
 L&=-m+\tfrac12mv^2+O(mv^4),\\
 H&=m+\tfrac12mv^2+O(mv^4).
\end{align*}
By the mass shell, the exact kinetic energy above rest is
\begin{equation*}
 K:=H-m=m(\gamma-1)=\frac{\bp^2}{E+m}\in\R_{\ge0}.
\end{equation*}
This and both expansions recover $mv^2/2$
at low speed after removal of the constant rest term.

\section{Acceleration}
\label{sec:acceleration}

Coordinate acceleration is $\dot\bv=\dd\bv/\dd t$; four-acceleration is
$\dd\pU/\dd s$. Proper acceleration is measured by an accelerometer.

\subsection{Four-acceleration and proper acceleration}
\label{sec:proper-acceleration}

Coordinate-time derivatives are first obtained:
\begin{align*}
 \dot{\gamma}&=\gamma^3\left(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv}\right),\\
 \dot{\pU}&=\dot{\gamma}
 +\frac{\dd\left(\gamma\bv\right)}{\dd t}\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \frac{\dd\left(\gamma\bv\right)}{\dd t}
 &=\gamma\dot{\bv}+\dot{\gamma}\bv.
\end{align*}
For $\bv\ne0$, the coordinate acceleration is resolved into tangential
(parallel to the velocity) and perpendicular components:
\[
 \dot{\bv}_{\parallel}:=\frac{(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv})\bv}{\bv^2},
 \qquad \dot{\bv}_{\perp}:=\dot{\bv}-\dot{\bv}_{\parallel},
 \qquad \dot{\bv}=\dot{\bv}_{\perp}+\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}.
\]
The projections of $\dot{\bv}$ are indicated by these subscripts; at $\bv=0$, the unsplit formulas are used.
Since $(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv})\bv=\bv^2\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}$ and
$1+\gamma^2\bv^2=\gamma^2$, the spatial derivative becomes
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\dd\left(\gamma\bv\right)}{\dd t}
 &=\gamma\dot{\bv}_{\perp}
   +(\gamma+\gamma^3\bv^2)\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}\\
 &=\gamma\dot{\bv}_{\perp}+\gamma^3\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}.
\end{align*}
Thus the full coordinate-time derivative of four-velocity is
\begin{equation}
 \dot{\pU}
 =\gamma^3(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv})
  +\left(\gamma\dot{\bv}_{\perp}+\gamma^3\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}\right)\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:four-velocity-coordinate-derivative}
\end{equation}
\paragraph{Orthogonality and determinant.}
Differentiating $\pU^*\pU=1$ gives
\[
 \dot{\pU}^*\pU+\pU^*\dot{\pU}=0.
\]
By taking scalar parts,
\[
 \scpart{\pU^*\dot{\pU}}=0.
\]
Thus $\pU$ and $\dot{\pU}$ are Minkowski-orthogonal. Although
$\dot{\pU}$ can have a scalar part, the product $\pU^*\dot{\pU}$
is scalar-free and belongs to $\{0\}\oplus\C^3$.
Multiplication on the right by $\pU^*$ also gives
\[
 \operatorname{adj} \dot{\pU}=-\pU^*\dot{\pU}\pU^*.
\]
By expansion of the commutator and the product,
\begin{align*}
 [\pU,\dot{\pU}]
 &=2\ii\gamma^2\left(\bv\times\dot{\bv}\right)\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \pU^*\dot{\pU}
 &=\gamma^2\left(\dot{\bv}-\ii\bv\times\dot{\bv}\right)\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
The cross product depends only on acceleration perpendicular to $\bv$.
Since $\det\pU^*=1$, multiplying by $\pU^*$ preserves the determinant.
The determinant of a scalar-free paravector is the negative of its square.
Using $\dot{\bv}\dotprod\left(\bv\times\dot{\bv}\right)=0$ therefore gives
\begin{align*}
 \det \dot{\pU}
 &=-\left(\pU^*\dot{\pU}\right)^2\\
 &=-\gamma^4\left(\dot{\bv}^2-\left(\bv\times\dot{\bv}\right)^2\right)\\
 &=-\gamma^2\left(\dot{\bv}^2+\gamma^2\left(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv}\right)^2\right).
\end{align*}
The last equality uses
$\left(\bv\times\dot{\bv}\right)^2=\bv^2\dot{\bv}^2-\left(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv}\right)^2$ and
$1-\bv^2=\gamma^{-2}$.
Using the parallel and perpendicular components defined above,
$\dot{\bv}^2=\dot{\bv}_{\perp}^2+\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}^2$ and
$\left(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv}\right)^2=\bv^2\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}^2$ give
\[
 \det \dot{\pU}
 =-\gamma^2\dot{\bv}_{\perp}^2-\gamma^4\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}^2.
\]
\paragraph{Conversion to proper time.}
Multiplying $\dot{\pU}$ by $\gamma$ gives~\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~141}{page=147}
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}=\gamma\dot{\pU}
 =\gamma^4\left(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv}\right)
 +\left(\gamma^2\dot{\bv}+\gamma^4\left(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv}\right)\bv\right)\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:proper-acceleration-components}
\end{equation}
The determinant is quadratic, so
\begin{align*}
 \det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)
 &=\gamma^2\det\left(\dot{\pU}\right)\\
 &=-\gamma^4\dot{\bv}_{\perp}^2-\gamma^6\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}^2.
\end{align*}
The same scaling gives $\scpart{\pU^*\dd\pU/\dd s}=0$, so
four-velocity and four-acceleration are Minkowski-orthogonal.
Nonzero four-acceleration is space-like. Its negative determinant is
the squared magnitude of the acceleration measured in the instantaneous
rest frame, called the proper acceleration.
At rest, $\gamma=1$ and $\dd\pU/\dd s=\dot{\bv}\dotprod\bsig$, so the
 determinant is $-\dot{\bv}^2$ and the proper acceleration has magnitude $\lVert\dot{\bv}\rVert$.

\begin{SIunits}{Proper acceleration}
Here $t,\tau$ are measured in seconds, $s=c\tau$ and $\br$ in metres,
and $\bv=\dd\br/\dd t$ in metres per second. Dots denote derivatives
with respect to this SI time. With $\pX=ct+\br\dotprod\bsig$,
\begin{gather*}
 \gamma=\frac1{\sqrt{1-\bv^2/c^2}},\qquad
 \pU=\frac{\dd\pX}{\dd s}
 =\gamma\left(1+\frac{\bv}{c}\dotprod\bsig\right),\\
 c^2\sqrt{-\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)}
 =\sqrt{\gamma^6\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}^2
       +\gamma^4\dot{\bv}_{\perp}^2}.
\end{gather*}
Four-velocity remains dimensionless; both sides of the last equation give
the accelerometer magnitude in $\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$.
\end{SIunits}

\subsection{Force, work, and inertia}
\label{sec:energy-inertia}
Here $\gamma=(1-\bv^2)^{-1/2}$ is the particle Lorentz factor.
For constant invariant mass $m>0$, four-momentum is $\pP=m\pU$,
so $\dot{\pP}=m\,\dot{\pU}$. Its scalar and spatial parts are
$E=m\gamma$ and $\bp=m\gamma\bv$.
The acceleration projections are parallel and perpendicular to $\bv$,
as in Section~\ref{sec:proper-acceleration}.
Multiplying the spatial part of
\eqref{eq:four-velocity-coordinate-derivative} by $m$ therefore gives
\begin{equation}
 \dot{\bp}=m\gamma\dot{\bv}_{\perp}+m\gamma^3\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}.
 \label{eq:relativistic-inertia}
\end{equation}
Taking its dot product with $\bv$ gives the work rate
\begin{equation}
 \dot{E}=m\gamma^3\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv}
 =\bv\dotprod\dot{\bp}.
 \label{eq:relativistic-work}
\end{equation}
At rest the force is $m\dot{\bv}$.

\begin{SIunits}{Force and energy}
In the following SI formulas,
$t$ is in seconds, $\bv$ in metres per second, and $\dot{\bp}$ in newtons:
\begin{gather*}
 \frac{\dd\bp}{\dd t}
 =m\gamma^3\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}
 +m\gamma\dot{\bv}_{\perp},\\
 E=\gamma mc^2.
\end{gather*}
\end{SIunits}

In the natural units used elsewhere,
invariant $m$ is retained: the longitudinal and transverse inertia in
\eqref{eq:relativistic-inertia} differ.
For the Lorentz force, the corresponding acceleration components are
derived in \eqref{eq:lorentz-acceleration-projections}.

\subsection{Fixed-axis acceleration}
\label{sec:fixed-axis-acceleration}
For the orthonormal pair $\pU,\pN$ in Section~\ref{sec:fixed-axis},
the first derivatives exchange the two directions:
\[
 \dot{\pU}=\dot{\theta}\pN,\qquad
 \dot{\pN}=\dot{\theta}\pU,\qquad
 \det\dot{\pU}=-\dot{\theta}^2.
\]
Differentiating $\beta=\tanh\theta$ gives
\[
 \dot{\beta}=\gamma^{-2}\dot{\theta}.
\]
Since $\dot{s}=1/\gamma$, the chain rule gives
\[
 \dot{\theta}=\frac{\dd\theta}{\dd s}\dot{s},\qquad
 \frac{\dd\theta}{\dd s}=\gamma\dot{\theta}=\gamma^3\dot{\beta}.
\]
These are ordinary derivatives of the same rapidity along the path.
The signed proper acceleration is $\dd\theta/\dd s$.
By scaling the coordinate derivatives,
\[
 \frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}=\frac{\dd\theta}{\dd s}\pN=\gamma\dot{\theta}\pN,\qquad
 \frac{\dd\pN}{\dd s}=\gamma\dot{\theta}\pU,\qquad
 \det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)=-\gamma^2\dot{\theta}^2.
\]
The proper-acceleration magnitude is therefore $\gamma|\dot\theta|$.

\subsection{Constant coordinate acceleration}
\label{sec:constant-coordinate-acceleration}
In the following examples, subscript $0$ denotes the initial event $t=s=0$.
Constant coordinate acceleration means that $\dot{\bv}=\dot{\bv}_0$
is a fixed real vector in a chosen inertial frame. With initial position
$\br(0)=\br_0$ and velocity $\bv(0)=\bv_0$, integration gives
\begin{align}
 \bv(t)&=\bv_0+\dot{\bv}_0t,\\
 \pX(t)&=t+\left(\br_0+\bv_0t+\tfrac12\dot{\bv}_0t^2\right)\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:constant-coordinate-acceleration-worldline}
\end{align}
These familiar coordinate-time formulas also describe a relativistic
massive particle, provided $\lVert\bv_0+\dot{\bv}_0t\rVert<1$ throughout the
interval considered. A nonzero constant coordinate acceleration can
therefore persist only for a finite coordinate-time interval.

For motion from rest at the origin, the notation $\dot{\bv}_0=\dot{v}_0\bu$ is used,
where $\dot{v}_0>0$ is the scalar acceleration along the fixed real unit
vector $\bu$. Then
\[
 \bv(t)=\dot{v}_0t\bu,\qquad
 \pX(t)=t+\tfrac12\dot{v}_0t^2\bu\dotprod\bsig,\qquad
 \gamma(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\dot{v}_0^2t^2}},
 \qquad 0\le t<\frac{1}{\dot{v}_0}.
\]
The particle's clock runs at $\dot{s}=1/\gamma(t)$. Taking $s(0)=0$
in \eqref{eq:elapsed-proper-time} gives
\begin{equation}
 s(t)=\int_0^t\sqrt{1-\dot{v}_0^2t'^2}\,\dd t'
 =\frac{\arcsin(\dot{v}_0t)+\dot{v}_0t\sqrt{1-\dot{v}_0^2t^2}}{2\dot{v}_0}.
 \label{eq:constant-coordinate-acceleration-clock}
\end{equation}
Four-velocity and four-acceleration follow by differentiating the world line:
\begin{align*}
 \pU(t)&=\gamma(t)(1+\dot{v}_0t\bu\dotprod\bsig),\\
 \frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}
 &=\gamma\dot{\pU}
 =\gamma^4\dot{v}_0^2t+\gamma^4\dot{v}_0\bu\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
Consequently,
\[
 \det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)
 =\gamma^8\dot{v}_0^2(\dot{v}_0^2t^2-1)
 =-\gamma^6\dot{v}_0^2.
\]
The proper-acceleration magnitude is therefore
\[
 \sqrt{-\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)}=\gamma^3\dot{v}_0
 =\frac{\dot{v}_0}{(1-\dot{v}_0^2t^2)^{3/2}}.
\]
Although the coordinate acceleration stays fixed, the acceleration
measured in the instantaneous rest frame grows without bound as
$t\to1/\dot{v}_0$. The next example instead holds the proper acceleration fixed.

\subsection{Constant proper acceleration}
\label{sec:constant-proper-acceleration}
A fixed \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit axis} $\bu$ is chosen,
and the initial conditions $\pX(0)=0$, $\pU(0)=1$ are imposed, with
initial coordinate acceleration $\dot{\bv}_0=\dot{v}_0\bu$, where $\dot{v}_0>0$.
At rest $\gamma_0=1$ and $\dot{\theta}_0=\dot{v}_0$. Holding the signed
proper acceleration along this axis constant gives
$\theta(s)=\dot{v}_0s$, and hence
\[
 \pU(s)=\pL(\dot{v}_0s,\bu)^{-2}=\cosh(\dot{v}_0s)+\sinh(\dot{v}_0s)\bu\dotprod\bsig.
\]
The stated acceleration is verified by differentiation:
\[
 \det\left(\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}\right)
 =-\dot{v}_0^2\bigl(\cosh^2(\dot{v}_0s)-\sinh^2(\dot{v}_0s)\bigr)=-\dot{v}_0^2.
\]
Integrating \eqref{eq:worldline-integral} yields~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.33), pp.~142--143}{page=148}
\begin{equation}
 \pX(s)=\frac{\sinh(\dot{v}_0s)}{\dot{v}_0}
       +\frac{\cosh(\dot{v}_0s)-1}{\dot{v}_0}\bu\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:hyperbolic-worldline}
\end{equation}
In components,
\begin{align*}
 \bv(t(s))&=\tanh(\dot{v}_0s)\bu,\\
 \bigl(1+\dot{v}_0\bu\dotprod\br\bigr)^2-(\dot{v}_0t)^2&=1.
\end{align*}
Thus \eqref{eq:hyperbolic-worldline} is the usual hyperbolic motion, with
speed below light speed at every finite $s$.
Eliminating $s$ using $\dot{v}_0t=\sinh(\dot{v}_0s)$ makes the coordinate acceleration explicit:
\[
 \bv(t)=\frac{\dot{v}_0t}{\sqrt{1+\dot{v}_0^2t^2}}\bu,\qquad
 \dot{\bv}(t)
 =\frac{\dot{v}_0}{(1+\dot{v}_0^2t^2)^{3/2}}\bu.
\]
Here $\gamma(t)=\sqrt{1+\dot{v}_0^2t^2}$, so
$\gamma^3\lVert\dot{\bv}(t)\rVert=\dot{v}_0$ is constant while the coordinate
acceleration decreases as the particle gains speed.

\subsection{A trip with midpoint braking}
\label{sec:rocket-trip}
Departure and destination are at rest in one inertial frame, separated by
$\ell>0$ along a fixed real unit vector $\bu$. Flat spacetime and initial
conditions $t=s=0$, $\br=0$, $\bv=0$ are assumed. Two equal burns at
constant proper-acceleration magnitude $\dot v_0>0$ are joined by an
instantaneous thrust reversal; arrival is at rest. This is a prescribed trajectory;
fuel consumption and the duration of the reversal are not modeled.

\paragraph{Rapidity and derivatives.}
The reversal times are denoted by $s_m$ and $t_m$; arrival occurs at
$2s_m$ and $2t_m$. With $\dd\theta/\dd s=+\dot v_0$ during acceleration
and $-\dot v_0$ during braking, integration gives
\begin{equation}
 \theta(s)=\begin{cases}
  \dot v_0s,&0\le s\le s_m,\\
  \dot v_0(2s_m-s),&s_m\le s\le2s_m.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:rocket-rapidity}
\end{equation}
Throughout the trip,
\[
 v=\tanh\theta,\qquad \bv=v\bu,\qquad
 \gamma=\cosh\theta,\qquad \dot s=\gamma^{-1}.
\]
On the open burn intervals, the upper sign denotes acceleration and the
lower sign braking. The derivatives are obtained directly:
\begin{align}
 \pU&=\cosh\theta+\sinh\theta\,\bu\dotprod\bsig,\notag\\
 \frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}
 &=\pm\dot v_0(\sinh\theta+\cosh\theta\,\bu\dotprod\bsig),\notag\\
 \dot\pU&=\frac{1}{\gamma}\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}
 =\pm\dot v_0(v+\bu\dotprod\bsig),\notag\\
 \dot v&=\pm\frac{\dot v_0}{\gamma^3},\qquad
 \dot{\bv}=\dot v\bu,\qquad
 \dot\gamma=\pm\dot v_0v.
 \label{eq:rocket-derivatives}
\end{align}
The acceleration is entirely tangential; the perpendicular component
vanishes. Although $\lVert\dot{\bv}\rVert$ varies, the proper-acceleration
magnitude is fixed:
\[
 \det\pU=1,\qquad
 \sqrt{-\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)}
 =\gamma^3\lVert\dot{\bv}\rVert=\dot v_0.
\]

\paragraph{Position, clocks, and arrival.}
By integrating $\dd t/\dd s=\cosh\theta$ and
$\dd\br/\dd s=\sinh\theta\,\bu$, the continuous worldline is obtained:
\begin{align}
 t(s)&=\begin{cases}
  \sinh\theta(s)/\dot v_0,&0\le s\le s_m,\\
  2t_m-\sinh\theta(s)/\dot v_0,&s_m\le s\le2s_m,
 \end{cases}\notag\\
 \br(s)&=\begin{cases}
  \bigl(\cosh\theta(s)-1\bigr)\bu/\dot v_0,&0\le s\le s_m,\\
  \bu\bigl(\ell-\bigl(\cosh\theta(s)-1\bigr)/\dot v_0\bigr),
    &s_m\le s\le2s_m.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:rocket-worldline}
\end{align}
Continuity at the reversal fixes the midpoint and burn durations:
\begin{align}
 \br(s_m)&=\tfrac12\bu \ell,\qquad
 \gamma_m:=\cosh(\dot v_0s_m)=1+\frac{\dot v_0\ell}{2},\notag\\
 s_m&=\frac{\operatorname{arcosh}(1+\dot v_0\ell/2)}{\dot v_0},\qquad
 t_m=\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{\dot v_0}+\frac{\ell^2}{4}},\notag\\
 v_{\max}&=\sqrt{1-\gamma_m^{-2}}.
 \label{eq:rocket-trip-times}
\end{align}
Thus $\br(2s_m)=\bu \ell$ and $\pU(0)=\pU(2s_m)=1$.
For evaluation in coordinate time, the same expressions are used with
\[
 \theta(t)=\begin{cases}
  \operatorname{arsinh}(\dot v_0t),&0\le t\le t_m,\\
  \operatorname{arsinh}\bigl(\dot v_0(2t_m-t)\bigr),&t_m\le t\le2t_m.
 \end{cases}
\]
In particular, $\lVert\dot{\bv}\rVert$ decreases to
$\dot v_0/\gamma_m^3$ before the reversal and increases back to
$\dot v_0$ during braking.

\paragraph{The thrust reversal.}
Position, both clocks, velocity, and four-velocity are continuous at the
midpoint. The one-sided values of $\dot{\bv}$, $\dot\pU$, and
$\dd\pU/\dd s$ are opposite; these derivatives are undefined at the ideal
instantaneous reversal. The thrust is reversed, not the direction of travel.
The jerk during each burn and through a finite reversal is discussed in
Section~\ref{sec:reversal-jerk}.

\begin{SIunits}{Trip parameters}
In the following SI formulas, $\dot v_0$ is the initial acceleration in
metres per second squared, also equal to the constant proper-acceleration
magnitude. The distance $\ell$ is in metres, $t_{\mathrm{trip}}$ is
the elapsed departure-frame time in seconds, and $\tau_{\mathrm{trip}}$
is the elapsed onboard proper time in seconds. Restoring units in
\eqref{eq:rocket-trip-times} gives
\begin{align*}
 &t_{\mathrm{trip}}
 =\sqrt{\frac{\ell^2}{c^2}+\frac{4\ell}{\dot v_0}},\\
 &\tau_{\mathrm{trip}}
 =\frac{2c}{\dot v_0}\operatorname{arcosh}\left(1+\frac{\dot v_0\ell}{2c^2}\right),\\
 &v_{\max}
 =c\sqrt{1-\left(1+\frac{\dot v_0\ell}{2c^2}\right)^{-2}}.
\end{align*}
For a specified onboard duration and peak speed, the required proper
acceleration is fixed by the midpoint rapidity:
\[
 \dot v_0=\frac{2c}{\tau_{\mathrm{trip}}}
 \operatorname{artanh}\!\left(\frac{v_{\max}}{c}\right).
\]
\end{SIunits}

\section{Jerk}
\label{sec:jerk}
Three quantities are distinguished: the coordinate jerk $\ddot{\bv}$,
the proper-time derivative of four-acceleration, and its projection into
the instantaneous rest space. Proper jerk is the vector rate of change
of measured acceleration per unit proper time, referred to nonrotating
carried axes. Dots still mean $t$ derivatives.

\subsection{Coordinate derivatives, then proper-time derivatives}
The expressions for $\dot{\gamma}$ and $\dot{\pU}$ in
Section~\ref{sec:proper-acceleration} are differentiated once more with respect to $t$.
Using
\[
 \frac{\dd\left(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv}\right)}{\dd t}
 =\dot{\bv}^2+\bv\dotprod\ddot{\bv},
\]
the following is obtained by a second differentiation:
\begin{align*}
 \ddot{\gamma}
 &=\gamma^3\left(\dot{\bv}^2+\bv\dotprod\ddot{\bv}\right)
   +\frac{3\dot{\gamma}^2}{\gamma},\\
 \ddot{\pU}
 &=\ddot{\gamma}
 +\left(\gamma\ddot{\bv}+2\dot{\gamma}\dot{\bv}
       +\ddot{\gamma}\bv\right)\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
The conversion $\dd/\dd s=\gamma\dd/\dd t$ is now applied. The first derivative is
$\dd\pU/\dd s=\gamma\dot{\pU}$, and the second is
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 =\gamma\frac{\dd\left(\gamma\dot{\pU}\right)}{\dd t}
 =\gamma^2\ddot{\pU}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}\dot{\pU}.
 \label{eq:jerk-time-conversion}
\end{equation}
The same conversion applies to every twice-differentiable scalar, vector,
or paravector along the worldline. In particular,
\begin{align}
 \frac{\dd^2\gamma}{\dd s^2}
 &=\gamma^2\ddot{\gamma}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}^2,\notag\\
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 &=\frac{\dd^2\gamma}{\dd s^2}+
 \left(\gamma^3\ddot{\bv}+3\gamma^2\dot{\gamma}\dot{\bv}
 +\frac{\dd^2\gamma}{\dd s^2}\bv\right)\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:four-jerk-components}
\end{align}
This derivative of four-acceleration is the \emph{unprojected four-jerk}:
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd}{\dd s}\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)
 =\frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 =\frac{\dd^3\pX}{\dd s^3}.
 \label{eq:four-jerk-definition}
\end{equation}
By comparison, $\dd\dot{\bv}/\dd s=\gamma\ddot{\bv}$ still differentiates
the coordinate acceleration of the chosen inertial frame.

\subsection{Projection into the instantaneous rest space}
The coordinate-time normalization identities are used:
\[
 \scpart{\pU^*\dot{\pU}}=0,\qquad
 \scpart{\pU^*\ddot{\pU}}=-\det\left(\dot{\pU}\right).
\]
The second follows by differentiating the first. Using
\[
 \det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)=\gamma^2\det\dot{\pU},
\]
substitution into \eqref{eq:jerk-time-conversion} gives
\[
 \scpart{\pU^*\frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}}
 =-\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right).
\]
Thus the derivative of four-acceleration need not be orthogonal to
four-velocity. Since $\det\pU=1$, the right-hand side is its coefficient of
$\pU$ in the unprojected four-jerk. Removing that component defines the
projected relativistic jerk~\citeat{russo-townsend}{Eqs.~(2.8)--(2.13)}{page=7}.
With the signature $(+,-,-,-)$, its orthogonality is
\begin{equation}
 \scpart{\pU^*\left(\frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 +\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)\pU\right)}=0.
 \label{eq:projected-jerk}
\end{equation}
At an instant of rest, $\pU=1$ and
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}&=\dot{\bv}^2+\ddot{\bv}\dotprod\bsig\\
 &=-\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)\pU
 +\ddot{\bv}\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
Here the coordinate derivatives are evaluated in the inertial frame
in which the particle is instantaneously at rest.
The projected expression is therefore the measured proper jerk there;
it is space-like whenever nonzero.

\paragraph{Invariant magnitude.}
Expanding the determinant using the metric pairing and
\eqref{eq:projected-jerk} gives
\begin{equation}
 \det\left(\frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 +\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)\pU\right)
 =\det\left(\frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}\right)
 -\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)^2
 \le0.
 \label{eq:proper-jerk-determinant}
\end{equation}
The negative of this determinant is the squared proper-jerk magnitude.
The unprojected four-jerk can instead be time-like, null, or space-like;
its determinant includes the positive contribution from the component
along $\pU$.

\paragraph{When proper jerk vanishes.}
In \eqref{eq:four-jerk-components}, subtracting the scalar coefficient
times $\bv$ from the vector coefficient leaves
$\gamma^3\left(\ddot{\bv}+3\dot{\gamma}\dot{\bv}/\gamma\right)$.
Its vanishing says that the unprojected four-jerk is parallel to $\pU$,
so its projection vanishes. Hence~\citeat{russo-townsend}{Eq.~(2.14)}{page=7}
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 +\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)\pU=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \ddot{\bv}=-3\frac{\dot{\gamma}}{\gamma}\dot{\bv}.
 \label{eq:zero-proper-jerk}
\end{equation}
Zero proper jerk therefore need not mean zero coordinate jerk.
An equivalent first integral is
\[
 \frac{\dd\left(\gamma^3\dot{\bv}\right)}{\dd t}=0,
 \qquad \gamma^3\dot{\bv}=\gamma_0^3\dot{\bv}_0.
\]
Here subscript $0$ denotes any reference event. Any nonzero coordinate
acceleration therefore retains a fixed direction, although the velocity need not be parallel
to it; the spatial trajectory is confined to a plane.
Also, differentiating the acceleration determinant gives
\[
 \frac{\dd}{\dd s}\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)
 =2\scpart{\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)^*
              \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}}.
\]
The pairing is unchanged if the second factor is replaced by its
projected expression, because $\dd\pU/\dd s$ is orthogonal to $\pU$.
Consequently, zero projected jerk implies constant proper-acceleration
magnitude. The converse does not hold in general: the direction can still
change, as in uniform circular motion.

\subsection{Fixed-axis jerk}
\label{sec:fixed-axis-jerk}
Differentiating the paired first derivatives in
Section~\ref{sec:fixed-axis-acceleration} gives
\begin{align*}
 \ddot{\pU}&=\dot{\theta}^2\pU+\ddot{\theta}\pN,\\
 \ddot{\pN}&=\dot{\theta}^2\pN+\ddot{\theta}\pU.
\end{align*}
The $\dot{\theta}^2$ terms come from differentiating the moving basis;
the $\ddot{\theta}$ terms come from differentiating its rate of change.
By orthonormality,
\[
 \det\ddot{\pU}=-\det\ddot{\pN}
 =\dot{\theta}^4-\ddot{\theta}^2.
\]
Differentiating $\dot\beta=\gamma^{-2}\dot\theta$ also gives
\[
 \ddot{\beta}=\gamma^{-2}\ddot{\theta}
 -2\gamma^{-3}\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}.
\]

\paragraph{Conversion to proper time.}
By \eqref{eq:jerk-time-conversion}, the signed proper jerk is
\[
 \frac{\dd^2\theta}{\dd s^2}
 =\gamma^2\ddot{\theta}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}.
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:jerk-time-conversion} to each member of the pair gives
\begin{align}
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 &=\gamma^2\dot{\theta}^2\pU
 +\left(\gamma^2\ddot{\theta}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}\right)\pN,\notag\\
 \frac{\dd^2\pN}{\dd s^2}
 &=\gamma^2\dot{\theta}^2\pN
 +\left(\gamma^2\ddot{\theta}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}\right)\pU.
 \label{eq:rectilinear-jerk}
\end{align}
Removing the component along $\pU$ leaves
\[
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}-\gamma^2\dot{\theta}^2\pU
 =\left(\gamma^2\ddot{\theta}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}\right)\pN
 =\frac{\dd^2\theta}{\dd s^2}\pN.
\]
The proper-jerk magnitude is
$\left|\gamma^2\ddot{\theta}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}\right|$.
The corresponding determinant identities are
\begin{align*}
 \det\left(\frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}-\gamma^2\dot{\theta}^2\pU\right)
 &=-\left(\gamma^2\ddot{\theta}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}\right)^2,\\
 \det\left(\frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}\right)
 &=-\det\left(\frac{\dd^2\pN}{\dd s^2}\right)\\
 &=\gamma^4\dot{\theta}^4
 -\left(\gamma^2\ddot{\theta}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}\right)^2.
\end{align*}
The sign reversal again comes from exchanging the unit time and spatial
directions. Using $\dot{\theta}=\gamma^2\dot{\beta}$ and
$\dot{\gamma}=\gamma^3\beta\dot{\beta}$ also gives
\begin{align}
 \frac{\dd^2\theta}{\dd s^2}
 &=\gamma^4\ddot{\beta}+3\gamma^3\dot{\gamma}\dot{\beta}
 =\gamma^4\ddot{\beta}+3\gamma^6\beta\dot{\beta}^2,\notag\\
 \ddot{\beta}&=\gamma^{-4}\frac{\dd^2\theta}{\dd s^2}
 -3\frac{\dot{\gamma}}{\gamma}\dot{\beta}.
 \label{eq:coordinate-proper-jerk}
\end{align}
For fixed-axis motion, zero proper jerk is exactly
$\gamma\ddot{\theta}+\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}=0$, or equivalently
$\dd^2\theta/\dd s^2=0$. Thus $\gamma\dot{\theta}$ is constant and rapidity
is affine in proper time. The unprojected four-jerk remains nonzero
whenever that constant proper acceleration is nonzero.

\paragraph{Null combinations and constant acceleration.}
The symmetry of the paired derivatives becomes especially simple on
adding or subtracting $\pU$ and $\pN$:
\[
 \pU\pm\pN=e^{\pm\theta}\left(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bsig\right),\qquad
 \det\left(\pU\pm\pN\right)=0.
\]
These two null directions are fixed; only their scalar factors change.
Their coordinate-time derivatives are
\begin{align*}
 \dot{\pU}\pm\dot{\pN}
 &=\pm\dot{\theta}\left(\pU\pm\pN\right),\\
 \ddot{\pU}\pm\ddot{\pN}
 &=\left(\dot{\theta}^2\pm\ddot{\theta}\right)\left(\pU\pm\pN\right).
\end{align*}
The proper-time derivatives follow by the same conversion:
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\dd\left(\pU\pm\pN\right)}{\dd s}
 &=\pm\gamma\dot{\theta}\left(\pU\pm\pN\right),\\
 \frac{\dd^2\left(\pU\pm\pN\right)}{\dd s^2}
 &=\left(\gamma^2\dot{\theta}^2
 \pm\left(\gamma^2\ddot{\theta}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}\right)\right)
 \left(\pU\pm\pN\right).
\end{align*}
These formulas allow varying acceleration. If the signed proper acceleration
$\dd\theta/\dd s=\gamma\dot{\theta}$ is constant, then
\[
 \frac{\dd^2\theta}{\dd s^2}
 =\gamma^2\ddot{\theta}+\gamma\dot{\gamma}\dot{\theta}=0.
\]
The second-derivative formula therefore reduces to
\[
 \frac{\dd^2\left(\pU\pm\pN\right)}{\dd s^2}
 =\gamma^2\dot{\theta}^2\left(\pU\pm\pN\right)
 =\left(\pm\gamma\dot{\theta}\right)^2\left(\pU\pm\pN\right).
\]
Only under this constant-acceleration assumption is each null combination
multiplied by the same constant factor at every proper-time differentiation.
For integers $n\ge0$, the $n=2$ case above is included in
\[
 \frac{\dd^n\left(\pU\pm\pN\right)}{\dd s^n}
 =\left(\pm\gamma\dot{\theta}\right)^n\left(\pU\pm\pN\right).
\]
Adding and subtracting recovers the derivatives of $\pU$ and $\pN$:
even orders preserve each direction, while odd orders exchange them.
These ordinary derivatives remain nonzero for nonzero constant proper
acceleration, even though the projected jerk is zero.
The constant-proper-acceleration worldline in
Section~\ref{sec:constant-proper-acceleration} realizes this pattern;
Section~\ref{sec:constant-jerk} treats nonzero constant proper jerk.

\subsection{Jerk at a thrust reversal}
\label{sec:reversal-jerk}
For the trip in Section~\ref{sec:rocket-trip}, away from the reversal,
\[
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}=\dot v_0^2\pU,\qquad
 \ddot{\bv}=-\frac{3\dot v_0^2v}{\gamma^4}\bu.
\]
The projected proper jerk is zero during each constant-acceleration burn,
although coordinate jerk is generally nonzero. At the ideal instantaneous
reversal, acceleration is discontinuous and jerk is undefined.
A finite reversal must be described by a transition with nonzero proper jerk.

\subsection{Constant jerk}
\label{sec:constant-jerk}
A distinction is made between constant coordinate jerk in a chosen inertial frame,
constant signed proper jerk along a fixed axis, and constant components
of the unprojected four-jerk. The last condition is preserved by constant
Lorentz transformations. Subscript $0$ denotes the initial event $t=s=0$.

\subsubsection{Constant coordinate jerk}
For $\ddot{\bv}=\ddot{\bv}_0$ in a chosen inertial frame, integration gives
\begin{align}
 \dot{\bv}(t)&=\dot{\bv}_0+\ddot{\bv}_0\,t,\notag\\
 \bv(t)&=\bv_0+\dot{\bv}_0t+\tfrac12\ddot{\bv}_0\,t^2,\notag\\
 \pX(t)&=t+
 \left(\br_0+\bv_0t+\tfrac12\dot{\bv}_0t^2+\tfrac16\ddot{\bv}_0\,t^3\right)\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:constant-coordinate-jerk}
\end{align}
These formulas apply on the interval where $\bv(t)^2<1$.
Equations~\eqref{eq:lorentz-factor}, \eqref{eq:proper-velocity}, and
\eqref{eq:elapsed-proper-time} give $\gamma$, $\pU$, and $s(t)$;
\eqref{eq:four-jerk-components} gives the generally varying four-jerk.
For example, from rest with zero initial acceleration, the following is chosen:
$\ddot{\bv}_0=\ddot{v}_0\bu$, where $\ddot{v}_0>0$ is the scalar jerk
along the fixed real unit axis $\bu$. Then
\[
 \bv(t)=\tfrac12\ddot{v}_0t^2\bu,\qquad
 \br(t)=\br_0+\tfrac16\ddot{v}_0t^3\bu,\qquad
 0\le t<\sqrt{\frac{2}{\ddot{v}_0}}.
\]
Here $\dot{\beta}=\ddot{v}_0t$ and $\beta=\ddot{v}_0t^2/2$, so
\eqref{eq:coordinate-proper-jerk} gives
$\dd^2\theta/\dd s^2=\gamma^4\ddot{v}_0+3\gamma^6\beta\left(\ddot{v}_0t\right)^2$.
Thus constant coordinate jerk does not give constant proper jerk.

\subsubsection{Constant proper jerk}
For fixed-axis motion, the signed quantity $\dd^2\theta/\dd s^2$ is held constant. Its value and the initial signed
proper acceleration are expressed in coordinate-time data using
$\gamma_0=\cosh\theta_0$ and $\dot{\gamma}_0=\sinh\theta_0\dot{\theta}_0$.
By integration with respect to proper time,
\begin{align}
 \frac{\dd\theta}{\dd s}
 &=\gamma_0\dot{\theta}_0
 +\left(\gamma_0^2\ddot{\theta}_0
       +\gamma_0\dot{\gamma}_0\dot{\theta}_0\right)s,\notag\\
 \theta(s)&=\theta_0+\gamma_0\dot{\theta}_0s
 +\frac12\left(\gamma_0^2\ddot{\theta}_0
       +\gamma_0\dot{\gamma}_0\dot{\theta}_0\right)s^2.
 \label{eq:constant-proper-jerk}
\end{align}
If the particle starts at rest, $\theta_0=0$, $\gamma_0=1$, and
$\dot{\gamma}_0=0$, so this reduces to
$\theta(s)=\dot{\theta}_0s+\tfrac12\ddot{\theta}_0s^2$.
The corresponding world line, with $t(0)=0$, is
\begin{align}
 t(s)&=\int_0^s\cosh\theta(s')\,\dd s',\notag\\
 \br(s)&=\br_0+\bu\int_0^s\sinh\theta(s')\,\dd s',\qquad
 \pX(s)=t(s)+\br(s)\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:constant-proper-jerk-worldline}
\end{align}
The trajectory is specified exactly by these integrals; a related derivation is given in
\citeat{russo-townsend}{Eqs.~(5.8)--(5.9)}{page=19}.
Because $\dd t/\dd s=\cosh\theta>0$, they also define a unique
coordinate-time trajectory. Its velocity, acceleration, and jerk are
\begin{align*}
 \bv(t(s))&=\tanh\theta(s)\bu,\\
 \dot{\bv}(t(s))&=\frac{1}{\cosh^3\theta(s)}\frac{\dd\theta}{\dd s}\bu,\\
 \ddot{\bv}(t(s))&=\frac{1}{\cosh^4\theta(s)}
 \left(\frac{\dd^2\theta}{\dd s^2}-3\left(\frac{\dd\theta}{\dd s}\right)^2\tanh\theta(s)\right)\bu.
\end{align*}
The projected jerk is $(\dd^2\theta/\dd s^2)\pN$, as derived in
Section~\ref{sec:fixed-axis-jerk}. Its magnitude is constant, while its inertial-frame
components change with $\pN$. The unprojected derivative also contains
the changing component $(\dd\theta/\dd s)^2\pU$.
Setting $\theta_0=0$, $\ddot{\theta}_0=0$, and
$\dot{\theta}_0=\dot{v}_0>0$ recovers the
constant-proper-acceleration example in Section~\ref{sec:constant-proper-acceleration}.

\subsubsection{Constant unprojected four-jerk}
If the components of $\dd^2\pU/\dd s^2$ are constant in an inertial frame,
its value is determined by the initial coordinate-time derivatives:
\[
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 =\gamma_0^2\ddot{\pU}_0+\gamma_0\dot{\gamma}_0\dot{\pU}_0.
\]
Here $\gamma_0$ and $\dot{\gamma}_0$ are the scalar parts of
$\pU_0$ and $\dot{\pU}_0$, respectively.
By direct integration,
\begin{align*}
 \pU(s)&=\pU_0+s\gamma_0\dot{\pU}_0
 +\frac{s^2}{2}\left(\gamma_0^2\ddot{\pU}_0
                   +\gamma_0\dot{\gamma}_0\dot{\pU}_0\right),\\
 \pX(s)&=\pX_0+s\pU_0+\frac{s^2}{2}\gamma_0\dot{\pU}_0
 +\frac{s^3}{6}\left(\gamma_0^2\ddot{\pU}_0
                   +\gamma_0\dot{\gamma}_0\dot{\pU}_0\right).
\end{align*}
With $\pU_0$ future-directed, the following coefficient conditions
are necessary and sufficient for $\det\pU(s)=1$. Equating powers of $s$
using the metric pairing in \eqref{eq:lorentz-pairing} gives
\begin{gather*}
 \det\pU_0=1,\qquad \scpart{\pU_0^*\dot{\pU}_0}=0,\qquad
 \scpart{\pU_0^*\ddot{\pU}_0}=-\det\left(\dot{\pU}_0\right),\\
 \gamma_0\scpart{\dot{\pU}_0^*\ddot{\pU}_0}
 +\dot{\gamma}_0\det\left(\dot{\pU}_0\right)=0,\qquad
 \det\left(\gamma_0\ddot{\pU}_0+\dot{\gamma}_0\dot{\pU}_0\right)=0.
\end{gather*}
In particular, a nonzero constant $\dd^2\pU/\dd s^2$ must be null.
If it is zero, the normalization conditions force $\dot{\pU}_0=0$,
so the motion is inertial.
Thus a constant four-acceleration paravector must vanish; constant proper
acceleration does not mean constant inertial-frame components.
The same conditions imply constant $\det(\dd\pU/\dd s)$.
By \eqref{eq:proper-jerk-determinant}, the proper-jerk magnitude is then
the square of the proper-acceleration magnitude for this entire class.

For a concrete example~\citeat{russo-townsend}{Eq.~(3.40)}{page=15},
an initial rest state at the origin is chosen, with nonzero initial coordinate
acceleration $\dot{\bv}_0$ and jerk $\ddot{\bv}_0$ constrained by
\[
 \dot{\bv}_0\dotprod\ddot{\bv}_0=0,\qquad
 \ddot{\bv}_0^2=\left(\dot{\bv}_0^2\right)^2.
\]
Here $\gamma_0=1$ and $\dot{\gamma}_0=0$, so the initial paravector data are
\[
 \pX_0=0,\qquad \pU_0=1,\qquad
 \dot{\pU}_0=\dot{\bv}_0\dotprod\bsig,\qquad
 \ddot{\pU}_0=\dot{\bv}_0^2+\ddot{\bv}_0\dotprod\bsig.
\]
The resulting world line is
\begin{equation}
 \pX(s)=s+\tfrac16\dot{\bv}_0^2s^3
 +\left(\tfrac12\dot{\bv}_0s^2+\tfrac16\ddot{\bv}_0s^3\right)\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:constant-four-jerk-worldline}
\end{equation}
By differentiation, the following is verified:
\[
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}=\dot{\bv}_0^2+\ddot{\bv}_0\dotprod\bsig,\qquad
 \det\pU=1.
\]
Its invariants are
\[
 \det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)=-\dot{\bv}_0^2,\qquad
 \det\left(\frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}\right)=0.
\]
Thus the proper-acceleration magnitude $\lVert\dot{\bv}_0\rVert$ and,
by \eqref{eq:proper-jerk-determinant}, the proper-jerk magnitude
$\dot{\bv}_0^2=\lVert\ddot{\bv}_0\rVert$ are both constant.
Also $\dd t/\dd s=1+\dot{\bv}_0^2s^2/2>0$, so $s$ is indeed future-directed
proper time. This curved spatial path is a different motion from the
fixed-axis constant-proper-jerk example.

\section{Kinematics in a Frenet frame}
\label{sec:frenet-kinematics}

Velocity, acceleration, and jerk are resolved along the tangent and two
normals of the spatial path. Turning and twisting are described by
curvature and torsion.

\subsection{Spatial frame and curvature}
In the chosen inertial frame, the speed is $v:=\lVert\bv\rVert\in(0,1)$
and spatial arc length is $\ell$, with $\dot\ell=v$.
Dots denote $t$ derivatives. On a curved portion of the spatial path,
the real unit tangent, principal normal, and binormal are defined by
\[
 \bu:=\frac{\bv}{v},\qquad
 k:=\left\lVert\frac{\dd\bu}{\dd\ell}\right\rVert
 =\frac{\lVert\dot\bu\rVert}{v}>0,\qquad
 \bu':=\frac{\dot\bu}{vk},\qquad
 \bu'':=\bu\times\bu'.
\]
Here $\bu,\bu',\bu''\in\R^3$ form a right-handed orthonormal frame.
The primes are frame labels; derivatives are indicated by dots or fractions.
The instantaneous plane of curvature is spanned by $\bu,\bu'$.
Spatial curvature $k$ is the turning angle per unit distance along
the path; the radius of its local osculating circle is $1/k$.
The signed torsion $\tau\in\R$ is defined by
\[
 \tau:=-\frac{\dd\bu''}{\dd\ell}\dotprod\bu'
 =-\frac{\dot{\bu}''\dotprod\bu'}{v}.
\]
Here $\tau$ denotes spatial torsion; proper time remains $s$.
The turning of the curvature plane per unit distance is described by
$\tau$; for a planar curved path, $\tau=0$.

By differentiating the orthonormality relations and using
$\dd/\dd t=v\,\dd/\dd\ell$, the Frenet equations are obtained
\citeat{frenet-curves}{Secs.~2.4--2.6}{page=10}:
\begin{equation}
 \dot\bu=vk\bu',\qquad
 \dot{\bu}'=-vk\bu+v\tau\bu'',\qquad
 \dot{\bu}''=-v\tau\bu'.
 \label{eq:frenet-frame}
\end{equation}
At $v=0$ the tangent is undefined; at $k=0$ the principal normal
and binormal are not fixed by the path. The unsplit kinematic formulas
remain valid there. On a straight segment, fixed transverse axes may be
chosen and the turning terms set to zero.

\subsection{Coordinate velocity, acceleration, and jerk}
Differentiating $\bv=v\bu$ and applying \eqref{eq:frenet-frame} gives
\begin{align}
 \bv&=v\bu,\notag\\
 \dot{\bv}&=\underbrace{\dot v\bu}_{\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}:\ \text{tangential}}
 +\underbrace{v^2k\bu'}_{\dot{\bv}_{\perp}:\ \text{perpendicular}},\notag\\
 \ddot{\bv}&=(\ddot v-v^3k^2)\bu
 +(3v\dot vk+v^2\dot k)\bu'
 +v^3k\tau\bu''.
 \label{eq:frenet-coordinate-kinematics}
\end{align}
Speed is changed by tangential acceleration; direction is changed by
perpendicular acceleration. Acceleration is confined to the tangent--normal
plane; a binormal component is introduced in jerk through torsion.
Curvature is therefore the perpendicular-acceleration magnitude divided
by speed squared. Both geometric quantities are recovered from
coordinate derivatives as
\begin{align}
 k&=\frac{\lVert\dot{\bv}_{\perp}\rVert}{v^2}
 =\frac{\lVert\bv\times\dot{\bv}\rVert}{v^3},\notag\\
 \tau&=\frac{(\bv\times\dot{\bv})\dotprod\ddot{\bv}}
 {\lVert\bv\times\dot{\bv}\rVert^2}.
 \label{eq:frenet-curvature-torsion}
\end{align}

\subsection{Spacetime derivatives in the moving frame}
The earlier longitudinal rest direction $\pN$ is extended to the varying
tangent $\bu(t)$:
\[
 \pU=\gamma(1+v\bu\dotprod\bsig),\qquad
 \pN:=\gamma(v+\bu\dotprod\bsig),\qquad
 \pU,\pN\in\R\oplus\R^3.
\]
The four directions $\pU,\pN,\bu'\dotprod\bsig,\bu''\dotprod\bsig$ are
Minkowski-orthogonal, with determinants $1,-1,-1,-1$.
Thus a spatial Frenet frame is used to resolve spacetime quantities;
$\pU$ is the spacetime tangent, while $\bu$ is the spatial tangent.
The three rest directions generally rotate relative to nonrotating
carried axes.

With $\dot\gamma=\gamma^3v\dot v$ and
$\bu\dotprod\bsig=\gamma(\pN-v\pU)$, the complete frame evolution is
\begin{align*}
 \dot\pU&=\gamma^2\dot v\pN+\gamma v^2k\bu'\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \dot\pN&=\gamma^2\dot v\pU+\gamma vk\bu'\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \dot{\bu}'\dotprod\bsig
 &=\gamma v^2k\pU-\gamma vk\pN+v\tau\bu''\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \dot{\bu}''\dotprod\bsig&=-v\tau\bu'\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
Coefficients are paired with the same sign between time and spatial directions,
and with opposite signs between spatial directions. Thus Minkowski orthonormality is
preserved. Differentiating the first row gives
\begin{align*}
 \ddot\pU
 &=(\gamma^4\dot v^2+\gamma^2v^4k^2)\pU\\
 &\quad+\bigl(\gamma^2(\ddot v-v^3k^2)
                  +2\gamma\dot\gamma\dot v\bigr)\pN\\
 &\quad+\bigl(3\gamma v\dot vk+2\dot\gamma v^2k
                  +\gamma v^2\dot k\bigr)\bu'\dotprod\bsig
       +\gamma v^3k\tau\bu''\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
The proper-time derivatives follow by $\dd/\dd s=\gamma\dd/\dd t$:
\begin{align}
 \frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}
 &=\gamma\dot\pU
 =\gamma^3\dot v\pN+\gamma^2v^2k\bu'\dotprod\bsig,
 \label{eq:frenet-four-acceleration}\\
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 &=\gamma^2\ddot\pU+\gamma\dot\gamma\dot\pU.
 \notag
\end{align}
The first is four-acceleration and the second is unprojected four-jerk.
In the instantaneous rest frame, the tangential and perpendicular
acceleration components are scaled by $\gamma^3$ and $\gamma^2$,
respectively, relative to their coordinate values.
By orthogonality, the projection of four-jerk as in \eqref{eq:projected-jerk} is
\begin{align}
 &\frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 +\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)\pU\notag\\
 &\quad=\bigl(\gamma^4(\ddot v-v^3k^2)
                 +3\gamma^6v\dot v^2\bigr)\pN\notag\\
 &\qquad+\bigl(3\gamma^5v\dot vk+\gamma^3v^2\dot k\bigr)
               \bu'\dotprod\bsig
        +\gamma^3v^3k\tau\bu''\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:frenet-proper-jerk}
\end{align}
The squared proper-jerk magnitude is the sum of the squares of these
three rest-space coefficients. In particular, differentiating only the
acceleration coefficients would omit the rotation of the frame.

\paragraph{Spatial curvature versus worldline curvature.}
The invariant curvature of the spacetime worldline is the magnitude of
its proper-time tangent's derivative:
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt{-\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)}
 =\sqrt{\gamma^6\dot v^2+\gamma^4v^4k^2}.
 \label{eq:frenet-worldline-curvature}
\end{equation}
In the natural units used here, this is the proper-acceleration magnitude.
Both changing speed and changing spatial direction contribute. Spatial
curvature $k$ depends on the inertial frame; worldline curvature is
Lorentz invariant. A spatially straight accelerated path has $k=0$
but nonzero worldline curvature. For the trip in Section~\ref{sec:rocket-trip},
these are $k=0$ and $\dot v_0$ on each burn, respectively.
In both cases the curvature is that of a path in flat spacetime;
no curvature of spacetime itself is implied.

For uniform circular motion, $v$ and $k$ are constant and $\tau=0$.
The coordinate derivatives reduce to
\[
 \dot{\bv}=v^2k\bu',\qquad
 \ddot{\bv}=-v^3k^2\bu.
\]
The proper-acceleration magnitude is the constant $\gamma^2v^2k$,
while \eqref{eq:frenet-proper-jerk} reduces to
\[
 \frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 +\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)\pU
 =-\gamma^4v^3k^2\pN\ne0.
\]
Thus constant acceleration magnitude is compatible with nonzero proper
jerk: the acceleration direction is changing.

\section{Coordinates, frames, and differentiation}
\label{sec:differentiated-fields}

Fields have domain $\R^4$ (or an open coordinate region).
The symbols $\partial_t$, $\pdr$, $\pvpartial$, $\pvpartial^*$, and $\Box$
are differential operators. On a scalar field $f:\R^4\to\C$,
$\partial_t f,\Box f\in\C$, $\pdr f\in\C^3$, and
$\pvpartial f,\pvpartial^*f\in\C\oplus\C^3$ pointwise.
For real $f$, these outputs lie in the corresponding real spaces.
On a paravector field, $\pvpartial$ and $\pvpartial^*$ use algebra multiplication,
so even a real input may have a complex output.

\subsection{Paravector differential operators}

Cartesian inertial coordinates are denoted by $t\in\R$ and
$\br=\{r_1,r_2,r_3\}\in\R^3$.
The scalar and vector derivatives are defined by
\[
 \partial_t:=\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\qquad
 \pdr:=\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial r_1},
              \frac{\partial}{\partial r_2},
              \frac{\partial}{\partial r_3}\right\}.
\]
The partial paravector and its sigma conjugate are defined by~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(176)}{page=29}
\begin{equation}
 \pvpartial:=\partial_t+\bsig\dotprod\pdr
 =\partial_t+\sum_{n=1}^{3}\pvsigma_n\frac{\partial}{\partial r_n},
 \label{eq:paravector-derivative}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation*}
 \pvpartial^*=\operatorname{adj} \pvpartial=\partial_t-\bsig\dotprod\pdr.
\end{equation*}
The sigmas are constant.  Derivatives act from the left on
everything to their right unless parentheses specify their scope.
For fields with commuting second derivatives, anticommutation cancels
all mixed spatial terms:
\begin{align*}
 (\bsig\dotprod\pdr)^2
 &=\sum_{n=1}^{3}\frac{\partial^2}{\partial r_n^2}
 +\sum_{n<m}(\pvsigma_n\pvsigma_m+\pvsigma_m\pvsigma_n)
           \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r_n\partial r_m}\\
 &=\pdr\dotprod\pdr.
\end{align*}
The mixed time--space terms likewise cancel.  Both orders therefore give
the scalar d'Alembertian~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(178)}{page=30}
\begin{equation}
 \pvpartial\pvpartial^*=\pvpartial^*\pvpartial
 =\partial_t^2-\pdr\dotprod\pdr=:\Box.
 \label{eq:dalembertian}
\end{equation}
These are operator identities: $\pvpartial\pvpartial^*\pZ$ means
$\pvpartial(\pvpartial^*\pZ)$.

\subsection{Components and the product rule}

Real scalar functions $a,b:\R^4\to\R$ and
real vector functions $\bX,\bY:\R^4\to\R^3$ are combined into the complex
paravector field
\[
 \pZ(t,\br)=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\ii\bY)\dotprod\bsig.
\]
Ordered vector multiplication gives the real and imaginary scalar and vector parts of $\pvpartial \pZ$:
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \pvpartial \pZ={}&(\partial_ta+\pdr\dotprod\bX)
 +\ii(\partial_tb+\pdr\dotprod\bY)\\
 &+(\pdr a+\partial_t\bX-\pdr\times\bY)\dotprod\bsig\\
 &+\ii(\pdr b+\pdr\times\bX+\partial_t\bY)\dotprod\bsig.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Multiplication by $\ii$ exchanges real and imaginary parts, with the
former imaginary parts acquiring a minus sign.

Here $\pZ:\R^4\to\C\oplus\C^3$. For a second field
$\pZ':\R^4\to\C\oplus\C^3$, the prime labels the
field rather than a derivative.  The scalar product rule is applied before
any factors are reordered:
\begin{align*}
 \pvpartial(\pZ \pZ')
 ={}&(\partial_t\pZ)\pZ'+\pZ(\partial_t\pZ')\\
 &+\sum_{n=1}^{3}\pvsigma_n
   \left(\frac{\partial \pZ}{\partial r_n}\pZ'
          +\pZ\frac{\partial \pZ'}{\partial r_n}\right)\\
 ={}&(\pvpartial \pZ)\pZ'+\pZ(\pvpartial \pZ')
    +\sum_{n=1}^{3}[\pvsigma_n,\pZ]\frac{\partial \pZ'}{\partial r_n}.
\end{align*}
For a complex scalar first factor, the commutators vanish and the usual
product rule is recovered.

Partial derivatives are taken at fixed remaining coordinates. Along a
path $\br(t)$ with $\bv=\dot\br$, the total derivative is instead
\[
 \dot\pZ=\partial_t\pZ
 +\sum_{n=1}^{3}\dot r_n\frac{\partial\pZ}{\partial r_n}
 =(\partial_t+\bv\dotprod\pdr)\pZ.
\]
All fields and partial derivatives are evaluated at $(t,\br(t))$.

\subsection{Generalized coordinates and clock rates}
\label{sec:generalized}

Here $(t,\br)$ are real chart coordinates. Smooth functions
$a:\R^4\to\R$ and $\bX:\R^4\to\R^3$, restricted to an open region
with nonsingular Jacobian, specify inertial coordinates:
\[
 \pX=a(t,\br)+\bX(t,\br)\dotprod\bsig\in\R\oplus\R^3.
\]
Here $a$ is scalar-valued inertial time and $\bX$ is
vector-valued inertial position. Chart time $t$ and chart position $\br$
need not be clock readings and Cartesian distances.

\paragraph{Interval.}
By differentiation,
\begin{align*}
 \dd\pX&=(\partial_t a+\partial_t\bX\dotprod\bsig)\dd t
 +\sum_{k=1}^3(\partial_{r_k}a+\partial_{r_k}\bX\dotprod\bsig)\dd r_k,\\
 \det(\dd\pX)&=\dd a^2-\dd\bX^2.
\end{align*}
This is the flat Minkowski interval in changed coordinates; varying
coefficients alone do not imply spacetime curvature.

\paragraph{Motion and clock rates.}
Along a future-directed time-like path, $s$ is proper time,
$\dd s^2=\det(\dd\pX)>0$, and $\dd a>0$.
On a segment parametrized by increasing chart time $t$, overdots denote
total $t$ derivatives. The inertial velocity and particle Lorentz factor are
\[
 \bv:=\frac{\dd\bX}{\dd a}=\frac{\dot{\bX}}{\dot a}\in\R^3,
 \qquad \bv^2<1,\qquad
 \gamma:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\bv^2}}=\frac{\dd a}{\dd s}.
\]
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \dot s=\sqrt{\dot a^2-\dot{\bX}^2}=\frac{\dot a}{\gamma},\qquad
 \frac{\dd}{\dd s}=\frac{\gamma}{\dot a}\frac{\dd}{\dd t},\qquad
 \pU=\gamma(1+\bv\dotprod\bsig).
 \label{eq:chart-clock-rate}
\end{equation}
Here $\dot a>0$. The earlier rule $\dd/\dd s=\gamma\dd/\dd t$ is
recovered when $t=a$. The two acceleration derivatives are related by
\[
 \dot{\bv}=\frac{\ddot{\bX}\dot a-\dot{\bX}\ddot a}{\dot a^2},
 \qquad \frac{\dd\bv}{\dd a}=\frac{\dot\bv}{\dot a}.
\]
Equation~\eqref{eq:proper-acceleration-components} is applied with inertial
time $a$ and acceleration $\dd\bv/\dd a$.
For inertial motion, $\dd\pU/\dd s=0$ implies $\dd\bv/\dd a=0$, hence
\[
 \bX=\bX_0+\bv_0 a,\qquad
 \bX_0,\bv_0\in\R^3,\quad \bv_0^2<1.
\]
The reference inertial time is $a=0$; the same path may be nonlinear in
chart coordinates.

\subsection{Varying frame transformations}
\label{sec:varying-transformations}

A coordinate chart is held fixed while the local frame is varied along a
curve. Here $s$ is any real curve parameter; it is
specialized to proper time in Section~\ref{sec:carried-derivatives}.
A smooth complex paravector $\pT(s)$ with $\det\pT=1$ is chosen.
Here $\pT(s),\pZ(s)\in\C\oplus\C^3$. The paravector
$\pvOmega(s):=(\dd \pT/\dd s)\pT^{-1}\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3$ is defined.
Its scalar part vanishes because
$\dd(\det \pT)/\dd s=2\scpart\pvOmega=0$.
By the inverse rule,
\[
 \frac{\dd \pT^{-1}}{\dd s}=-\pT^{-1}\pvOmega.
\]
For a complex paravector $\pZ(s)$ transformed by similarity,
$\pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{-1}$, product differentiation yields
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd \pZ'}{\dd s}
 =\pT\frac{\dd \pZ}{\dd s}\pT^{-1}+[\pvOmega,\pZ'].
 \label{eq:varying-similarity}
\end{equation}
Differentiating again and replacing
$\pT(\dd \pZ/\dd s)\pT^{-1}$ by its first-derivative expression gives
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{\dd^2 \pZ'}{\dd s^2}={}&\pT\frac{\dd^2\pZ}{\dd s^2}\pT^{-1}
 +2\left[\pvOmega,\frac{\dd \pZ'}{\dd s}\right]\\
 &+\left[\frac{\dd\pvOmega}{\dd s},\pZ'\right]
 -[\pvOmega,[\pvOmega,\pZ']].
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
For a real paravector transformed instead by congruence,
$\pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}$, the same rule gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd \pZ'}{\dd s}
 =\pT\frac{\dd \pZ}{\dd s}\pT^{\mathsf H}
 +\pvOmega \pZ'+\pZ'\pvOmega^{\mathsf H}.
 \label{eq:varying-congruence}
\end{equation}
Equations~\eqref{eq:varying-similarity} and \eqref{eq:varying-congruence}
include the frame terms omitted by a constant-factor transformation.
For a varying rotation $\pT=\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta(s)\bu(s)\dotprod\bsig/2)$,
with real $\theta(s)$ and \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu(s)$, unitarity gives
$\pvOmega^{\mathsf H}=-\pvOmega$ and $\pvOmega\in\{0\}\oplus\ii\R^3$,
so both formulas agree.

Position-dependent sigma bases and metric constructions are developed in
Section~\ref{sec:variable-bases}.

\subsection{Physical derivatives in a carried frame}
\label{sec:carried-derivatives}

Here $s$ is proper time along a future-directed time-like worldline,
$\pU=\dd\pX/\dd s\in\R\oplus\R^3$, and $\det\pU=1$.
From \eqref{eq:varying-congruence}, the physical four-acceleration expressed
in the local frame is
\begin{equation}
 \pT\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\pT^{\mathsf H}
 =\frac{\dd\pU'}{\dd s}-\pvOmega\pU'-\pU'\pvOmega^{\mathsf H}.
 \label{eq:carried-acceleration}
\end{equation}
The derivative of the transformed components includes motion of the
frame; its contribution is removed by the two $\pvOmega$ terms.
Similarly, $\dd\pX'/\dd s$ includes frame terms and generally differs
from the transformed tangent $\pU'$.

If the frame is kept instantaneously at rest with the particle, then
$\pU'=\pT\pU\pT^{\mathsf H}=1$ along the curve, so
\[
 \pT\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\pT^{\mathsf H}
 =-(\pvOmega+\pvOmega^{\mathsf H})=-2\repart{\pvOmega}.
\]
A constant rest-frame four-velocity therefore does not imply zero
physical acceleration. Its components are encoded in the Hermitian
part of $\pvOmega$.

Differentiating this expression, removing the frame terms, and applying
the projection in \eqref{eq:projected-jerk} gives
\begin{equation}
 \pT\left(\frac{\dd^2\pU}{\dd s^2}
 +\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)\pU\right)\pT^{\mathsf H}
 =-\frac{\dd(\pvOmega+\pvOmega^{\mathsf H})}{\dd s}
 +[\pvOmega,\pvOmega^{\mathsf H}].
 \label{eq:carried-jerk}
\end{equation}
Rotation of the carried axes is accounted for by the commutator. For axes
kept aligned with ideal torque-free gyroscopes,
$\pvOmega=\pvOmega^{\mathsf H}$, so the proper jerk is
$-2\dd\pvOmega/\dd s$: the proper-time rate of the measured acceleration.
The fixed-axis results of Section~\ref{sec:fixed-axis-jerk} are recovered with
$\pT=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$ and fixed $\bu$.

\section{Electromagnetism}
\label{sec:electromagnetism}

All coefficient fields depend on real inertial coordinates $(t,\br)$.
The electric and magnetic fields $\bE,\bBf:\R^4\to\R^3$
are combined into the complex field vector $\bF:\R^4\to\C^3$ and its scalar-free
paravector $\pF\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3$~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(23)}{page=19}:
\begin{equation}
 \bF:=\bE+\ii\bBf,\qquad \pF:=\bF\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:em-field-definition}
\end{equation}
The charge density $\rho:\R^4\to\R$ and current density
$\bJ:\R^4\to\R^3$ are combined into the real four-current paravector:
\[
 \pJ:=\rho+\bJ\dotprod\bsig\in\R\oplus\R^3,
 \qquad \pJ^*=\rho-\bJ\dotprod\bsig.
\]
The paravector derivative of \eqref{eq:paravector-derivative} is used,
with spatial gradient $\pdr$ and d'Alembertian
$\Box=\pvpartial^*\pvpartial=\partial_t^2-\pdr^2$.
The symbols $\pvpartial$, $\pvpartial^*$, and $\Box$ denote differential operators.

The dot/cross product~\eqref{eq:vector-product} combines metric and
orientation. With a spatial derivative it gives divergence and curl,
packing the six real electromagnetic components into one complex field $\pF$.
The product explains the compact form; Maxwell dynamics remains physical
input, derived from an action in Section~\ref{sec:maxwell-action}.

\subsection{Maxwell's equations and wave propagation}

Maxwell's four equations are the single field law~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(177)}{page=29}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pvpartial \pF=\pJ^*.}
 \label{eq:maxwell}
\end{equation}
The product rule expands its left side into
\begin{align*}
 \pvpartial \pF={}&\underbrace{\pdr\dotprod\bE}_{\text{real scalar}}
 +\underbrace{\ii\pdr\dotprod\bBf}_{\text{imaginary scalar}}\\
 &+\underbrace{(\partial_t\bE-\pdr\times\bBf)\dotprod\bsig}_{\text{real vector}}\\
 &+\underbrace{\ii(\partial_t\bBf+\pdr\times\bE)\dotprod\bsig}_{\text{imaginary vector}}.
\end{align*}
Equating the four parts gives~\citeat{tong-em}{Eqs.~(1.4)--(1.7)}{page=14}:
\begin{align}
 \text{Real scalar:}\quad &\pdr\dotprod\bE=\rho,
 \label{eq:gauss-electric}\\
 \text{Imaginary scalar:}\quad &\pdr\dotprod\bBf=0,
 \label{eq:gauss-magnetic}\\
 \text{Real vector:}\quad &\partial_t\bE-\pdr\times\bBf=-\bJ,
 \label{eq:ampere-maxwell}\\
 \text{Imaginary vector:}\quad &\partial_t\bBf+\pdr\times\bE=0.
 \label{eq:faraday}
\end{align}
These are Gauss' electric and magnetic laws, Amp\`ere--Maxwell's law,
and Faraday's law.

\begin{SIunits}{Maxwell's equation}
With $t$ in seconds, $\br$ in metres, electric field in volts per metre,
magnetic field in teslas, charge density in coulombs per cubic metre,
and current density in amperes per square metre,
\begin{gather*}
 \pF=(\bE+\ii c\bBf)\dotprod\bsig,\qquad
 \pvpartial=\frac1c\partial_t+\pdr\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \pvpartial\pF=\frac1{\epsilon_0}
 \left(\rho-\frac{\bJ}{c}\dotprod\bsig\right).
\end{gather*}
The electric and magnetic terms now have the same units. By separating
the scalar and vector parts, the four SI Maxwell equations are recovered;
the vacuum constants satisfy $\epsilon_0\mu_0c^2=1$.
\end{SIunits}

In natural units, applying $\pvpartial^*$ gives the sourced wave equation:
\[
 \pvpartial^*(\pvpartial \pF)=\Box \pF=\pvpartial^*\pJ^*.
\]
Taking its star yields~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(152)}{page=26}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\Box \pF^*=\pvpartial \pJ.}
 \label{eq:maxwell-wave}
\end{equation}
By expansion of the source derivative,
\[
 \pvpartial \pJ
 =(\partial_t\rho+\pdr\dotprod\bJ)
 +(\partial_t\bJ+\pdr\rho+\ii\pdr\times\bJ)\dotprod\bsig.
\]
Because $\pF$ is scalar-free, its real and imaginary scalar parts vanish.
Matching all four parts gives~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(1.1)}{page=12}:
\begin{align}
 \text{Real scalar:}\quad &0=\partial_t\rho+\pdr\dotprod\bJ,
 \label{eq:charge-continuity}\\
 \text{Imaginary scalar:}\quad &0=0,\notag\\
 \text{Real vector:}\quad &\Box\bE=-\partial_t\bJ-\pdr\rho,
 \label{eq:electric-wave}\\
 \text{Imaginary vector:}\quad &\Box\bBf=\pdr\times\bJ.
 \label{eq:magnetic-wave}
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{eq:charge-continuity} is charge conservation;
\eqref{eq:electric-wave} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-wave} are the sourced field waves.
In source-free vacuum, $\pJ=0$ and $\Box \pF=0$: both fields obey the free wave equation.

\subsection{Potentials and gauge freedom}

The scalar potential $V:\R^4\to\R$ and vector potential
$\bA:\R^4\to\R^3$ are combined into the real four-potential; its four-divergence is also defined:
\[
 \pA:=V+\bA\dotprod\bsig\in\R\oplus\R^3,
 \qquad S:=\scpart{\pvpartial \pA}\in\R.
\]
By the product rule,
\begin{align*}
 \pvpartial \pA={}&(\partial_tV+\pdr\dotprod\bA)
 +(\partial_t\bA+\pdr V)\dotprod\bsig
 +\ii(\pdr\times\bA)\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
Subtracting its scalar part gives the field~\citeat{tong-em}{p.~105}{page=113}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pF^*=\pvpartial \pA-S.}
 \label{eq:potential-field}
\end{equation}
Thus $\pvpartial \pA=S+\pF^*\in\R\oplus\C^3$, with components:
\begin{align}
 \text{Real scalar:}\quad &S=\partial_tV+\pdr\dotprod\bA,\notag\\
 \text{Imaginary scalar:}\quad &0=0,\notag\\
 \text{Real vector:}\quad &\bE=-\partial_t\bA-\pdr V,
 \label{eq:electric-potential}\\
 \text{Imaginary vector:}\quad &\bBf=\pdr\times\bA.
 \label{eq:magnetic-potential}
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{eq:potential-field} separates the four-divergence from the field;
\eqref{eq:electric-potential} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-potential} satisfy
\eqref{eq:gauss-magnetic} and \eqref{eq:faraday} identically.

Applying $\pvpartial^*$ gives the source equations in any gauge:
\[
 \Box \pA=\pvpartial^*(\pvpartial \pA)
 =\pvpartial^*(S+\pF^*)=\pvpartial^*S+(\pvpartial \pF)^*.
\]
Using Maxwell's equation~\eqref{eq:maxwell} gives~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(6.1)}{page=142}:
\[
 \boxed{\Box \pA=\pvpartial^*S+\pJ.}
\]
With scalar and vector parts separated,
\begin{align*}
 \text{Real scalar:}\quad &\Box V-\partial_tS=\rho,\\
 \text{Imaginary scalar:}\quad &0=0,\\
 \text{Real vector:}\quad &\Box\bA+\pdr S=\bJ,\\
 \text{Imaginary vector:}\quad &\vstyle{0}=\vstyle{0}.
\end{align*}
These source equations hold before gauge fixing.
For a real gauge function $\lambda:\R^4\to\R$, the transformation is defined by~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(6.2)}{page=142}:
\[
 \pA':=\pA+\pvpartial^*\lambda.
\]
By differentiation, $\pvpartial \pA'=\pvpartial \pA+\Box\lambda$.
Only the scalar changes, so the field is gauge invariant:
\[
 S'=S+\Box\lambda,\qquad \pF'=\pF.
\]
In the Lorenz gauge $S=0$, the potential wave equations are~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(6.4)}{page=143}:
\begin{align*}
 \text{Real scalar:}\quad &\Box V=\rho,\\
 \text{Real vector:}\quad &\Box\bA=\bJ.
\end{align*}

\subsection{The electromagnetic field action}
\label{sec:maxwell-action}

For real scalar potential $V(t,\br)$ and vector potential
$\bA(t,\br)$, the notation $\pA=V+\bA\dotprod\bsig$ is used.  With prescribed conserved
current $\pJ=\rho+\bJ\dotprod\bsig$,
Maxwell dynamics follows from the scalar Lagrangian density
$L:\R^4\to\R$~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.42)}{page=133}\,\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.43)}{page=134}
\begin{equation}
 L=\tfrac12\repart{\pF^2}-\scpart{\pA^*\pJ}
  =\tfrac12(\bE^2-\bBf^2)-\rho V+\bJ\dotprod\bA.
 \label{eq:maxwell-lagrangian}
\end{equation}
For boundary-vanishing variations $\delta V\in\R$ and $\delta\bA\in\R^3$,
the induced field variations $\delta\bE,\delta\bBf\in\R^3$ satisfy
\begin{align*}
 \delta\bE&=-\pdr\delta V-\partial_t\delta\bA,\\
 \delta\bBf&=\pdr\times\delta\bA.
\end{align*}
By integration by parts,
\begin{equation*}
 \delta\int L\dd t\dd^3\br
 =\int\left((\pdr\dotprod\bE-\rho)\delta V
 +(\partial_t\bE-\pdr\times\bBf+\bJ)\dotprod\delta\bA\right)\dd t\dd^3\br.
\end{equation*}
From independent variations,
\eqref{eq:gauss-electric} and \eqref{eq:ampere-maxwell}; the potential
identities supply \eqref{eq:gauss-magnetic} and \eqref{eq:faraday}.
Together they recover \eqref{eq:maxwell}.

\subsection{Field invariants}

Since $\pF\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3$, its adjugate is $-\pF$.
Its square is a complex scalar, $\pF^2\in\C$~\citeat{baylis-applications}{p.~106}{page=117}
\begin{equation}
 \pF^2=-\det \pF=\bE^2-\bBf^2+2\ii\bE\dotprod\bBf.
 \label{eq:field-invariants}
\end{equation}
For a complex \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} $\pT$, the field similarity
$\pF'=\pT \pF \pT^{-1}$ preserves this scalar by \eqref{eq:det-similarity}.
Physical rotations and boosts use
$\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$ and
$\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$, respectively, with
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$
and real angle or rapidity $\theta$; their field laws are derived in
Section~\ref{sec:transformations}.
The real and imaginary parts of \eqref{eq:field-invariants} are
therefore observer-independent.
At a point with a nonzero field, they give the classification
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
Condition & Consequence\\
\midrule
$\bE\dotprod\bBf=0$, $\bE^2>\bBf^2$ & Some frame has $\bBf'=0$.\\
$\bE\dotprod\bBf=0$, $\bE^2<\bBf^2$ & Some frame has $\bE'=0$.\\
$\bE\dotprod\bBf\ne0$ & Neither field can be eliminated.\\
$\bE\dotprod\bBf=0$, $\bE^2=\bBf^2$ & Null field: equal, perpendicular fields.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For the first two rows, the following factors are chosen, respectively:
\[
 \tanh\theta\,\bu=\frac{\bE\times\bBf}{\bE^2},
 \qquad
 \tanh\theta\,\bu=\frac{\bE\times\bBf}{\bBf^2}.
\]
Both speeds are less than one.  Substitution in
\eqref{eq:electric-boost} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-boost} gives
$\bBf'=0$ and $\bE'=0$, respectively.

\subsection{Lorentz force from the action}

A dot is used for a total derivative along the particle's path with
respect to coordinate time $t$, for example $\dot{\bp}=\dd\bp/\dd t$.
The field derivatives $\partial_t$ are partial derivatives at fixed position.

Notation: the real four-velocity $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s=\gamma(1+\bv\dotprod\bsig)$,
an \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$.
Charge and constant mass are denoted by $q\in\R$ and $m>0$, respectively.  The real momentum is
$\pP=m\pU=E+\bp\dotprod\bsig$, with $E=m\gamma$ and $\bp=m\gamma\bv$.
The real particle Lagrangian $L$ is fixed by the action~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.41)}{page=132}
\begin{equation}
 \int L\dd t=-m\int\dd s-q\int\scpart{\pA^*\pU}\dd s.
 \label{eq:charged-particle-action}
\end{equation}
Since $\scpart{\pA^*\pU}=\gamma(V-\bv\dotprod\bA)$, the Lagrangian is
\begin{equation*}
 L=-m/\gamma-qV+q\bv\dotprod\bA.
\end{equation*}
Its Euler--Lagrange terms are
\begin{align*}
 \partial_{\bv}L&=\bp+q\bA,\\
 \frac{\dd\left(\partial_{\bv}L\right)}{\dd t}
 &=\dot{\bp}+q\partial_t\bA+q(\bv\dotprod\pdr)\bA,\\
 \pdr L&=-q\pdr V+q\pdr(\bv\dotprod\bA).
\end{align*}
Here spatial differentiation holds the independent velocity argument fixed.
Using
$\pdr(\bv\dotprod\bA)-(\bv\dotprod\pdr)\bA
=\bv\times(\pdr\times\bA)$ gives~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.32)}{page=124}
\begin{equation}
 \dot{\bp}=q(\bE+\bv\times\bBf).
 \label{eq:lorentz-momentum}
\end{equation}
Here \eqref{eq:electric-potential} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-potential}
turn the potential derivatives into the fields.
The Hamiltonian is
\begin{equation}
 H=\bv\dotprod(\bp+q\bA)-L=E+qV.
 \label{eq:charged-hamiltonian}
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{eq:hamiltonian-energy-balance} in \eqref{eq:charged-hamiltonian},
\begin{align*}
 \dot{E}+q\partial_tV+q\bv\dotprod\pdr V
 &=-\partial_tL=q\partial_tV-q\bv\dotprod\partial_t\bA.
\end{align*}
Canceling $q\partial_tV$ and using \eqref{eq:electric-potential} gives~\citeat{tong-em}{p.~116}{page=124}
\begin{equation}
 \dot{E}=q\bv\dotprod\bE.
 \label{eq:lorentz-energy}
\end{equation}
This is the mechanical work rate. Thus $\bp+q\bA$ and $H=E+qV$ are
canonical momentum and energy. The momentum $\bp$ is kinetic; the particle energy $E$ includes rest energy and excludes
the potential energy $qV$.
Canonical energy is conserved for static potentials in the chosen gauge.
A time-dependent gauge transformation can change $H$ while leaving $E$
and the force unchanged.

The complex product of the field and four-velocity is expanded:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{\pF \pU}{\gamma}={}&\bv\dotprod\bE
   +(\bE+\bv\times\bBf)\dotprod\bsig\\
 &+\ii\left(\bv\dotprod\bBf
   +(\bBf-\bv\times\bE)\dotprod\bsig\right).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:force-product}
\end{equation}
The real scalar and vector parts reproduce
\eqref{eq:lorentz-energy} and \eqref{eq:lorentz-momentum}.
Since $\pP$ is real and $\dd/\dd s=\gamma\dd/\dd t$, they combine into~\citeat{baylis-charge}{Eq.~(2)}{page=4}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\frac{\dd \pP}{\dd s}
 =q\repart{\pF \pU}=\frac q2(\pF \pU+\pU \pF^{\mathsf H}).}
 \label{eq:lorentz}
\end{equation}
Hermitian extraction enforces real kinetic
four-momentum and reproduces the action-derived force.  The imaginary part
need not vanish for an electric charge.

\subsection{Acceleration and the mass shell}
\label{sec:lorentz-proper-acceleration}

Notation: the real four-velocity $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$, an
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$ and
$\pU^{-1}=\pU^*$, real momentum $\pP=m\pU$, and coordinate acceleration
$\dot{\bv}=\dd\bv/\dd t$.  Scalar-part cyclicity proves tangency to the mass shell:
\begin{align*}
 \scpart{\pU^*\dd \pP/\dd s}
 &=\frac q2\scpart{\pU^*\pF \pU+\pF^{\mathsf H}}\\
 &=\frac q2\left(\scpart {\pF}+\scpart{\pF^{\mathsf H}}\right)=0.
\end{align*}
This agrees with $\scpart{\pU^*\dd \pU/\dd s}=0$ in
Section~\ref{sec:proper-acceleration}.
Combining \eqref{eq:proper-acceleration-components} with the force components
in \eqref{eq:force-product} gives
\begin{align*}
 m\gamma^3\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv}&=q\bv\dotprod\bE,\\
 m\gamma\left(\dot{\bv}+\gamma^2(\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv})\bv\right)
 &=q(\bE+\bv\times\bBf).
\end{align*}
The coordinate acceleration is obtained by eliminating $\bv\dotprod\dot{\bv}$
\begin{equation*}
 \boxed{\dot{\bv}=\frac{q}{m\gamma}
 \left(\bE+\bv\times\bBf-(\bv\dotprod\bE)\bv\right).}
\end{equation*}
The magnetic term changes momentum without doing work.

\paragraph{Parallel and perpendicular components.}
For $\bv\ne0$, all projections here are relative to the instantaneous
velocity, as in Section~\ref{sec:proper-acceleration}:
\[
 \bE_{\parallel}=\frac{(\bv\dotprod\bE)\bv}{\bv^2},\qquad
 \bE_{\perp}=\bE-\bE_{\parallel}.
\]
Since $\bv\dotprod(\bv\times\bBf)=0$, projecting
\eqref{eq:lorentz-momentum} and using \eqref{eq:relativistic-inertia} gives
\begin{align*}
 \dot{\bp}_{\parallel}
 &=m\gamma^3\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}=q\bE_{\parallel},\\
 \dot{\bp}_{\perp}
 &=m\gamma\dot{\bv}_{\perp}=q(\bE_{\perp}+\bv\times\bBf).
\end{align*}
The subscripts denote projections of the force along the instantaneous
velocity axis. Thus
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 \dot{\bv}_{\parallel}&=\frac{q}{m\gamma^3}\bE_{\parallel},\\
 \dot{\bv}_{\perp}&=\frac{q}{m\gamma}
 (\bE_{\perp}+\bv\times\bBf).
 \end{aligned}}
 \label{eq:lorentz-acceleration-projections}
\end{equation}
The parallel component changes speed; the perpendicular component changes
direction. The magnetic force contributes only to the latter.
At $\bv=0$, the velocity axis is undefined and the unsplit equation gives
$\dot{\bv}=q\bE/m$.

\paragraph{Proper acceleration and the rest-frame field.}
Taking the determinant of the real force gives
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 -\det\left(\frac{\dd \pP}{\dd s}\right)
 &=-m^2\det\left(\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}\right)\\
 &=q^2\gamma^2\left((\bE+\bv\times\bBf)^2-(\bv\dotprod\bE)^2\right).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
The invariant squared proper acceleration is obtained by division by $m^2$.
For $\bv\ne0$, orthogonality gives
\[
 (\bE+\bv\times\bBf)^2-(\bv\dotprod\bE)^2
 =\gamma^{-2}\bE_{\parallel}^2
  +\lVert\bE_{\perp}+\bv\times\bBf\rVert^2.
\]
Equivalently, substituting \eqref{eq:lorentz-acceleration-projections}
into the acceleration determinant from Section~\ref{sec:proper-acceleration}
gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 -\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)
 &=\gamma^6\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}^2
   +\gamma^4\dot{\bv}_{\perp}^2\\
 &=\frac{q^2}{m^2}\left(\bE_{\parallel}^2
   +\gamma^2\lVert\bE_{\perp}+\bv\times\bBf\rVert^2\right).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:lorentz-proper-acceleration-projections}
\end{equation}
The boost into the instantaneous rest frame gives
$\bE'=\bE_{\parallel}+\gamma(\bE_{\perp}+\bv\times\bBf)$,
as derived in \eqref{eq:electric-boost}. Thus the squared proper
acceleration is $q^2\lVert\bE'\rVert^2/m^2$: the particle experiences
only electric force at that instant in its rest frame.

\begin{SIunits}{Lorentz force and acceleration}
Here $t$ is in seconds, $\bv$ in metres per second, $m$ in kilograms,
$q$ in coulombs, $\bE$ in volts per metre, and $\bBf$ in teslas.
Dots denote derivatives with respect to this $t$, and
$\gamma=(1-\bv^2/c^2)^{-1/2}$. For $\bv\ne0$, the same projections give
\begin{align*}
 \dot{\bp}_{\parallel}&=q\bE_{\parallel},&
 \dot{\bp}_{\perp}&=q(\bE_{\perp}+\bv\times\bBf),\\
 \dot{\bv}_{\parallel}&=\frac{q}{m\gamma^3}\bE_{\parallel},&
 \dot{\bv}_{\perp}&=\frac{q}{m\gamma}(\bE_{\perp}+\bv\times\bBf).
\end{align*}
The force is in newtons and acceleration in $\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$.
The unsplit form, also valid at rest, is
\[
 \dot{\bv}=\frac{q}{m\gamma}
 \left(\bE+\bv\times\bBf
       -\frac{\bv\dotprod\bE}{c^2}\bv\right).
\]
With $\tau$ in seconds, $\br$ in metres, $s=c\tau$,
$\pX=ct+\br\dotprod\bsig$, and dimensionless
$\pU=\dd\pX/\dd s$, the squared proper-acceleration magnitude is
\begin{align*}
 -c^4\det\left(\frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}\right)
 &=\gamma^6\dot{\bv}_{\parallel}^2
   +\gamma^4\dot{\bv}_{\perp}^2\\
 &=\frac{q^2}{m^2}\left(\bE_{\parallel}^2
   +\gamma^2\lVert\bE_{\perp}+\bv\times\bBf\rVert^2\right),
\end{align*}
in $\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-4}}$. At rest it reduces to
$q^2\lVert\bE\rVert^2/m^2$ without choosing a projection axis.
\end{SIunits}

\subsection{Energy and momentum flux}

Notation: $\pF=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\dotprod\bsig$.  Its Hermitian product is~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(180)}{page=30}
\begin{equation*}
 \tfrac12\pF \pF^{\mathsf H}
 =\tfrac12(\bE^2+\bBf^2)+(\bE\times\bBf)\dotprod\bsig.
\end{equation*}
The energy density $u=(\bE^2+\bBf^2)/2\in\R_{\ge0}$ and Poynting
vector $\bS=\bE\times\bBf\in\R^3$ are defined.  These coefficients are frame-dependent,
whereas $\pF^2$ contains the Lorentz invariants.

\begin{SIunits}{Field energy and flux}
Using the SI field $\pF=(\bE+\ii c\bBf)\dotprod\bsig$,
\begin{gather*}
 u=\frac{\epsilon_0}{2}(\bE^2+c^2\bBf^2),\qquad
 \bS=\frac1{\mu_0}\bE\times\bBf,\\
 \frac{\epsilon_0}{2}\pF\pF^{\mathsf H}
 =u+\frac{\bS}{c}\dotprod\bsig.
\end{gather*}
Here $u$ is energy per unit volume in $\mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}$, and
$\bS$ is energy flux in $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$.
\end{SIunits}

In natural units, the full stress--energy tensor is represented by the real linear map~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(167)}{page=28}
\begin{equation}
 T(\pX):=\tfrac12\pF \pX \pF^{\mathsf H},
 \label{eq:electromagnetic-stress-map}
\end{equation}
Here $T:\R\oplus\R^3\to\R\oplus\R^3$ is a real-linear map, with
$\pX\in\R\oplus\R^3$; $T$ denotes the stress map, not a frame factor.  Its time-direction value is
$T(1)=u+\bS\dotprod\bsig$.  It is future causal because $u\geq0$ and
\begin{equation*}
 \det(T(1))=u^2-\bS^2=\tfrac14\lVert\det \pF\rVert^2\geq0.
\end{equation*}
Hence $\lVert\bS\rVert\le u$, with equality for null fields.

\begin{SIunits}{Field determinant and flux bound}
In SI units, the determinant and flux bound are
\begin{gather*}
 u^2-\frac{\bS^2}{c^2}
 =\frac{\epsilon_0^2}{4}\lVert\det\pF\rVert^2\ge0,\\
 \lVert\bS\rVert\le cu.
\end{gather*}
Equality holds for null fields.
\end{SIunits}

Returning to natural units, reversal of the order gives
$\pF^{\mathsf H}\pF=(\pF \pF^{\mathsf H})^*$: the same energy density and opposite
flux.  The transformation of the full map is derived in
Section~\ref{sec:transformations}.

Using Maxwell's vector equations,
\begin{align*}
 \partial_tu
 &=\bE\dotprod(\pdr\times\bBf-\bJ)-\bBf\dotprod(\pdr\times\bE)\\
 &=-\pdr\dotprod\bS-\bJ\dotprod\bE,
\end{align*}
where
$\pdr\dotprod(\bE\times\bBf)
=\bBf\dotprod(\pdr\times\bE)-\bE\dotprod(\pdr\times\bBf)$.
Thus Poynting's energy-balance law is~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.45)}{page=136}
\begin{equation}
 \partial_tu+\pdr\dotprod\bS=-\bJ\dotprod\bE.
 \label{eq:poynting}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the left side is
$\tfrac12\partial_t\scpart{\pF \pF^{\mathsf H}}
+\tfrac12\pdr\dotprod\vecpart{\pF \pF^{\mathsf H}}$.
The term $\bJ\dotprod\bE$ is the power delivered to matter per unit
volume.

\section{Quantum amplitudes and differential operators}
\label{sec:waves}

Here $\pX=t+\br\dotprod\bsig$ has real coordinates $(t,\br)\in\R^4$,
$m>0$ is mass, $q\in\R$ is charge, and $\hbar=c=1$.
Amplitudes are complex functions of these real coordinates;
their scalar or paravector type is specified below.

\subsection{Spinor amplitudes and probability density}
\label{sec:quantum-amplitudes}
\label{sec:paravector-inner-products}
\label{sec:axis-ideal-basis}

All products and norms here act on paravector values at one real event
$\pX$.  They sum algebra components only; no spatial integration is implied.
For $\pvvarphi,\pvpsi\in\C\oplus\C^3$, their algebraic inner product is
$\langle\pvvarphi\mid\pvpsi\rangle:=\scpart{\pvvarphi^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi}\in\C$.
Two entries separated by a vertical bar denote this inner product;
one entry in angle brackets denotes a normalized expectation below.

For arbitrary constant perpendicular \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vectors}
$\bu,\bu'$, a constant complex paravector $\pvpsi_+$ of unit norm
with $(\bu\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_+=\pvpsi_+$ is chosen, and $\pvpsi_-=(\bu'\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_+$ is defined.
Then
\begin{equation}
 (\bu\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_\pm=\pm\pvpsi_\pm.
 \label{eq:axis-ideal-basis}
\end{equation}
The pair is orthonormal:
\begin{align*}
 \scpart{\pvpsi_+^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_+}&=1,\\
 \scpart{\pvpsi_-^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_-}&=1,\\
 \scpart{\pvpsi_+^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_-}
 &=\scpart{\pvpsi_-^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_+}=0.
\end{align*}
The two cross terms are complex conjugates.
A Pauli amplitude or either Dirac entry is~\citeat{baylis-spin}{Eq.~(69a)}{page=13}
\begin{equation}
 \pvpsi(t,\br)=c_+(t,\br)\pvpsi_++c_-(t,\br)\pvpsi_-.
 \label{eq:axis-ideal-components}
\end{equation}
Here $c_+,c_-:\R^4\to\C$, and pointwise
\[
 \pvpsi\in\mathrm{span}_{\C}\{\pvpsi_+,\pvpsi_-\}\subset\C\oplus\C^3.
\]
This fixed two-dimensional complex subspace is preserved by left
multiplication by any paravector. Its coordinates are in $\C^2$,
not the full four-complex-dimensional paravector space.
Here $\pvpsi_\pm$ denote constant basis elements; in the Dirac sections they
denote fields taking values in this same subspace.
Left multiplication by $\bu\dotprod\bsig$ or $\bu'\dotprod\bsig$ preserves
this two-component form; these generate all paravector left actions.
The matrix verification is in Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix-spinors}.

The local squared norm $\rho:\R^4\to\R_{\ge0}$ is~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eq.~(1.1a)}{page=1}
\begin{equation}
 \rho:=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2=\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi}\geq0.
 \label{eq:probability-density}
\end{equation}
This $\rho$ denotes probability density, distinct from charge density
in the electromagnetic sections.
Orthonormality gives $\rho=\lVert c_+\rVert^2+\lVert c_-\rVert^2$.
For a Pauli wavefunction normalized to unit total probability, this is
its position density; Dirac uses the sum over its two entries.
Total spatial normalization is separate from the pointwise norm.
This probability interpretation does not apply to an arbitrary scalar KG field.

\subsection{Weighted densities and local expectations}
$\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ is taken to be real ($\pZ^{\mathsf H}=\pZ$),
with $c\in\R$ and $\bZ\in\R^3$.
It acts by multiplication, not differentiation.
For $\bZ\ne0$, the real unit vector $\bu=\bZ/\lVert\bZ\rVert$ is used
with the projectors $\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bsig)/2$ from
Section~\ref{sec:algebra-projectors}.  Their spectral decomposition gives
\begin{equation}
 \scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi}
 =(c+\lVert\bZ\rVert)\lVert\pvPi_+\pvpsi\rVert^2
 +(c-\lVert\bZ\rVert)\lVert\pvPi_-\pvpsi\rVert^2.
 \label{eq:weighted-density-spectral}
\end{equation}
For $\bZ=0$, this reduces to $c\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2$.
Thus the product is nonnegative for every $\pvpsi$ exactly when
$c\geq\lVert\bZ\rVert$.  For such a \emph{nonnegative weight}, the following is defined:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_\pZ^2
:=\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi}
 =\lVert\sqrt {\pZ}\,\pvpsi\rVert^2\geq0.}
 \label{eq:weighted-density}
\end{equation}
The square root has nonnegative eigenvalues, as in Section~\ref{sec:spectral}.
For $\pZ=1$, this is $\rho$.  It is a true squared norm when
$c>\lVert\bZ\rVert$; at equality it can vanish for nonzero $\pvpsi$,
so the notation then denotes a weighted density.  Projectors are an
important example.  Reality alone is insufficient: $\pZ=-1$ gives
$\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi}=-\rho$.

\paragraph{Local expectations.}
For $\rho>0$ and $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$ acting by multiplication,
the scalar expectation $\langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi\in\C$ is defined by~\citeat{sparks-quantum}{Eq.~(6.1)}{page=49}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi
:=\frac{\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi}}{\rho}.}
 \label{eq:paravector-expectation}
\end{equation}
For real $\pZ$, this is real and may have either sign.
Dividing \eqref{eq:weighted-density-spectral} by $\rho$ shows its meaning:
it averages the two eigenvalues with their projected probabilities.
For fields this mean is conditional on position; it is not a spatial
average.  A whole-wavefunction expectation additionally requires spatial
weighting.  The same formula extends linearly to complex $\pZ$, including
commutators.  For a nonnegative weight,
$\langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_\pZ^2/\rho$.
For a projector, this ratio is a Born probability.

\paragraph{Transforming the amplitude.}
For a constant complex \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} $\pT$,
the transformation $\pvpsi'=\pT\pvpsi$ is applied.  For a nonnegative weight $\pZ$, the following is obtained by substitution:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\lVert\pvpsi'\rVert_\pZ^2
 =\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pT^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pT\pvpsi}
 =\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_{\pT^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pT}^2.}
 \label{eq:weighted-density-pullback}
\end{equation}
This also proves that the transformed weight is nonnegative.
The same substitution in \eqref{eq:paravector-expectation} applies to
any complex $\pZ$.  With $\rho'=\lVert\pvpsi'\rVert^2>0$, it gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\langle \pZ\rangle_{\pvpsi'}
 =\frac{\rho}{\rho'}
   \langle \pT^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pT\rangle_\pvpsi.}
 \label{eq:amplitude-pullback}
\end{equation}
For $\pT=\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$, with real angle $\theta$
and real unit $\bu$, unitarity preserves both
$\rho'=\rho$ and $\langle\pvvarphi'\mid\pvpsi'\rangle=\langle\pvvarphi\mid\pvpsi\rangle$.
For $\pT=\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$, with real rapidity $\theta$,
this one-sided law instead gives
$\rho'=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_{\pL^2}^2$; the norm and inner product
are generally not invariant.  The two Dirac entries use opposite boost
factors, as derived in Section~\ref{sec:transformations}.

\Needspace{10\baselineskip}
\subsection{Energy and momentum as differential operators}
\label{sec:quantum-energy-momentum}

Notation: $\pX=t+\br\dotprod\bsig$, with real coordinates $(t,\br)\in\R^4$.
