\begin{SIunits}{Wave energy and momentum}
In the following wave relations, SI units are restored and Planck's
constant $h>0$ and $\hbar=h/(2\pi)$ are retained. For a free wave,
angular frequency in radians per second is denoted by $\omega\in\R$,
and the wavenumber vector in cycles per metre by $\bk\in\R^3$. For $\bk\ne0$, the wavelength
$\lambda>0$ satisfies
$\lVert\bk\rVert=1/\lambda$.

The physical input is the Planck--Einstein energy relation and de Broglie
momentum relation~\citeat{debroglie1929}{p.~248}{page=5}\,\citeat{debroglie1929}{p.~249}{page=6}:
\begin{gather}
 E=\hbar\omega,
 \label{eq:planck-einstein}\\
 \bp=h\bk.
 \label{eq:debroglie-momentum}
\end{gather}
Here $E\in\R$ is total energy in joules, including rest
energy, and $\bp\in\R^3$ is momentum in
$\mathrm{kg\,m\,s^{-1}}$.
Photoelectric measurements test the energy relation~\citeat{millikan1924}{p.~62}{page=9};
electron diffraction tests the momentum relation~\citeat{debroglie1929}{p.~255}{page=12}.

For constant $E$ and $\bp$, with time
$t$ in seconds and position $\br$ in metres,
the complex scalar plane wave is
\begin{gather*}
 f(t,\br):=\exp\!\left(-\ii\left(\omega t
             -2\pi\bk\dotprod\br\right)\right),\\
 f(t,\br)=\exp\!\left(-\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\left(Et
             -\bp\dotprod\br\right)\right).
\end{gather*}
The second line substitutes \eqref{eq:planck-einstein} and
\eqref{eq:debroglie-momentum}.  By differentiation,
\begin{gather*}
 +\ii\hbar\partial_t f=Ef,\\
 -\ii\hbar\pdr f=\bp f.
\end{gather*}
These operators represent energy and momentum on each mode and extend
to superpositions by linearity.
\end{SIunits}

Returning to natural units $\hbar=c=1$ gives $h=2\pi$. With
$\pX=t+\br\dotprod\bsig$ and
$\pP=E+\bp\dotprod\bsig$, the following is obtained~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eq.~(4.104)}{page=106}
\begin{equation}
 f(\pX,\pP)=\exp\!\left(-\ii(Et-\bp\dotprod\br)\right).
 \label{eq:plane-wave}
\end{equation}
The two eigenvalue equations combine into
\begin{equation}
 \ii\pvpartial^*f=\pP f.
 \label{eq:momentum-operator-eigenvalues}
\end{equation}

The canonical energy and momentum operators are defined by
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \hE&:=+\ii\partial_t,\\
 \hbp&:=-\ii\pdr,
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:canonical-operators}
\end{equation}
which combine to form the canonical four-momentum operator:
\begin{equation}
 \hP:=\ii\pvpartial^*=\hE+\hbp\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:canonical-paravector}
\end{equation}

Here $\hE$ is a scalar differential operator, $\hbp$ is a triple of
scalar differential operators, and $\hP$ is a paravector differential
operator. These act on fields, not elements of the numerical coefficient
spaces. On a smooth scalar field $\psi$, the pointwise outputs satisfy
$\hE\psi\in\C$, $\hbp\psi\in\C^3$, and
$\hP\psi\in\C\oplus\C^3$.
On spinor fields, $\hP$ preserves the fixed subspace of
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}. The coupled operators have the same types.

In a field, the following notation is retained: the real potential paravector
$\pA(t,\br)=V(t,\br)+\bA(t,\br)\dotprod\bsig$:
$V:\R^4\to\R$, $\bA:\R^4\to\R^3$, and
$\pA:\R^4\to\R\oplus\R^3$.
Within the coupling formulas, subscript $0$ labels the uncoupled
operators of \eqref{eq:canonical-operators}:
\begin{align*}
 \hE_0&:=+\ii\partial_t,\\
 \hbp_0&:=-\ii\pdr,\\
 \hP_0&:=\ii\pvpartial^*.
\end{align*}
The charge is $q\in\R$.  The particle action gives canonical energy
$E+qV$ and momentum $\bp+q\bA$; identifying them with translation
operators defines the gauge-covariant energy and kinetic momentum operators~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eqs.~(2.11)--(2.12)}{page=6}:
\begin{align}
 \hEq&:=\hE_0-qV,
 \label{eq:mechanical-energy-operator}\\
 \hbpq&:=\hbp_0-q\bA,
 \label{eq:mechanical-momentum-operator}
\end{align}
which combine to form the kinetic four-momentum operator:
\begin{equation}
 \hPq:=\hP_0-q\pA=\hEq+\hbpq\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:mechanical-paravector}
\end{equation}
Here $\hEq$ includes rest energy and excludes the potential energy $qV$.
Hats distinguish named differential
operators from physical values and mode eigenvalues $E,\bp$.
The derivative symbols $\pvpartial$, $\pdr$, and $\Box$ remain unhatted.
Fields obtained by differentiating potentials are coefficient fields,
not differential operators. The operators act to the right.
The adjugate of $\hPq$ reverses the vector part without conjugating $\ii$.
Operator order matters in a field; an ordinary paravector determinant
cannot replace their product.  The canonical operators commute, giving
\begin{equation}
 \hP\,\adj\hP=\det\hP=\hE^2-\hbp^2=-\Box.
 \label{eq:free-momentum-product}
\end{equation}
The same result is obtained by reversal of the order.

\subsection{Field strength from noncommuting derivatives}
Notation: the real electric and magnetic fields from
\eqref{eq:electric-potential} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-potential}, and
$S=\partial_tV+\pdr\dotprod\bA$, the real scalar in
\eqref{eq:potential-field}.  The real potential is $\pA=V+\bA\dotprod\bsig$;
$\pF=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\dotprod\bsig$ is a complex vector paravector.

The operators are applied to a differentiable complex amplitude.
The product rule cancels all derivatives of the amplitude in the
commutators; writing $\hat P_{q,j}:=(\hbpq)_j$ for Cartesian components
$j,k=1,2,3$,
\begin{align*}
 [\hEq,\hat P_{q,j}]&=-\ii q(\partial_t\pA_j+\partial_jV)=\ii qE_j,\\
 [\hat P_{q,j},\hat P_{q,k}]&=\ii q(\partial_j\pA_k-\partial_k\pA_j),\\
 \hbpq\times\hbpq&=\ii q\bBf.
\end{align*}
Sigma multiplication and the energy commutator consequently give~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eq.~(2.13)}{page=6}\,\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{p.~6}{page=6}
\begin{equation}
 (\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)^2=\hbpq^2-q\bBf\dotprod\bsig,
 \label{eq:kinetic-sigma-square}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 [\hEq,\hbpq\dotprod\bsig]=\ii q\bE\dotprod\bsig.
 \label{eq:kinetic-electric-commutator}
\end{equation}
Here $E_j$ is an electric-field component. Reversing the commutator
order and combining the electric and magnetic terms also gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 [\hbpq\dotprod\bsig,\hEq]&=-\ii q\bE\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \hbpq^2+\ii q\pF
 &=(\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)^2-[\hbpq\dotprod\bsig,\hEq].
 \end{aligned}
\]
Using \eqref{eq:kinetic-sigma-square} and
\eqref{eq:kinetic-electric-commutator} in the ordered products gives
\begin{align}
 (\hEq+\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)(\hEq-\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)
 &=\hEq^2-(\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)^2-[\hEq,\hbpq\dotprod\bsig]\notag\\
 &=\hEq^2-\hbpq^2-\ii q\pF,
 \label{eq:covariant-product-plus}\\[0.35em]
 (\hEq-\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)(\hEq+\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)
 &=\hEq^2-(\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)^2+[\hEq,\hbpq\dotprod\bsig]\notag\\
 &=\hEq^2-\hbpq^2-\ii q\pF^*.
 \label{eq:covariant-product-minus}
\end{align}
Their common scalar part gives KG; the vector terms give the Dirac
spin coupling.


\section{The Klein--Gordon equation}
\label{sec:klein-gordon-development}

\subsection{The mass shell as a wave equation}

A scalar field $\psi:\R^4\to\C$ of constant mass $m\in\R_{>0}$ is considered.
The derivation in Section~\ref{sec:four-momentum} gives the real
momentum $\pP=m\pU\in\R\oplus\R^3$ and its mass shell
$\det \pP=E^2-\bp^2=m^2$.
Represent $E\in\R$ and $\bp\in\R^3$ by the translation operators
$\hE=\ii\partial_t$ and $\hbp=-\ii\pdr$ from
\eqref{eq:canonical-operators}. Imposing the mass shell on the scalar
wavefunction gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 0&=(\det\hP-m^2)\psi\\
  &=(\hE^2-\hbp^2-m^2)\psi\\
  &=(-\partial_t^2+\pdr^2-m^2)\psi\\
  &=-(\Box+m^2)\psi.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here the free coefficients commute, so the operator determinant is
the scalar operator in \eqref{eq:free-momentum-product}.
This yields the Klein--Gordon equation~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eq.~(1.13)}{page=15}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{(\Box+m^2)\psi=0.}
 \label{eq:kg}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Positive and negative frequency}
Notation: the complex scalar field $\psi(t,\br)$ and real mass $m>0$.
Here the branch fields $\psi_\pm:\R^4\to\C$ are scalars, not Dirac entries.
The positive scalar operator is defined by
\[
 \hOmega:=\sqrt{m^2-\pdr^2},\qquad
 \hOmega^2=\det(m+\pdr\dotprod\bsig).
\]
Since $\hOmega$ commutes with $\partial_t$, KG factors as
\begin{equation*}
 \Box+m^2=\partial_t^2+\hOmega^2
        =(\partial_t-\ii\hOmega)(\partial_t+\ii\hOmega).
\end{equation*}
The reverse product is the same.  Its two frequency branches obey
\[
 (\partial_t\pm\ii\hOmega)\psi_\pm=0,
 \qquad
 \psi_\pm(t,\br)=e^{\mp\ii\hOmega t}\psi_\pm(0,\br).
\]
Their sum gives the general solution:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\psi(t,\br)=e^{-\ii\hOmega t}\psi_+(0,\br)
                         +e^{\ii\hOmega t}\psi_-(0,\br).}
 \label{eq:kg-general-solution}
\end{equation}
The two initial data determine its complex branch fields by
\[
 \psi_\pm(0,\br)=\tfrac12\left(\psi(0,\br)
             \pm\ii\hOmega^{-1}\partial_t\psi(0,\br)\right).
\]

\subsection{Square roots from Fourier series}
For spatially periodic free KG solutions, the following discrete Fourier series is used:
\[
 \psi=\sum_n c_n f_n,\qquad
 f_n(t,\br)=\exp\!\left(\ii(\omega_n t+2\pi\bk_n\dotprod\br)\right).
\]
Here $n$ labels modes, $c_n\in\C$ are constant coefficients,
$\omega_n\in\R$ are signed angular phase frequencies, and $\bk_n\in\R^3$
are wavenumber vectors in cycles per unit length.  For $\bk_n\ne0$,
its real wavelength $\lambda_n>0$ satisfies $\lVert\bk_n\rVert=1/\lambda_n$.
By \eqref{eq:debroglie-momentum}, the mode momentum is $2\pi\bk_n$
in these units.  The $+\omega_n t$ convention gives energy
eigenvalue $-\omega_n$.
By differentiation,
\begin{align*}
 \Box\psi&=\sum_n(4\pi^2\bk_n^2-\omega_n^2)c_n f_n,\\
 (\Box+m^2)\psi&=\sum_n(4\pi^2\bk_n^2+m^2-\omega_n^2)c_n f_n.
\end{align*}
Thus each nonzero KG coefficient requires
\[
 \omega_n^2=4\pi^2\bk_n^2+m^2,\qquad
 \omega_n=\pm\sqrt{4\pi^2\bk_n^2+m^2}.
\]
Both signs are independent modes~\citeat{tong-qft}{pp.~33--34}{page=39}; no common time period is assumed.
A square-root operator acts on the same coefficients by
\[
 \sqrt\Box\,\psi=\sum_n\sqrt{4\pi^2\bk_n^2-\omega_n^2}\,c_n f_n.
\]
One square-root branch is chosen consistently.  Squaring restores $\Box$, giving
\begin{equation*}
 \Box+m^2=(m-\ii\sqrt\Box)(m+\ii\sqrt\Box).
\end{equation*}
For the positive spatial root, the same series gives
\begin{align*}
 \hOmega^2\psi&=\sum_n(m^2+4\pi^2\bk_n^2)c_n f_n,\\
 \hOmega\psi&=\sum_n\sqrt{m^2+4\pi^2\bk_n^2}\,c_n f_n.
\end{align*}
Thus $e^{\mp\ii\hOmega t}$ multiplies each spatial mode by
$e^{\mp\ii\sqrt{m^2+4\pi^2\bk_n^2}\,t}$.
Unlike $\hOmega$, $\sqrt\Box$ has imaginary eigenvalues on KG modes;
its branch does not select the frequency sign.

\subsection{Electromagnetic coupling}
Notation: the real potentials $V,\bA$, charge $q$, and coupled operators
$\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$, $\hbpq=-\ii\pdr-q\bA$.
The real potential paravector is $\pA=V+\bA\dotprod\bsig$, with real
scalar $S=\partial_tV+\pdr\dotprod\bA$.
The scalar equation in a field is~\citeat{lenz-quantum}{Eq.~(299)}{page=59}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{(-\hEq^2+\hbpq^2+m^2)\psi=0.}
 \label{eq:kg-field}
\end{equation}
The mass-shell equation uses the scalar part $\hEq^2-\hbpq^2$ of
\eqref{eq:covariant-product-plus} or \eqref{eq:covariant-product-minus}.
By the product rule,
\begin{align}
 \hEq^2&=-\partial_t^2-\ii q(\partial_tV)
       -2\ii qV\partial_t+q^2V^2,
 \label{eq:energy-operator-square}\\
 \hbpq^2&=-\pdr^2+\ii q(\pdr\dotprod\bA)
       +2\ii q\bA\dotprod\pdr+q^2\bA^2.
 \label{eq:momentum-operator-square}
\end{align}
Substituting \eqref{eq:energy-operator-square} and
\eqref{eq:momentum-operator-square} into \eqref{eq:kg-field} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \boxed{\left(\Box+\ii qS-q^2\det \pA+m^2
 +2\ii q(V\partial_t+\bA\dotprod\pdr)\right)\psi=0.}
\end{equation*}

All potential derivatives are retained.

\section{The Dirac equation}
\label{sec:dirac-development}

\subsection{Block form and free factorization}

Notation: $m>0$ and real space--time coordinates $(t,\br)$.
In the Weyl representation, a Dirac field is the two-entry block
\begin{equation*}
 \bpsi(t,\br)=\begin{Bmatrix}\pvpsi_+(t,\br)\\\pvpsi_-(t,\br)\end{Bmatrix}.
\end{equation*}
Each entry takes values in the same fixed two-dimensional complex subspace
of $\C\oplus\C^3$ from \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}.
Each has coordinates in $\C^2$, so the block has coordinates in $\C^4$.
On blocks, Hermitian conjugation also transposes entries; sigma
conjugation acts entrywise.  Summing the two local squared norms gives
the nonnegative real density~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eqs.~(4.97)--(4.99)}{page=105}
\begin{equation}
 \lVert\bpsi\rVert^2
 =\scpart{\pvpsi_+^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_+}
 +\scpart{\pvpsi_-^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_-}.
 \label{eq:dirac-norm}
\end{equation}

For $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$, the complex-linear block map is defined by
\begin{equation}
 \Wop(\pZ):=\begin{Bmatrix}0&\adj {\pZ}\\\pZ&0\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:dirac-block-map}
\end{equation}
In spinor coordinates, $\Wop(\pZ)$ and $\bI$ are matrices in $\C^{4\times4}$.
For differential-operator arguments, $\Wop(\hP)$, $\hD$, and $\hDq$
are instead matrix differential operators on four-component fields.
Here $\bI$ is the two-entry block identity, represented by $\bI_4$.  Applying $\Wop(\pZ)$ twice
multiplies the first entry by $\adj {\pZ}\,\pZ$ and the second by $\pZ\adj {\pZ}$.
Both products are the paravector determinant, so
\begin{equation*}
 \Wop(\pZ)^2=\det(\pZ)\bI.
\end{equation*}
Sigma conjugation on each block entry gives $\Wop(\pZ)^*=\Wop(\pZ^*)$;
Hermitian conjugation also transposes the block:
\begin{equation*}
 \Wop(\pZ)^{\mathsf H}=\Wop(\pZ^*).
\end{equation*}
Thus $\Wop(\pZ)$ is not generally Hermitian.  For operator arguments,
$\operatorname{adj}$ reverses the vector part without reordering derivatives.
For the free field ($q=0$), the canonical operators in
\eqref{eq:canonical-operators} are used.  The block map and Dirac operator are
\begin{align}
 \Wop(\hP)&=\begin{Bmatrix}0&\adj\hP\\\hP&0\end{Bmatrix},\notag\\
 \hD(m)&:=\Wop(\hP)-m\bI,
 \label{eq:dirac-operator}\\
 \hD(-m)&=\Wop(\hP)+m\bI. \notag
\end{align}
The Dirac equation is~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eq.~(4.51)}{page=96}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\hD(m)\bpsi=0.}
 \label{eq:dirac}
\end{equation}
Using $\Wop(\hP)^2=(\det\hP)\bI=-\Box\bI$ from
\eqref{eq:free-momentum-product}, multiplication by the opposite-mass operator gives~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eqs.~(4.53)--(4.54)}{page=96}
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \hD(m)\hD(-m)&=\hD(-m)\hD(m)\\
 &=\Wop(\hP)^2-m^2\bI
 =-(\Box+m^2)\bI.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:dirac-free-factorization}
\end{equation}
Thus every free Dirac solution satisfies the wave form of the mass shell
$\det \pP=E^2-\bp^2=m^2$ in \eqref{eq:mass-shell}:
\begin{equation*}
 (\det\hP-m^2)\bpsi=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad (\Box+m^2)\bpsi=0.
\end{equation*}
The original first-order constraint is the coupled pair~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eq.~(4.108)}{page=107}
\begin{align}
 \ii\pvpartial\pvpsi_-&=(\hE-\hbp\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_-=m\pvpsi_+,\label{eq:dirac-free-first}\\
 \ii\pvpartial^*\pvpsi_+&=(\hE+\hbp\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_+=m\pvpsi_-.
 \label{eq:dirac-free-second}
\end{align}

On one Fourier mode, the eigenvalue $s\in\C$ is defined by $\hD(m)\bpsi=s\bpsi$
for a nonzero amplitude.  Here $\Box$ denotes its scalar mode eigenvalue.
Since $\hD(m)+m\bI=\Wop(\hP)$, \eqref{eq:dirac-free-factorization} gives
\[
 (\hD(m)+m\bI)^2\bpsi=-\Box\bpsi
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \bigl((s+m)^2+\Box\bigr)\bpsi=0.
\]
Thus the eigenvalues are
\[
 s=-m\pm\ii\sqrt\Box.
\]
When distinct, each allows two independent complex coefficients in
$\pvpsi_+$, with $\pvpsi_-=\ii\pvpartial^*\pvpsi_+/(s+m)$.
Each root therefore occurs twice, giving the scalar characteristic equation
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bigl((s+m)^2+\Box\bigr)^2=0.}
 \label{eq:dirac-characteristic}
\end{equation}
At $\Box=0$ the roots coalesce with total algebraic multiplicity four.
For KG modes, $\Box=-m^2$, so $s=0,-2m$; the Dirac equation selects $s=0$.
The matrix verification is \eqref{eq:matrix-dirac-characteristic}.

\subsection{Reconstructing free solutions from Klein--Gordon}
A KG solution $\psi':\R^4\to\C$ is combined with the constant basis amplitude
$\pvpsi_+$.  For constants $a,b\in\C$, a two-constant construction is
\begin{equation}
 \bpsi=\frac1m
 \begin{Bmatrix}
  ma+\ii b\pvpartial\\
  \ii a\pvpartial^*+mb
 \end{Bmatrix}\psi'\pvpsi_+.
 \label{eq:kg-dirac-general-lift}
\end{equation}
Substituting \eqref{eq:kg-dirac-general-lift} into
\eqref{eq:dirac-free-first} and \eqref{eq:dirac-free-second} gives
\begin{align*}
 \ii\pvpartial\pvpsi_--m\pvpsi_+
    &=-\frac{a}{m}(\Box+m^2)\psi'\pvpsi_+=0,\\
 \ii\pvpartial^*\pvpsi_+-m\pvpsi_-
    &=-\frac{b}{m}(\Box+m^2)\psi'\pvpsi_+=0.
\end{align*}
Taking $a=1,b=0$ gives one scalar-seeded solution.  To span every
Dirac field, $\pvpsi$ is instead expressed in the form \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components},
with both scalar coefficients solving KG.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bpsi=\begin{Bmatrix}\pvpsi\\\ii\pvpartial^*\pvpsi/m\end{Bmatrix}.}
 \label{eq:kg-dirac-complete-lift}
\end{equation}
The second Dirac equation holds by construction; the first is KG.
Conversely, every massive free solution has this form with $\pvpsi=\pvpsi_+$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:kg-general-solution} evolves its two coefficients.

\subsection{Potential and paravector components}
Notation: the real $\pA=V+\bA\dotprod\bsig$, real charge $q$, and
$\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$, $\hbpq=-\ii\pdr-q\bA$.
The kinetic four-momentum is $\hPq=\hP_0-q\pA$ from
\eqref{eq:mechanical-paravector}.
For the fixed potential $\pA$ and charge $q$, the following interacting Dirac operator is used:
\begin{align}
 \hDq(m)&:=\Wop(\hPq)-m\bI
 \label{eq:dirac-field-operator}\\
 &=\begin{Bmatrix}
 -m&\adj\hPq\\
 \hPq&-m
 \end{Bmatrix}. \notag
\end{align}
Minimal coupling gives~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(191)}{page=32}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\hDq(m)\bpsi=\bigl(\Wop(\hPq)-m\bI\bigr)\bpsi=0.}
 \label{eq:dirac-field}
\end{equation}
Its entries are
\begin{align}
 (\hEq-\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_-&=m\pvpsi_+,\label{eq:dirac-entry-one}\\
 (\hEq+\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_+&=m\pvpsi_-.
 \label{eq:dirac-entry-two}
\end{align}
With the time derivative isolated,
$\ii\partial_t\bpsi=\hH\bpsi$, where the Hamiltonian is~\citeat{lenz-quantum}{Eq.~(412)}{page=75}
\begin{equation}
 \hH:=qV\bI+\Wop(1)\bigl(m\bI-\Wop(\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)\bigr).
 \label{eq:dirac-hamiltonian}
\end{equation}
For real potentials, the Dirac equation conserves total probability
when the spatial boundary flux vanishes.  The pointwise density
\eqref{eq:dirac-norm} can still vary through probability flux.

Each coefficient is exposed by the decomposition
$\pvpsi_\pm=a_\pm+\bb_\pm\dotprod\bsig$, with complex scalar
functions $a_\pm:\R^4\to\C$ and vector functions
$\bb_\pm:\R^4\to\C^3$. Their pairs $(a_\pm,\bb_\pm)$ are restricted to
the fixed spinor space, so their four complex coefficients per entry are
not independent. By sigma multiplication,
\begin{align*}
 \hEq a_--\hbpq\dotprod\bb_-&=ma_+,\\
 \hEq\bb_--\hbpq a_--\ii\hbpq\times\bb_-&=m\bb_+,\\
 \hEq a_++\hbpq\dotprod\bb_+&=ma_-,\\
 \hEq\bb_++\hbpq a_++\ii\hbpq\times\bb_+&=m\bb_-.
\end{align*}
Every operator acts on the following coefficient.  Each entry still
has the two independent complex components of \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}.
Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix-dirac-components} converts the system to
the conventional four-component Dirac equation.

\subsection{Interacting factorization and reconstruction}
\label{sec:interacting-factorization}

Notation: the real potential $\pA=V+\bA\dotprod\bsig$, the complex field
$\pF=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\dotprod\bsig$, and
$S=\partial_tV+\pdr\dotprod\bA$.
For any field $\pvpsi:\R^4\to\C\oplus\C^3$, the product rule gives
\begin{align}
 \pvpartial^*(\pA^*\pvpsi)+\pA\pvpartial\pvpsi
 &=(S+\pF)\pvpsi+2(V\partial_t+\bA\dotprod\pdr)\pvpsi,
 \label{eq:potential-product-one}\\
 \pvpartial(\pA\pvpsi)+\pA^*\pvpartial^*\pvpsi
 &=(S+\pF^*)\pvpsi+2(V\partial_t+\bA\dotprod\pdr)\pvpsi.
 \label{eq:potential-product-two}
\end{align}
The difference of \eqref{eq:potential-product-one} and
\eqref{eq:potential-product-two} is $(\pF-\pF^*)\pvpsi=2(\bE\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi$.

Applying the block map $\Wop$ twice gives
\begin{align*}
 \Wop(\pvpartial^*)\Wop(\pA)\bpsi
   &=\begin{Bmatrix}\pvpartial(\pA\pvpsi_+)\\\pvpartial^*(\pA^*\pvpsi_-)\end{Bmatrix},\\
 \Wop(\pA)\Wop(\pvpartial^*)\bpsi
   &=\begin{Bmatrix}\pA^*\pvpartial^*\pvpsi_+\\\pA\pvpartial\pvpsi_-\end{Bmatrix},\\
 \Wop(\pA)^2&=\det \pA\,\bI.
\end{align*}
Differentiating a parenthesized product includes the potential derivative.
The argument $-m$ changes only the mass sign, keeping $q$ and $\pA$
fixed.  Multiplying and using the preceding mixed-derivative identities gives
\begin{equation}
 \hDq(-m)\hDq(m)
 =(\hEq^2-\hbpq^2-m^2)\bI
 -\ii q\begin{Bmatrix}\pF^*&0\\0&\pF\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:dirac-interacting-operator-factorization}
\end{equation}
The derivatives act on potentials as well as amplitudes.
For every Dirac solution,
\[
 \hDq(-m)\hDq(m)\bpsi=0.
\]
At $q=0$, this recovers \eqref{eq:dirac-free-factorization} and hence the
free Klein--Gordon equation. With coupling, its entries obey
\begin{align*}
 \left((\pvpartial+\ii q\pA^*)(\pvpartial^*+\ii q\pA)+m^2\right)\pvpsi_+&=0,\\
 \left((\pvpartial^*+\ii q\pA)(\pvpartial+\ii q\pA^*)+m^2\right)\pvpsi_-&=0.
\end{align*}
The exact second-order equations are obtained by expansion~\citeat{feynman-gellmann}{Eq.~(6)}{page=2}
\begin{align}
 (-\hEq^2+\hbpq^2+m^2+\ii q\pF^*)\pvpsi_+&=0,
 \label{eq:dirac-squared-first}\\
 (-\hEq^2+\hbpq^2+m^2+\ii q\pF)\pvpsi_-&=0.
 \label{eq:dirac-squared-field}
\end{align}
For the spin-term components, the notation $\bZ=\bE+\ii\bBf$ is used, with
$\pvpsi=a+\bb\dotprod\bsig$, with complex functions
$a:\R^4\to\C$ and $\bb,\bZ:\R^4\to\C^3$.
Here $\bZ^*$ conjugates its scalar coefficients.  By sigma multiplication,
\begin{align*}
 \ii q\pF\pvpsi
 &=\ii q\bZ\dotprod\bb+
   (\ii qa\bZ-q\bZ\times\bb)\dotprod\bsig,
 \\
 \ii q\pF^*\pvpsi
 &=-\ii q\bZ^*\dotprod\bb+
   (-\ii qa\bZ^*+q\bZ^*\times\bb)\dotprod\bsig.
\end{align*}
Solutions are constructed from a complex paravector amplitude $\pvpsi$ of
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components} satisfying
\eqref{eq:dirac-squared-field} with $\pvpsi$ in place of $\pvpsi_-$. The Dirac field is defined by
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bpsi=\begin{Bmatrix}(\hEq-\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi/m\\\pvpsi\end{Bmatrix}.}
 \label{eq:dirac-field-reconstruction}
\end{equation}
The first Dirac equation holds by construction; the second is the seed
equation.  Every massive solution has this form with $\pvpsi=\pvpsi_-$.
That seed equation must be solved for the chosen potential: a scalar KG
seed lacks the spin term, and independently solved squared entries need
not satisfy the first-order coupling.

\subsection{Gauge phase}
Notation: the real scalar $\lambda:\R^4\to\R$ and gauge transformation
$\pA'=\pA+\pvpartial^*\lambda$, or
$V'=V+\partial_t\lambda$, $\bA'=\bA-\pdr\lambda$.
For $\hEq':=\ii\partial_t-qV'$, $\hbpq':=-\ii\pdr-q\bA'$, and
$\hPq':=\hEq'+\hbpq'\dotprod\bsig$, the product rule gives
\begin{align*}
 (\hEq+\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)(e^{\ii q\lambda}\pvpsi_\pm')
 &=e^{\ii q\lambda}
   (\hEq-q\partial_t\lambda+\hbpq\dotprod\bsig
      +q(\pdr\lambda)\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_\pm'\\
 &=e^{\ii q\lambda}(\hEq'+\hbpq'\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_\pm'.
\end{align*}
The adjugate block follows with the vector signs reversed.
Thus the new amplitude is
\begin{equation*}
 \bpsi'=e^{-\ii q\lambda}\bpsi.
\end{equation*}
Equivalently,
\begin{align*}
 \hEq'\bpsi'&=e^{-\ii q\lambda}\hEq\bpsi,\\
 \hbpq'\bpsi'&=e^{-\ii q\lambda}\hbpq\bpsi.
\end{align*}
Consequently~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eqs.~(6.68)--(6.69)}{page=143}
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\bigl(\Wop(\hPq')-m\bI\bigr)\bpsi'\\
 &\qquad=e^{-\ii q\lambda}
       \bigl(\Wop(\hPq)-m\bI\bigr)\bpsi.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:gauge-operator-covariance}
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:gauge-operator-covariance} preserves the Dirac equation;
the phase sign is fixed by the potential shift.
The factor $e^{-\ii q\lambda}\in\C$ has modulus one and preserves the
spinor space, so the norm is unchanged.  The same product rules
preserve the KG and Pauli equations.

\section{Low-energy limits}
\label{sec:low-energy-limits}

Notation: $m\in\R_{>0}$, $q\in\R$, and potentials
$V:\R^4\to\R$, $\bA:\R^4\to\R^3$.
The coupled operators are $\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$ and
$\hbpq=-\ii\pdr-q\bA$; the fields are
$\bE=-\pdr V-\partial_t\bA$ and $\bBf=\pdr\times\bA$.
The rest oscillation is removed and the leading nonrelativistic terms are retained.
Schr\"odinger is obtained from KG, and Pauli from Dirac.

\subsection{Klein--Gordon to Schr\"odinger}
\label{sec:schrodinger-limit}

Schr\"odinger dynamics is obtained from the scalar Klein--Gordon equation
after removal of the rest oscillation. The coupled operators $\hEq,\hbpq$
defined above are retained. For the full scalar KG field $\psi':\R^4\to\C$,
its scalar envelope $\psi:\R^4\to\C$ is defined by~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eqs.~(2.103)--(2.104)}{page=47}
\begin{equation}
 \psi'(t,\br)=e^{-\ii mt}\psi(t,\br).
 \label{eq:kg-rest-phase}
\end{equation}
The product rule makes the rest-energy shift explicit:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \hEq\psi'
 &=(\ii\partial_t-qV)(e^{-\ii mt}\psi)\\
 &=e^{-\ii mt}(m\psi+\ii\partial_t\psi-qV\psi)\\
 &=e^{-\ii mt}(m+\hEq)\psi,\\[4pt]
 \hbpq\psi'&=e^{-\ii mt}\hbpq\psi.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Since $m$ is constant, applying $\hEq$ again gives
$\hEq^2\psi'=e^{-\ii mt}(\hEq+m)^2\psi$; spatial derivatives leave the
phase untouched.  Substitution into \eqref{eq:kg-field} therefore gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 0&=(-\hEq^2+\hbpq^2+m^2)\psi'\\
  &=e^{-\ii mt}\bigl(-(\hEq+m)^2+\hbpq^2+m^2\bigr)\psi\\
  &=e^{-\ii mt}(-\hEq^2-2m\hEq+\hbpq^2)\psi.
 \end{aligned}
\]
On the envelope, $\hEq$ measures energy above rest, excluding $qV$.
The rest terms cancel. By division by the nonzero phase, the following is obtained:
\begin{equation}
 \hEq\psi=\frac{\hbpq^2-\hEq^2}{2m}\psi.
 \label{eq:kg-residual-recursion}
\end{equation}
For weak, slowly varying fields and a slow positive-energy envelope,
a real expansion parameter $0<v\ll1$ is chosen with $\hEq/m=O(v^2)$.
Then $\hEq^2\psi$ is
smaller than $2m\hEq\psi$ by $O(v^2)$.  Dropping it at leading order
gives~\citeat{feynman-schrodinger}{Eq.~(21.3)}{Eq:III:21:3}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\ii\partial_t\psi=\left(
 \frac{(-\ii\pdr-q\bA)^2}{2m}+qV\right)\psi.}
 \label{eq:schrodinger}
\end{equation}
With $\bA=0$, hence $\bBf=0$, the kinetic-energy operator is $-\pdr^2/(2m)$,
and the conventional scalar form is
\[
 \ii\partial_t\psi=\left(-\frac{\pdr^2}{2m}+qV\right)\psi.
\]
Schr\"odinger and Pauli occur at the same leading order $1/m$.
The following Dirac limit retains the magnetic spin term; when $\bBf=0$,
each complex coefficient of the Pauli amplitude obeys this same scalar
Schr\"odinger equation.

\subsection{Dirac to Pauli}
\label{sec:low-energy-recursion}
\label{sec:pauli-limit}

The fields $\pvpsi_\pm,\pvpsi_\pm',\pvphi_\pm$ all take values in the same fixed
two-dimensional complex subspace of $\C\oplus\C^3$, with coordinate
columns in $\C^2$. The phase and change of blocks below preserve this domain.

The Dirac envelopes are defined by the same rest-energy phase shift~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eq.~(2.1)}{page=4}:
\begin{equation}
 \pvpsi_\pm':=e^{\ii mt}\pvpsi_\pm.
 \label{eq:dirac-rest-phase}
\end{equation}
By the product rule,
\begin{equation*}
 \hEq\pvpsi_\pm=e^{-\ii mt}(m+\hEq)\pvpsi_\pm'.
\end{equation*}
On the envelopes, $\hEq$ measures energy above rest, excluding $qV$.
The two rows of the Dirac equation, \eqref{eq:dirac-entry-one} and
\eqref{eq:dirac-entry-two}, become
\begin{align*}
 (m+\hEq-\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_-'&=m\pvpsi_+',\\
 (m+\hEq+\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_+'&=m\pvpsi_-'.
\end{align*}
At free positive-energy rest, $\pvpsi_+'=\pvpsi_-'$.  The large and small components are defined by
\begin{align}
 \pvphi_+&:=\frac{\pvpsi_+'+\pvpsi_-'}{\sqrt2},
 \label{eq:dirac-large-amplitude}\\
 \pvphi_-&:=\frac{\pvpsi_-'-\pvpsi_+'}{\sqrt2}.
 \label{eq:dirac-small-amplitude}
\end{align}
The exact pair is obtained by addition and subtraction~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eqs.~(2.15)--(2.16)}{page=6}:
\begin{align}
 \hEq\pvphi_+&=(\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)\pvphi_-,
 \label{eq:dirac-large-component}\\
 (2m+\hEq)\pvphi_-&=(\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)\pvphi_+.
 \label{eq:dirac-small-recursion}
\end{align}
For nonrelativistic positive-energy states in weak, slowly varying fields,
$\hEq\pvphi_-$ is neglected relative to $2m\pvphi_-$. The small component
is substituted into the first equation, and \eqref{eq:kinetic-sigma-square} is applied:
\begin{align*}
 \pvphi_-&\simeq\frac{\hbpq\dotprod\bsig}{2m}\pvphi_+,\\
 \hEq\pvphi_+&\simeq\frac{(\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)^2}{2m}\pvphi_+
 =\frac{\hbpq^2-q\bBf\dotprod\bsig}{2m}\pvphi_+.
\end{align*}
Restoring $\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$ gives Pauli's equation through order
$1/m$~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eq.~(2.13)}{page=6}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\ii\partial_t\pvphi_+=\left(
  \frac{(-\ii\pdr-q\bA)^2}{2m}+qV
  -\frac{q}{2m}\bBf\dotprod\bsig\right)\pvphi_+.}
 \label{eq:pauli}
\end{equation}

\begin{SIunits}{Pauli's equation}
With $m$ in kilograms, $q$ in coulombs, $V$ in volts, $\bA$ in
$\mathrm{T\,m}$, and $\bBf$ in teslas, restoring $\hbar$ gives
\[
 \ii\hbar\partial_t\pvphi_+
 =\left(\frac{(-\ii\hbar\pdr-q\bA)^2}{2m}+qV
 -\frac{q\hbar}{2m}\bBf\dotprod\bsig\right)\pvphi_+.
\]
Each term in parentheses has energy units. The same nonrelativistic
approximation as in \eqref{eq:pauli} is retained.
\end{SIunits}

Returning to natural units, the change of entries preserves the density exactly:
\begin{equation*}
 \lVert\bpsi\rVert^2=\lVert\pvphi_+\rVert^2+\lVert\pvphi_-\rVert^2.
\end{equation*}
The leading probability density is $\lVert\pvphi_+\rVert^2$.
Its two complex components are the Pauli amplitudes, as in
Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix-spinors}. This is the amplitude used in the spin discussion.
For a real expansion parameter $0<v\ll1$, the scaling $\hbpq/m=O(v)$, $\hEq/m=O(v^2)$,
$qV/m=O(v^2)$, $q\bBf/m^2=O(v^2)$, $q\bE/m^2=O(v^3)$,
with fields varying on spatial scales $1/(mv)$ and time scales $1/(mv^2)$
or longer, gives $\pvphi_-=O(v\pvphi_+)$ and omitted energy terms of order $mv^4$.

\subsection{Spin components in a uniform magnetic field}
\label{sec:uniform-spin}

For a constant field $\bm B\in\R^3\setminus\{0\}$, its \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{unit direction} and \hyperref[sec:algebra-projectors]{spin projectors} are defined by:

\[
\vstyle{u}:=\frac{\vstyle{B}}{\lVert\vstyle{B}\rVert}\in\R^3,
\qquad \pvPi_\pm:=\frac12(1\pm\vstyle{u}\dotprod\bsig)\in\R\oplus\R^3.
\]

\hyperref[sec:spectral]{Spectral decomposition} gives

\[
\vstyle{B}\dotprod\bsig
=\lVert\vstyle{B}\rVert(\pvPi_+-\pvPi_-),
\qquad
\pvPi_\pm(\vstyle{B}\dotprod\bsig)
=\pm\lVert\vstyle{B}\rVert\pvPi_\pm.
\]

The two spin amplitudes $\phi_{++},\phi_{+-}:\R^4\to\C$ are encoded in an aligned paravector
$\pvphi_+:\R^4\to\mathrm{span}_{\C}\{1,\bm u\dotprod\bsig\}\subset\C\oplus\C^3$
by spectral decomposition:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\pvphi_+&=\phi_{++}\pvPi_++\phi_{+-}\pvPi_-,\\
\pvPi_\pm\pvphi_+&=\pvphi_+\pvPi_\pm=\phi_{+\pm}\pvPi_\pm.
\end{aligned}
\]

Constant $\pvPi_\pm$ commute with $\hEq$ and $\hbpq^2$,
so the Pauli equation gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
0&=\pvPi_\pm\left(2m\hEq-\hbpq^2+q\bm B\dotprod\bsig\right)\pvphi_+\\
&=\left(\left(2m\hEq-\hbpq^2
\pm q\lVert\bm B\rVert\right)\phi_{+\pm}\right)\pvPi_\pm.
\end{aligned}
\]

Since $\pvPi_\pm\ne0$, the scalar coefficients vanish.
Restoring $\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$ gives
\[
\boxed{\ii\partial_t\phi_{+\pm}=
\left(\frac{\hbpq^2\mp q\lVert\bm B\rVert}{2m}+qV\right)\phi_{+\pm}.}
\]

States with \hyperref[sec:spin-half]{spin projections} $\pm\tfrac12$ along $\bm u$ have opposite
\hyperref[sec:uniform-spin]{Zeeman shifts}. The
\hyperref[eq:mechanical-momentum-operator]{kinetic momentum}
$\hbpq=-\ii\partial_{\bm r}-q\bm A$ retains the orbital coupling.

The separation $\Delta E$ of the two spin-energy contributions, excluding
orbital energy, is
\[
 \Delta E=\frac{|q|}{m}\lVert\bBf\rVert.
\]
\begin{SIunits}{Zeeman splitting}
Restoring SI units gives
\[
 \Delta E=\frac{|q|\hbar}{m}\lVert\bBf\rVert.
\]
This is the splitting from the derived Dirac magnetic coupling; anomalous
magnetic-moment corrections are not included.
\end{SIunits}

This representation applies to uniform-field Pauli evolution; the full
Dirac equation and spin measurements use the fixed spinor space.

The four complex amplitudes of the Dirac spinor, after removal of
the rest-energy phase, are arranged as:
\[
\vstyle{\phi}:=\left(\,\begin{matrix}
\phi_{++}&\phi_{+-}\\
\phi_{-+}&\phi_{--}
\end{matrix}\,\right)\in\C^{2\times2}.
\]
Dirac blocks are indexed by rows and spin projections by columns; the upper row is governed by the Pauli limit.

In a basis diagonalizing $\bm u\dotprod\bsig$, the aligned paravector is
$\mathrm{diag}(\phi_{++},\phi_{+-})$. The corresponding spinor in the
fixed subspace of \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components} is
$\phi_{++}\pZ_++\phi_{+-}\pZ_-$, where constant orthonormal $\pZ_\pm$ satisfy
$\pvPi_\pm \pZ_\pm=\pZ_\pm$ and $\pvPi_\pm \pZ_\mp=0$.
Both representations give the same scalar Pauli equations.
The full Dirac coupling $\hbpq\dotprod\bsig$ need not preserve alignment.

For the original spinor blocks, the lower block still satisfies
$\pvphi_-\simeq(\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)\pvphi_+/(2m)$.
For a nonzero field with varying direction, its projectors depend on $(t,\br)$.
With $\hH$ the Pauli Hamiltonian in \eqref{eq:pauli},
the product rule for the original spinor gives
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \ii\partial_t(\pvPi_\pm\pvphi_+)
 &=\ii(\partial_t\pvPi_\pm)\pvphi_++\pvPi_\pm \hH\pvphi_+\\
 &=\hH(\pvPi_\pm\pvphi_+)
   +\left(\ii\partial_t\pvPi_\pm
          +\frac{[\pvPi_\pm,\hbpq^2]}{2m}\right)\pvphi_+.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Only the projector derivatives and kinetic commutator couple the
sectors; the magnetic term commutes with its own projectors.

\subsection{Evolution about an alternate spin axis}
\label{sec:alternate-spin-alignment}
A constant unit vector $\bm u'\in\R^3$ and $0\le\theta\le\pi$ are chosen.
Initial scalar wavefunctions $\phi_{++},\phi_{+-}:\R^3\to\C$ are chosen along this axis:
\[
\begin{gathered}
\bm u'\dotprod\bm u=\cos\theta,\qquad
\pvPi_\pm':=\frac12(1\pm\bm u'\dotprod\bsig)\in\R\oplus\R^3,\\
\pvphi_+(0)=\phi_{++}\pvPi_+'+\phi_{+-}\pvPi_-'\in\C\oplus\C^3.
\end{gathered}
\]

The magnetic term has both diagonal and off-diagonal parts in these projectors:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\pvPi_\pm'(\bm u\dotprod\bsig)\pvPi_\pm'
&=\pm\cos\theta\,\pvPi_\pm',\\
\pvPi_\mp'(\bm u\dotprod\bsig)\pvPi_\pm'
&=\bigl((\bm u-\cos\theta\,\bm u')\dotprod\bsig\bigr)\pvPi_\pm'.
\end{aligned}
\]

By projection of the Pauli equation,
\[
\begin{aligned}
2m\hEq\pvPi_\pm'\pvphi_+
&=\left(\hbpq^2\mp q\lVert\bm B\rVert\cos\theta\right)\pvPi_\pm'\pvphi_+\\
&\quad-q\lVert\bm B\rVert
\pvPi_\pm'(\bm u\dotprod\bsig)\pvPi_\mp'\pvphi_+.
\end{aligned}
\]

Initially $\pvPi_\mp'\pvphi_+(0)\pvPi_\pm'=0$, but
\[
\left.\ii\partial_t\bigl(\pvPi_\mp'\pvphi_+\pvPi_\pm'\bigr)\right|_{t=0}
=-\frac{q\lVert\bm B\rVert}{2m}\,
\phi_{+\pm}\bigl((\bm u-\cos\theta\,\bm u')\dotprod\bsig\bigr)\pvPi_\pm'.
\]

For $q\ne0$ and $\sin\theta\ne0$, evolution generates off-diagonal components:
two scalar multiples of the fixed $\pvPi_\pm'$ no longer suffice.
At $\theta=0$ the earlier equations return; at $\theta=\pi$ the signs exchange.
For perpendicular axes, the magnetic term is entirely off-diagonal.

For time-independent $V:\R^3\to\R$ and $\bm A:\R^3\to\R^3$,
the orbital operator commutes with the constant magnetic term.
The full Pauli evolution of this initial paravector is
\[
\begin{aligned}
\pvphi_+(t)
&=e^{-\ii t(\hbpq^2/(2m)+qV)}
  e^{\,\ii q\lVert\bm B\rVert t\,\bm u\dotprod\bsig/(2m)}\pvphi_+(0)\\
&=e^{-\ii t(\hbpq^2/(2m)+qV)}
  \left(\cos\frac{q\lVert\bm B\rVert t}{2m}
  +\ii\bm u\dotprod\bsig\sin\frac{q\lVert\bm B\rVert t}{2m}\right)\pvphi_+(0).
\end{aligned}
\]
The exponential follows from $(\bm u\dotprod\bsig)^2=1$.
Its transverse contribution produces the off-diagonal terms above.

\subsection{The next free order}
\label{sec:free-energy-expansion}

For the free complex scalar envelope $\pvpsi(t,\br)$, the conditions $qV=0$ and
$q\bA=0$ are imposed, so $\hEq=\hE$, $\hbpq=\hbp$, and the operators commute.
On the positive-frequency KG branch, solving
\eqref{eq:kg-residual-recursion} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \hE\pvpsi=\left(\sqrt{m^2+\hbp^2}-m\right)\pvpsi.
\end{equation*}
The same energy acts on either normalized positive-energy Dirac spin
component.  Iteration starts with $\hE\pvpsi\simeq\hbp^2\pvpsi/(2m)$;
its square supplies the next term~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eq.~(2.19)}{page=6}:
\begin{equation*}
 \hE\pvpsi=\left(\frac{\hbp^2}{2m}-\frac{\hbp^4}{8m^3}
             +O\!\left(\frac{\hbp^6}{m^5}\right)\right)\pvpsi.
\end{equation*}

For the charged scalar equation, the same iteration applies whenever
$\hEq$ commutes with $\hbpq^2$. On the positive-energy branch,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \hEq\pvpsi
 &=\left(\sqrt{m^2+\hbpq^2}-m\right)\pvpsi\\
 &=\left(\frac{\hbpq^2}{2m}-\frac{\hbpq^4}{8m^3}
   +O\!\left(\frac{\hbpq^6}{m^5}\right)\right)\pvpsi.
 \end{aligned}
\]
This expansion applies when the kinetic momenta in the state are small
compared with $m$.

\section{Spin and probability}
\label{sec:spin-and-probability}
\label{sec:paravector-spin-content}

Here $\pvpsi$ takes values in the fixed spinor space of
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}, with coordinates in $\C^2$.
Axes and polarizations $\bu,\bu',\ba\in\R^3$ are unit vectors;
angles $\theta,\varphi\in\R$, projectors $\pvPi_\pm,\pvPi_\pm'\in\R\oplus\R^3$,
and probabilities lie between $0$ and $1$, inclusive.
The density $\rho\in\R_{\ge0}$ is a probability density.

Spin is specified by the intrinsic rotation of an amplitude.  Here the state is rotated
at fixed coordinates.  A scalar KG amplitude stays unchanged
under this intrinsic action and has spin zero; a two-component amplitude
has the one-sided law below.  The wave equation alone does not fix spin:
free Dirac components also satisfy KG.

\subsection{Spin one-half and rotations}
\label{sec:spin-half}

Notation: the complex paravector amplitude $\pvpsi(t,\br)$ of
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}.  For a
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit axis} $\bu$ and real angle
$\theta$, the following unitary rotation factor is used:
$\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$ of
\eqref{eq:rotation-exponential}.  The spinor law is~\citeat{baylis-spin}{p.~4}{page=4}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pvpsi'=\pR\pvpsi.}
 \label{eq:spin-half-law}
\end{equation}
Writing its infinitesimal action as
$\pvpsi'=\pvpsi-\ii\theta \pS(\bu)\pvpsi+O(\theta^2)$ defines the spin
operator along $\bu$, given by left multiplication by the real pure-vector
paravector $\pS(\bu)\in\{0\}\oplus\R^3$~\citeat{sparks-quantum}{p.~62}{page=66}:
\begin{equation}
 \pS(\bu)\pvpsi=\tfrac12(\bu\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi.
 \label{eq:spin-half-generator}
\end{equation}
Since $(\bu\dotprod\bsig)^2=1$, its two spin projections are
$\pm1/2$ in units $\hbar=1$.  A $2\pi$ rotation gives $\pR=-1$,
reversing the amplitude's sign; a $4\pi$ rotation restores it.
Probabilities are unchanged by the overall sign, while a relative sign
can be detected by interference~\citeat{feynmanspin}{Eq.~(6.18)}{Eq:III:6:18}.
Each Dirac entry obeys this same active rotation law.

For a real paravector observable $\pZ$, the state law
\eqref{eq:spin-half-law} gives, by \eqref{eq:amplitude-pullback},
\begin{equation*}
 \langle \pZ\rangle_{\pvpsi'}=\langle \pR^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pR\rangle_\pvpsi.
\end{equation*}
The observable inside the original state's pairing rotates through
$-\theta$.  If the observable rotates with the state,
$\pZ'=\pR \pZ \pR^{\mathsf H}$, then
$\langle \pZ'\rangle_{\pvpsi'}=\langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi$.

\subsection{Projectors and probabilities}
\label{sec:physical-spinors}

Notation: a nonzero Pauli amplitude $\pvpsi$ of \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}.
For a real unit measurement axis $\bu$, the real projectors are
\begin{equation*}
 \pvPi_\pm=\tfrac12(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bsig).
\end{equation*}
Their algebra in Section~\ref{sec:algebra-projectors} gives
\begin{align*}
 \pvpsi&=\pvPi_+\pvpsi+\pvPi_-\pvpsi,\\
 \pS(\bu)(\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi)&=\pm\tfrac12\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi,\\
 \scpart{(\pvPi_+\pvpsi)^{\mathsf H}(\pvPi_-\pvpsi)}&=0,\\
 \lVert\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\rVert^2
 &=\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi}=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_{\pvPi_\pm}^2.
\end{align*}
By \eqref{eq:weighted-density}, these are nonnegative weighted densities.
The Born probability is this density divided by
$\rho=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2$.  In the fixed orthonormal basis
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-basis}, $\pvpsi=c_+\pvpsi_++c_-\pvpsi_-$ with
complex scalars $c_\pm$, so
$\rho=\lVert c_+\rVert^2+\lVert c_-\rVert^2$.
When the measurement axis is that basis axis, the probabilities are
$\lVert c_\pm\rVert^2/\rho$.
The same-side and cross-side projector products are already derived in
Section~\ref{sec:algebra-projectors}.

The real spin-polarization vector $\bu'\in\R^3$ is defined by its projection
along every real unit measurement axis $\bu$:
\begin{equation}
 \bu\dotprod\bu'
:=\frac{\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}(\bu\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi}}{\rho}.
 \label{eq:spin-state}
\end{equation}
The right side is real and linear in $\bu$, so it uniquely specifies
$\bu'$.  For the arbitrary perpendicular basis axes $\bu,\ba$ in
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}, expanding the same pairing gives
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \rho\bu'={}&(\lVert c_+\rVert^2-\lVert c_-\rVert^2)\bu\\
 &+2\repart{c_+^*c_-}\ba
 +2\impart{c_+^*c_-}(\bu\times\ba).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
These three directions are orthonormal, hence
\begin{equation*}
 \rho^2(\bu')^2
 =(\lVert c_+\rVert^2-\lVert c_-\rVert^2)^2+4\lVert c_+^*c_-\rVert^2
 =\rho^2.
\end{equation*}
Thus $\bu'$ is a real unit vector, with mean spin $\bu'/2$.
For any real unit measurement axis,
inserting $\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bsig)/2$ directly into the pairing gives
\[
 \lVert\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\rVert^2
 =\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi}
 =\tfrac12\rho(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bu').
\]
Therefore~\citeat{baylis-spin}{Eqs.~(66)--(67)}{page=12}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\mathrm{prob}(\pm\mid\bu)=\frac{\lVert\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\rVert^2}{\rho}
                    =\tfrac12(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bu').}
 \label{eq:spin-probability-intrinsic}
\end{equation}
The mean spin along $\bu$ is
$(\mathrm{prob}(+)-\mathrm{prob}(-))/2=\bu\dotprod\bu'/2$.
For a spatial wavefunction, \eqref{eq:spin-probability-intrinsic}
is the spin probability conditional on position, not a position density.

\subsection{Measurement along a second axis}
\label{sec:spin-measurement-angle}

The prepared spin direction and measurement axis are denoted by the real
unit vectors $\bu'$ and $\bu$, respectively. A nonzero spin-up amplitude
$\pvpsi=\pvPi_+'\pvpsi$ is prepared, where $\pvPi_+'=(1+\bu'\dotprod\bsig)/2$.
The angle between the axes is denoted by $0\le\theta\le\pi$, and the following notation is used:
\[
 K':=\bu'\dotprod\bsig,\qquad K:=\bu\dotprod\bsig,\qquad
 c':=\bu\dotprod\bu'=\cos\theta.
\]

Here $K,K'\in\{0\}\oplus\R^3$ and $-1\le c'\le1$.
The Pauli product gives $(K')^2=1$ and $K'K+KK'=2c'$.
Multiplication by $K'$ gives $K'KK'=2c'K'-K$, hence
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \pvPi_+'K\pvPi_+'
 &=\frac14(1+K')K(1+K')\\
 &=\frac14(K+K'K+KK'+K'KK')\\
 &=\frac14(2c'+2c'K')=c'\pvPi_+'.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The measurement projectors $\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm K)/2$ therefore satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \pvPi_+'\pvPi_\pm\pvPi_+'
 =\frac12\left(\pvPi_+'\pm\pvPi_+'K\pvPi_+'\right)
 =\frac{1\pm c'}{2}\pvPi_+'.
 \label{eq:spin-projector-overlap}
\end{equation}

Using $\lVert \pZ\rVert^2=\scpart{\pZ \pZ^{\mathsf H}}$ for $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$,
the denominator is
\[
\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2=\scpart{\pvpsi\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}}>0.
\]

For the prepared state, the projected squared norm is
\[
\begin{aligned}
\lVert\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\rVert^2
&=\scpart{(\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi)(\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi)^{\mathsf H}}\\
&=\scpart{\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}}\\
&=\scpart{\pvPi_+'\pvPi_\pm\pvPi_+'\pvpsi\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}}\\
&=\frac{1\pm\cos\theta}{2}\scpart{\pvpsi\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}}
=\frac{1\pm\cos\theta}{2}\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2.
\end{aligned}
\]

The Born rule divides by the nonzero squared norm, so no prior normalization
is needed:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \mathrm{prob}(\pm)
 :=\frac{\lVert\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\rVert^2}{\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2}
 =\frac{1\pm\cos\theta}{2},\\[4pt]
 \boxed{\mathrm{prob}(+)=\cos^2\frac\theta2,\qquad
 \mathrm{prob}(-)=\sin^2\frac\theta2.}
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:spin-half-angle-probabilities}
\end{equation}
Here $+$ and $-$ label measured spin projections $+1/2$ and $-1/2$ along $\bu$.
The probabilities sum to one. Aligned axes give spin up with certainty,
perpendicular axes give equal probabilities, and opposite axes give
spin down with certainty. Preparing $\pvpsi=\pvPi_-'\pvpsi$ interchanges the
probabilities. For a spatial wavefunction with the same spin preparation
at every position, integrating both norm densities gives the same result.

\subsection{Rotating the polarization}

For $\pR=\exp(-\ii\varphi\ba\dotprod\bsig/2)$, with real unit rotation
axis $\ba$ and real angle $\varphi$, the polarization
of the rotated amplitude $\pvpsi'=\pR\pvpsi$ is denoted by $\bu''$. The amplitude law
\eqref{eq:spin-half-law} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \bu\dotprod\bu''=\langle \pR^{\mathsf H}(\bu\dotprod\bsig)\pR\rangle_\pvpsi.
\end{equation*}
This is the inverse of Rodrigues' rotation~\eqref{eq:rodrigues}:
the measurement axis rotates through $-\varphi$, so $\bu''$ rotates
through $+\varphi$ about $\ba$.
Thus the polarization rotates as a vector although its amplitude has
spin one-half.  Its overlap with the original polarization is
\[
 \bu'\dotprod\bu''=(\ba\dotprod\bu')^2
   +\left(1-(\ba\dotprod\bu')^2\right)\cos\varphi.
\]
For $\ba\dotprod\bu'=0$, the probability to retain the initial spin
is $\cos^2(\varphi/2)$.

\section{Lorentz covariance and electromagnetic transformations}
\label{sec:transformations}

At corresponding events, the domains are
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \pX,\pU,\pP,\pA,\pJ\in\R\oplus\R^3,\qquad
 \pF\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3,\\
 \pT\in\C\oplus\C^3,\quad \det \pT=1,\qquad
 \pR\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3,\quad \pL\in\R\oplus\R^3,\\
 t,s,E,V,\rho,\theta,\beta,\gamma\in\R,\qquad
 \br,\bv,\bp,\bA,\bE,\bBf,\bJ,\bu\in\R^3.
 \end{gathered}
\]
Here $\bu^2=1$, $|\beta|<1$, $\gamma\ge1$, and $\rho$ is charge density.
Transformed quantities retain their types. Scalar KG fields take values in
$\C$; Dirac entries remain in their fixed two-dimensional complex spinor space.

The algebraic actions of Section~\ref{sec:preserved-quantities}
are now applied to the physical variables already defined.  Appendix~\ref{sec:conventions}
compares alternative exponent signs and sandwich orders.  Primed fields are evaluated at
$\pX'$ and unprimed fields at $\pX$.  The frame factors here are constant;
varying factors are treated in Section~\ref{sec:varying-transformations}.

\subsection{Transformation laws for fields and derivatives}

$\pT$ is taken to be a complex \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector}
with \hyperref[eq:unit-determinant-paravector]{$\det \pT=1$}.  It may be any ordered
product of $\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$ and
$\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$, with
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$ and real angle
or rapidity $\theta$.  Notation: the real event $\pX$, momentum $\pP$, potential
$\pA$, and electric current $\pJ$.
Each transforms by~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(24)}{page=20}
\begin{equation}
 \pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H},
 \qquad \pZ=\pX,\pP,\pA,\pJ.
 \label{eq:physical-lorentz-congruence}
\end{equation}
Since $\det \pT=1$, sigma conjugation gives $\pT^*=(\pT^{-1})^{\mathsf H}$.
The derivative and complex field $\pF=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\dotprod\bsig$ use~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(33)}{page=22}
\begin{align}
 \pvpartial'&=\pT^*\pvpartial \pT^{-1},
 \label{eq:derivative-transformation}\\
 \pF'&=\pT \pF \pT^{-1}.
 \label{eq:field-transformation}
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{eq:derivative-transformation} follows from the chain rule.
For a differentiable scalar $f:\R^4\to\C$ with $f'(\pX')=f(\pX)$,
\[
 \dd f=\scpart{(\pvpartial f)\dd \pX}
 =\scpart{\bigl(\pT^*(\pvpartial f)\pT^{-1}\bigr)\dd \pX'}.
\]
Since this holds for every real increment $\dd \pX'$, the coefficient is
$\pvpartial'f'$~\citeat{tong-em}{p.~101}{page=109}.

\subsection{Maxwell covariance}

The field law follows from the potential law.
The identity~\eqref{eq:potential-field}, with real scalar
$S=\scpart{\pvpartial \pA}$, gives
\[
 \pvpartial'\pA'=\pT^*(S+\pF^*)\pT^{\mathsf H}.
\]
By scalar-part cyclicity, $S'=S$.  Since $\pT^*=(\pT^{\mathsf H})^{-1}$,
sigma conjugation of the vector part gives
\eqref{eq:field-transformation}.  Maxwell covariance now follows by cancellation:
\begin{equation*}
 \pvpartial'\pF'=\pT^*(\pvpartial \pF)\pT^{-1}
 =\pT^*\pJ^*\pT^{-1}=\pJ'^*.
\end{equation*}
Thus the transformed potentials and fields satisfy the same
Maxwell equation~\eqref{eq:maxwell}; the scalar wave operator also obeys
$\Box'=\det \pvpartial'=\det \pvpartial=\Box$.

\subsection{Wave phases and spinor covariance}

For real $\pP,\pX$, the metric pairing~\eqref{eq:lorentz-pairing}
is the wave phase.  Adjugation of $\pP'$ and cyclicity give
\begin{equation*}
 \scpart{\pP'^*\pX'}
 =\scpart{\pT^*\pP^*\pX \pT^{\mathsf H}}
 =\scpart{\pP^*\pX}.
\end{equation*}
Consequently $E't'-\bp'\dotprod\br'=Et-\bp\dotprod\br$, and the
plane wave~\eqref{eq:plane-wave} satisfies $f(\pX',\pP')=f(\pX,\pP)$.

A scalar Klein--Gordon field uses $\psi'(\pX')=\psi(\pX)$; the derivative
and potential laws above preserve its wave equation.
For the two complex paravector Dirac entries, a change of coordinates
$\pX'=\pT \pX \pT^{\mathsf H}$ uses
\begin{align*}
 \pvpsi_+'(\pX')&=\pT^*\pvpsi_+(\pX),\\
 \pvpsi_-'(\pX')&=\pT\pvpsi_-(\pX).
\end{align*}
Primed coupled operators use the primed coordinates and potentials.
The first interacting equation~\eqref{eq:dirac-entry-one} transforms by cancellation:
\begin{equation*}
 (\hEq'-\hbpq'\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_-'
 =\pT^*(\hEq-\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_-=m\pvpsi_+'.
\end{equation*}
Equation~\eqref{eq:dirac-entry-two} transforms in the same way with left factor
$\pT$; $q=0$ gives the free case.
For $\pT=\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$, both entries transform
by $\pR$, consistently with the active rotation
\eqref{eq:spin-half-law}; the spatial argument also rotates.
A passive rotation of axes uses the inverse factor $\pT=\pR^{-1}$.
For $\pT=\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$, the two entries instead use
$\pL^{-1}$ and $\pL$, respectively~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eq.~(4.31)}{page=92}.
The factor $\pL$ already contains half the rapidity; no further square root
is needed for either entry.
Their cross-pairing $\scpart{\pvpsi_+^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_-}$ is invariant:
the two transformation factors cancel.  Their summed positive density
\eqref{eq:dirac-norm} is observer-dependent under boosts.
The full Dirac pair is used for boosts before the Pauli low-energy limit is taken.

\subsection{Boosted fields and energy flux}

For $\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)$ with the parameters above,
\eqref{eq:derivative-transformation} becomes
$\pvpartial'=\pL^{-1}\pvpartial \pL^{-1}$.  Its scalar and vector parts are
\begin{align*}
 \partial_{t'}&=\gamma(\partial_t+\beta\bu\dotprod\pdr),\\
 \partial_{\br'}&=\pdr+(\gamma-1)\bu(\bu\dotprod\pdr)
 +\gamma\beta\bu\partial_t.
\end{align*}
These inverse signs express the chain rule.
The real potential $\pA=V+\bA\dotprod\bsig$ and current
$\pJ=\rho+\bJ\dotprod\bsig$ follow the same component formulas as
\eqref{eq:energy-boost}--\eqref{eq:momentum-boost}, with $(E,\bp)$ replaced
by $(V,\bA)$ or $(\rho,\bJ)$.

At corresponding events, \eqref{eq:field-transformation} reduces to
\begin{equation}
 \pF'=\pL \pF \pL^{-1}.
 \label{eq:boost-field-action}
\end{equation}
The similarity preserves the field's zero scalar part, while its
imaginary vector coefficients represent the magnetic field.
As shown algebraically in \eqref{eq:boost-similarity-vector}, a real vector
can acquire an imaginary part under this action.  In contrast, a nonzero
boost congruence would give a scalar part to a nonzero field parallel to $\bu$.
Thus the field uses similarity, whereas events and the real four-potential
use congruence.
Both the field coefficients and their spacetime arguments transform:
\begin{equation}
 \pF'(\pX')=\pL \pF(\pL^{-1}\pX'\pL^{-1})\pL^{-1}.
 \label{eq:boosted-field-arguments}
\end{equation}
Here $\pX=\pL^{-1}\pX'\pL^{-1}$.  To expand \eqref{eq:boost-field-action},
\eqref{eq:boost-similarity-vector} is applied to
$\bE+\ii\bBf$ and separate its real and imaginary parts~\citeat{tong-em}{Eqs.~(5.17)--(5.18)}{page=116}:
\begin{align}
 \bE'&=\bE_{\parallel}+\gamma(\bE_{\perp}+\beta\bu\times\bBf),
 \label{eq:electric-boost}\\
 \bBf'&=\bBf_{\parallel}+\gamma(\bBf_{\perp}-\beta\bu\times\bE).
 \label{eq:magnetic-boost}
\end{align}
Parallel components are unchanged.  These are the usual boosted fields
along any \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit axis}.
The inverse field law is obtained by reversing $\beta$.

Similarity preserves $\pF'^2=\pL \pF^2\pL^{-1}=\pF^2$, since $\pF^2$ is scalar.
Consequently \eqref{eq:field-invariants} gives
\begin{align*}
 \bE'^2-\bBf'^2&=\bE^2-\bBf^2,\\
 \bE'\dotprod\bBf'&=\bE\dotprod\bBf.
\end{align*}
In contrast, $\lVert \pF\rVert^2=\bE^2+\bBf^2$ is twice the
observer-dependent energy density.  For example, if $\bBf=0$ and
$\bu\dotprod\bE=0$, \eqref{eq:electric-boost} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-boost}
give $\lVert \pF'\rVert^2=\gamma^2(1+\beta^2)\bE^2$, while
$\bE'^2-\bBf'^2=\bE^2$.

Notation: the stress map $T(\pX)=\pF \pX \pF^{\mathsf H}/2$ from
\eqref{eq:electromagnetic-stress-map}, with real paravector input $\pX$;
here $T$ denotes this map.  Since $\pX'=\pL \pX \pL$,
\begin{equation*}
 T'(\pX')=\tfrac12\pF'\pX'\pF'^{\mathsf H}=\pL T(\pX)\pL.
\end{equation*}
Here $T'$ is the map formed from $\pF'$.  To measure the primed
energy density and Poynting vector, $\pX'=1$ is used, hence $\pX=\pL^{-2}$:
\begin{equation*}
 T'(1)=\pL T(\pL^{-2})\pL=\tfrac12\pF'\pF'^{\mathsf H}.
\end{equation*}
The full map transforms covariantly; its value at $1$ depends on the
observer's time direction.

Finally, the following notation is retained: the real four-velocity $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$, an
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$,
and real momentum $\pP=m\pU$, with real charge $q$ and constant mass $m>0$.
With $\pU'=\pL \pU \pL$ and $\pP'=\pL \pP \pL$, the force
law~\eqref{eq:lorentz} transforms as
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{\dd \pP'}{\dd s}
 =\pL\frac q2(\pF \pU+\pU \pF^{\mathsf H})\pL
 =\frac q2(\pF'\pU'+\pU'\pF'^{\mathsf H}).
\end{equation*}
Thus the electric and magnetic components above reproduce the same
real four-force in the moving frame; the Hermitian extraction is preserved.


\section{Position-dependent paravector bases}
\label{sec:variable-bases}

Section~\ref{sec:generalized} starts with an inertial coordinate map
$\pX=a(t,\br)+\bX(t,\br)\dotprod\bsig$. Its exact differential gives
\[
 \dd\pX=\dd a+\dd\bX\dotprod\bsig,\qquad
 \dd s^2=\dd a^2-\dd\bX^2.
\]
This is flat spacetime in generalized coordinates. In the block notation
used below,
\[
 \begin{Bmatrix}\dd a\\\dd\bX\end{Bmatrix}
 =\bM^{\mathsf T}\begin{Bmatrix}\dd t\\\dd\br\end{Bmatrix};
\]
so $\bM^{\mathsf T}$ is the Jacobian of the same functions $a,\bX$.

Here we assign a local displacement directly:
\[
 \dd\pX=\dd t\,\pSigmaTPrime+\dd\br\dotprod\bsigRPrime,
 \qquad \dd s^2=\det(\dd\pX).
\]
A smooth real invertible $\bM(t,\br)$ specifies the basis elements,
without requiring $\bM^{\mathsf T}$ to be a coordinate Jacobian.
The notation $\dd\pX$ now denotes that local assignment; it need not
be an exact differential, even locally. For fixed coordinate increments
$\dd t,\dd\br$, the basis specifies their metric.
This is the usual local-frame (tetrad) construction, expressed
in paravector form~\citeat{carroll-gr}{Eq.~(3.119)}{page=96}.
The product, conjugations, and determinant retain their earlier definitions.

Generalized coordinates are a flat special case. A general basis can
describe curvature, although position dependence or failure of exactness
alone does not establish it: a varying Lorentz basis can still give a flat
metric. We use the metric's unique torsion-free, metric-compatible
parallel transport to determine free fall and curvature. This is the
standard geometric input; Einstein's field equation is additional
physical input selecting metrics from matter and boundary conditions.

\subsection{The basis and its interval}
\label{sec:matrix-basis}

Let $\bM(t,\br)$ be a smooth real invertible $4\times4$ matrix, partitioned as
\[
 \bM=\begin{Bmatrix}M_{tt}&\bM_{tr}\\\bM_{rt}&\bM_{rr}\end{Bmatrix}.
\]
Here $M_{tt}$ is a scalar, $\bM_{tr}$ a $1\times3$ row,
$\bM_{rt}$ a $3\times1$ column, and $\bM_{rr}$ a $3\times3$ matrix.
The blocks are independent: no symmetry of $\bM$ is assumed, and
$\bM_{rt}=\bM_{tr}^{\mathsf T}$ is not required.
Let $\bI$ be the $3\times3$ identity and $\vstyle{0}$ the zero
three-vector. The local basis is
\begin{equation}
 \begin{Bmatrix}\pSigmaTPrime\\\bsigRPrime\end{Bmatrix}
 =\bM\begin{Bmatrix}1\\\bsig\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:spacetime-matrix-basis}
\end{equation}
Here $\pSigmaTPrime$ is one real paravector and $\bsigRPrime$ is a
triple of real paravectors, all Hermitian. Their scalar and vector parts are
\[
 \pSigmaTPrime=M_{tt}+\bM_{tr}^{\mathsf T}\dotprod\bsig,
 \qquad \bsigRPrime=\bM_{rt}+\bM_{rr}\bsig.
\]
In the second expression, $\bM_{rt}$ collects the three scalar parts
and each row of $\bM_{rr}$ gives the corresponding vector part.
The labels $t,r$ identify basis entries, not algebraic scalar and vector grades.
Sigma conjugation acts componentwise and
leaves the real entries of $\bM$ unchanged:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \begin{Bmatrix}\pSigmaTPrime\\\bsigRPrime\end{Bmatrix}^{*}
 &=\bM\begin{Bmatrix}1\\\bsig\end{Bmatrix}^{*}
  =\bM\begin{Bmatrix}1\\-\bsig\end{Bmatrix}\\
 &=\bM\begin{Bmatrix}1&\vstyle{0}^{\mathsf T}\\\vstyle{0}&-\bI\end{Bmatrix}
       \begin{Bmatrix}1\\\bsig\end{Bmatrix}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:spacetime-basis-conjugation}
\end{equation}
Write the local displacement as
$\dd\pX=\dd t\,\pSigmaTPrime+\dd\br\dotprod\bsigRPrime$.
Its interval is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\dd s^2
 =\det(\dd t\,\pSigmaTPrime+\dd\br\dotprod\bsigRPrime).}
 \label{eq:spacetime-basis-interval}
\end{equation}
Its scalar--vector coefficient column in the fixed basis is
$\bM^{\mathsf T}\{\dd t,\dd\br\}^{\mathsf T}$, as derived below.
Taking the scalar part squared minus the vector part squared gives
the metric matrix
\[
 \bg=\bM\begin{Bmatrix}1&\vstyle{0}^{\mathsf T}\\\vstyle{0}&-\bI\end{Bmatrix}\bM^{\mathsf T}
 =\begin{Bmatrix}
  g_{tt}&\bg_{tr}\\
  \bg_{tr}^{\mathsf T}&\bg_{rr}
 \end{Bmatrix},
\]
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \dd s^2
 &=\begin{Bmatrix}\dd t\\\dd\br\end{Bmatrix}^{\mathsf T}
   \bg\begin{Bmatrix}\dd t\\\dd\br\end{Bmatrix}\\
 &=g_{tt}\dd t^2+2\dd t\,\bg_{tr}\dd\br
   +\dd\br^{\mathsf T}\bg_{rr}\dd\br.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:metric-block-determinant}
\end{equation}
Here $g_{tt}$ is a real scalar, $\bg_{tr}$ a real $1\times3$ row,
and $\bg_{rr}$ a real symmetric $3\times3$ matrix.
Explicitly,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 g_{tt}&=M_{tt}^2-\bM_{tr}\bM_{tr}^{\mathsf T},\\
 \bg_{tr}&=M_{tt}\bM_{rt}^{\mathsf T}-\bM_{tr}\bM_{rr}^{\mathsf T},\\
 \bg_{rr}&=\bM_{rt}\bM_{rt}^{\mathsf T}-\bM_{rr}\bM_{rr}^{\mathsf T}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Invertibility of $\bM$ ensures signature $(+,-,-,-)$ and
$\det\bg=-(\det\bM)^2<0$. Every smooth Lorentzian metric has such a
representation locally~\citeat{carroll-gr}{Eq.~(3.119)}{page=96}.
Along a time-like path with $\dd t/\dd s>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd s}{\dd t}
 =\sqrt{g_{tt}+2\bg_{tr}\dot\br
                    +\dot\br^{\mathsf T}\bg_{rr}\dot\br}.
 \label{eq:metric-block-clock}
\end{equation}

The earlier similarity basis $\bsigRPrime=\pT\bsig\pT^{-1}$ preserves
\[
 \det(\dd t+\dd\br\dotprod\bsigRPrime)=\dd t^2-\dd\br^2,
\]
so it retains the flat interval. The general real matrix $\bM$ allows
the metric blocks above to vary independently of that similarity law.

\subsection{Algebra and differentiation}

For the real local displacement
$\dd\pX=\dd t\,\pSigmaTPrime+\dd\br\dotprod\bsigRPrime$,
the coefficient row multiplies the basis column.
Substituting \eqref{eq:spacetime-matrix-basis} gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \dd\pX
 &=\begin{Bmatrix}\dd t\\\dd\br\end{Bmatrix}^{\mathsf T}
   \begin{Bmatrix}\pSigmaTPrime\\\bsigRPrime\end{Bmatrix}\\
 &=\begin{Bmatrix}\dd t\\\dd\br\end{Bmatrix}^{\mathsf T}
   \bM\begin{Bmatrix}1\\\bsig\end{Bmatrix}
 =\left(\bM^{\mathsf T}\begin{Bmatrix}\dd t\\\dd\br\end{Bmatrix}\right)^{\mathsf T}
   \begin{Bmatrix}1\\\bsig\end{Bmatrix}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Thus expansion in the fixed basis uses $\bM^{\mathsf T}$ on the
coefficient column. The transpose introduces no complex conjugation.
Transposition swaps and transposes the off-diagonal blocks; it does not equate them:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{Bmatrix}\scpart{\dd\pX}\\\vecpart{\dd\pX}\end{Bmatrix}
 =\bM^{\mathsf T}\begin{Bmatrix}\dd t\\\dd\br\end{Bmatrix}
 =\begin{Bmatrix}M_{tt}&\bM_{rt}^{\mathsf T}\\
                  \bM_{tr}^{\mathsf T}&\bM_{rr}^{\mathsf T}\end{Bmatrix}
   \begin{Bmatrix}\dd t\\\dd\br\end{Bmatrix}
 .
 \label{eq:spacetime-basis-coefficients}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the paravector is
\[
 \dd\pX=\left(M_{tt}\dd t+\bM_{rt}\dotprod\dd\br\right)
      +\left(\dd t\,\bM_{tr}^{\mathsf T}
                    +\bM_{rr}^{\mathsf T}\dd\br\right)\dotprod\bsig.
\]
The first term is $\scpart{\dd\pX}$; the coefficient of $\bsig$ is
$\vecpart{\dd\pX}$. In general these are not $\dd t$ and $\dd\br$.
To multiply paravectors, apply the existing rule~\eqref{eq:para-product}
to their scalar--vector parts, then apply $\bM^{-\mathsf T}$ to the
resulting coefficient column to return to the local basis.
The product may have complex coefficients even when both factors are real.

\paragraph{Differentiation.}
Holding the coordinate increments $\dd t,\dd\br$ fixed while
varying the base point gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \partial_t(\dd\pX)
 &=\left((\partial_t M_{tt})\dd t
            +(\partial_t\bM_{rt})\dotprod\dd\br\right)\\
 &\quad+\left(\dd t\,\partial_t\bM_{tr}^{\mathsf T}
            +(\partial_t\bM_{rr}^{\mathsf T})\dd\br\right)\dotprod\bsig.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Refactoring into the local basis gives
\begin{equation}
 \partial_t(\dd\pX)
 =\begin{Bmatrix}\pSigmaTPrime&\bsig_r^{\prime\mathsf T}\end{Bmatrix}
 \bM^{-\mathsf T}(\partial_t\bM^{\mathsf T})
 \begin{Bmatrix}\dd t\\\dd\br\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:spacetime-basis-field-derivative}
\end{equation}
The same rule holds for any coordinate or fixed-direction spatial
derivative. If the increments also vary, their derivatives contribute
the usual product-rule terms. Refactoring an ordinary derivative
changes its representation; it does not supply the metric's
torsion-free parallel transport.

\subsection{Expansion, free fall, and curvature}

For a smooth positive scale factor $\lambda(t)$, choose
\begin{equation}
 \bM=\begin{Bmatrix}1&\vstyle{0}^{\mathsf T}\\
                   \vstyle{0}&\lambda\bI\end{Bmatrix},\qquad
 \pSigmaTPrime=1,\qquad \bsigRPrime=\lambda\bsig.
 \label{eq:matrix-basis}
\end{equation}
The metric blocks are
\begin{equation}
 g_{tt}=1,\qquad \bg_{tr}=\vstyle{0}^{\mathsf T},\qquad
 \bg_{rr}=-\lambda(t)^2\bI.
 \label{eq:expansion-metric-blocks}
\end{equation}
For real vector increments $\dd\bX,\dd\bY$, the algebra gives
\[
 (\dd\bX\dotprod\bsigRPrime)(\dd\bY\dotprod\bsigRPrime)
 =\lambda^2\dd\bX\dotprod\dd\bY
  +\ii \lambda(\dd\bX\times\dd\bY)\dotprod\bsigRPrime.
\]
For $\dd\pX=\dd t\,\pSigmaTPrime+\dd\br\dotprod\bsigRPrime$,
the interval is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\dd s^2=\det(\dd\pX)=\dd t^2-\lambda(t)^2\dd\br^2,}
 \label{eq:matrix-basis-interval}
\end{equation}
the spatially flat Robertson--Walker metric
\citeat{carroll-gr}{Eqs.~(8.7)--(8.9)}{page=226}.
Its constant-time spatial slices are flat; its spacetime need not be.

\Needspace{14\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Free fall in an expanding universe.}
Observers at fixed $\br$ are comoving: their clocks measure $t$, and
the scale factor $\lambda(t)$ converts coordinate separations into physical distances.
For this metric, the quadratic geodesic action reduces to
\[
 \frac12\int\left(
    \left(\frac{\dd t}{\dd s}\right)^2
    -\lambda(t)^2\left(\frac{\dd\br}{\dd s}\right)^2\right)\dd s.
\]
Varying $t$ and $\br$ with fixed endpoints gives the affinely
parameterized geodesic equations
\citeat{carroll-gr}{pp.~68--70}{page=75}
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd^2t}{\dd s^2}
 =-\lambda\dot \lambda\left(\frac{\dd\br}{\dd s}\right)^2,
 \qquad
 \frac{\dd}{\dd s}\left(\lambda^2\frac{\dd\br}{\dd s}\right)
 =\vstyle{0}.
 \label{eq:matrix-basis-geodesics}
\end{equation}
Here $\dot \lambda=\dd \lambda/\dd t$. For a massive particle, choose $s$ to
be proper time. Its velocity relative to a comoving
observer is $\bv=\lambda\,\dd\br/\dd t$, so
$\gamma=\dd t/\dd s=(1-\bv^2)^{-1/2}$.
For constant mass $m>0$, the earlier momentum law $\pP=m\pU$ gives
\[
 E=m\gamma,\qquad
 \bp=m\lambda\frac{\dd\br}{\dd s}=m\gamma\bv.
\]
The spatial geodesic equation therefore has the direct physical meaning
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\frac{\dd}{\dd t}(\lambda\bp)=\vstyle{0},\qquad
        \frac{\dd\bp}{\dd t}=-\frac{\dot \lambda}{\lambda}\bp.}
 \label{eq:expansion-momentum-redshift}
\end{equation}
The momentum measured by comoving observers decreases as $1/\lambda$ during
expansion. This is the standard cosmological redshift of particle
momentum~\citeat{carroll-gr}{Eq.~(8.64)}{page=236}.
At low speeds, $\bp\simeq m\bv$, so the velocity relative to those
observers also decreases as $1/\lambda$. A particle initially at fixed $\br$
remains there.

\Needspace{12\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Parallel transport and redshift.}
Parallel transport carries a four-vector along a chosen path using
the metric's torsion-free, metric-compatible connection. Write the real
paravector in terms of its measured scalar and vector parts:
\[
 \pY=b\pSigmaTPrime+\frac{\bY}{\lambda}\dotprod\bsigRPrime
     =b+\bY\dotprod\bsig,\qquad b\in\R,\quad\bY\in\R^3.
\]
The factor $1/\lambda$ converts the measured vector $\bY$ into coefficients
of the scaled basis. The torsion-free transport law follows from
the quadratic velocity terms in \eqref{eq:matrix-basis-geodesics}:
replace one tangent by the carried vector and average the two placements.
Converting to the measured components above and using $t$ as the path
parameter, with $\bv=\lambda\,\dd\br/\dd t$, gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd b}{\dd t}=-\frac{\dot \lambda}{\lambda}\bv\dotprod\bY,
 \qquad
 \frac{\dd\bY}{\dd t}=-\frac{\dot \lambda}{\lambda}b\bv.
 \label{eq:matrix-basis-parallel-transport}
\end{equation}
These equations preserve $\det\pY=b^2-\bY^2$.
Choosing $\pY=\pU$, with $b=\gamma$ and $\bY=\gamma\bv$,
recovers free fall: a geodesic parallel-transports its own tangent.

For a freely propagating photon, take $\pY=\pP=E+\bp\dotprod\bsig$,
with $\bp=E\bv$ and $\bv^2=1$. The same transport rule gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\frac{\dd E}{\dd t}=-\frac{\dot \lambda}{\lambda}E,
        \qquad \lambda E=\text{constant}.}
 \label{eq:expansion-photon-redshift}
\end{equation}
Photon energy and frequency scale as $1/\lambda$: expansion produces the familiar
cosmological redshift~\citeat{carroll-gr}{Eq.~(8.66)}{page=236}.
Both results follow from the assigned metric for any smooth positive
$\lambda(t)$; determining $\lambda(t)$ from matter requires Einstein's field equation.

\Needspace{12\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Tidal acceleration and curvature.}
Two nearby comoving observers are both in free fall, with proper time
$t$ and fixed coordinate separation $\Delta\br$. Their physical
separation $\ell$ therefore obeys
\[
 \ell=\lambda\lVert\Delta\br\rVert,\qquad
 \dot\ell=\frac{\dot \lambda}{\lambda}\ell,\qquad
 \boxed{\ddot\ell=\frac{\ddot \lambda}{\lambda}\ell.}
\]
The middle relation is Hubble's law: recession speed is proportional
to distance. The last relation is geodesic deviation, the tidal
acceleration between neighboring freely falling observers
\citeat{carroll-gr}{Eq.~(3.113)}{page=94}.
Each observer has zero proper acceleration, yet their separation can
accelerate. The ratio $\ddot \lambda/\lambda$ measures this tidal effect.

Transport around infinitesimal loops gives curvature magnitudes
$|\ddot \lambda/\lambda|$ in each of the three orthonormal time--space planes
and $(\dot \lambda/\lambda)^2$ in each of the three spatial planes.
These are spacetime transport effects; each fixed-time spatial slice
has zero intrinsic curvature.
Contracting the Riemann tensor with itself counts each plane four
times. The resulting coordinate-independent \emph{Kretschmann scalar} is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{12\left(\left(\frac{\ddot \lambda}{\lambda}\right)^2
         +\left(\frac{\dot \lambda}{\lambda}\right)^4\right).}
 \label{eq:matrix-basis-curvature}
\end{equation}
A nonzero value establishes spacetime curvature that no coordinate
change can remove. In this spatially flat family it is nonzero wherever
$\dot \lambda$ or $\ddot \lambda$ is nonzero; the spacetime is flat on a time
interval precisely when $\lambda$ is constant there.
In particular, $\ddot \lambda=0$ alone does not imply flat spacetime.

\paragraph{The role of the assigned displacement.}
The curved metric above uses
$\dd\pX=\dd t+\lambda\dd\br\dotprod\bsig$.
An ordinary rescaling of inertial spatial coordinates instead gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \dd\left(t+\lambda(t)\br\dotprod\bsig\right)
 &=\dd t+\left(\lambda\dd\br+\dot \lambda\br\dd t\right)\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \det\!\left(\dd\left(t+\lambda(t)\br\dotprod\bsig\right)\right)
 &=\dd t^2-\left(\lambda\dd\br+\dot \lambda\br\dd t\right)^2.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Since $\lambda>0$, this is an invertible coordinate change of flat Minkowski
spacetime. The product-rule term $\dot \lambda\br\dd t$ distinguishes this
flat interval from \eqref{eq:matrix-basis-interval}: the two
displacements assign different metrics to the same $\dd t,\dd\br$.

\subsection{Newtonian motion and black-hole metrics}
\label{sec:gravity-metrics}

\paragraph{A common basis.}
On an open coordinate region in $\R\times\R^3$, let
$f(t,\br)\in\R$ be a smooth dimensionless scalar field and
$\bu(t,\br)\in\R^3$ a smooth unit vector field, $\bu^2=1$.
Writing $\bu$ for $\bu(t,\br)$, choose
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \pSigmaTPrime&=1-f(t,\br)+f(t,\br)\bu\dotprod\bsig,\\
 \bsigRPrime&=-f(t,\br)\bu
              +\left(\bI+f(t,\br)\bu\bu^{\mathsf T}\right)\bsig.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:kerr-schild-basis}
\end{equation}
The displacement separates into scalar and vector parts:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \dd\pX
 &=\left(\dd t-f(t,\br)(\dd t+\bu\dotprod\dd\br)\right)\\
 &\quad+\left(\dd\br
       +f(t,\br)(\dd t+\bu\dotprod\dd\br)\bu\right)\dotprod\bsig.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Subtracting the squared vector part from the squared scalar part,
the terms quadratic in $f(t,\br)$ cancel because $\bu^2=1$.
The resulting metric blocks are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 g_{tt}=1-2f(t,\br),\qquad
 \bg_{tr}=-2f(t,\br)\bu^{\mathsf T},\\
 \bg_{rr}=-\bI-2f(t,\br)\bu\bu^{\mathsf T}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:kerr-schild-blocks}
\end{equation}
Combining the three blocks gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\dd s^2=\dd t^2-\dd\br^2
       -2f(t,\br)(\dd t+\bu\dotprod\dd\br)^2.}
 \label{eq:kerr-schild-interval}
\end{equation}
This is the Kerr--Schild form~\citeat{bini-kerr-schild}{Eq.~(1)}{page=1}.
The basis is invertible for every finite $f(t,\br)$: its inverse change
is obtained by replacing $f(t,\br)$ with $-f(t,\br)$, and
$\det\bM=1$, so $\det\bg=-1$.
The algebra specifies the metric for any such fields; Einstein's equation
selects gravitational solutions.

\paragraph{Newtonian limit.}
For stationary $f(t,\br),\bu$, substituting
\eqref{eq:kerr-schild-blocks} into \eqref{eq:metric-block-clock} gives
\[
 \frac{\dd s}{\dd t}
 =\sqrt{1-\dot\br^2-2f(t,\br)(1+\bu\dotprod\dot\br)^2}.
\]
For $|f(t,\br)|\ll1$ and $\lVert\dot\br\rVert\ll1$, retain terms
linear in $f(t,\br)$ and quadratic in velocity, dropping their
products. The free-particle action per unit mass becomes
\[
 -\int\dd s\simeq\int\left(-1+\frac12\dot\br^2+f(t,\br)\right)\dd t.
\]
The Euler--Lagrange equation gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\ddot\br\simeq-\frac12\pdr g_{tt}=\pdr f(t,\br).}
 \label{eq:gravity-newtonian-limit}
\end{equation}
Thus $-f(t,\br)$ is the Newtonian gravitational potential
\citeat{carroll-gr}{Eqs.~(4.20)--(4.22)}{page=113}.

\paragraph{Schwarzschild.}
For stationary spherical symmetry, take
$r=\lVert\br\rVert>0$ and $\bu=\br/r$, with $f(t,\br)$ depending
on position only through $r$.
For this radial ansatz, the vacuum Einstein equation with zero
cosmological constant gives the angular condition
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd}{\dd r}(r\,f(t,\br))=0
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad f(t,\br)=\frac{Gm}{r}.
 \label{eq:schwarzschild-profile}
\end{equation}
The remaining radial condition follows by differentiating this
equation. Matching the Newtonian potential fixes the integration constant
to $Gm$~\citeat{carroll-gr}{Eqs.~(7.23)--(7.29)}{page=177}.
Here $G$ is Newton's gravitational constant and $m>0$ is the source
mass. Substitution into \eqref{eq:kerr-schild-blocks} gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 g_{tt}=1-\frac{2Gm}{r},\qquad
 \bg_{tr}=-\frac{2Gm}{r^2}\br^{\mathsf T},\\
 \bg_{rr}=-\bI-\frac{2Gm}{r^3}\br\br^{\mathsf T}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:schwarzschild-blocks}
\end{equation}
Since $\bu\dotprod\dd\br=\dd r$, these blocks give
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\dd s^2=\dd t^2-\dd\br^2-\frac{2Gm}{r}(\dd t+\dd r)^2.}
 \label{eq:schwarzschild-kerr-schild}
\end{equation}
This is the Schwarzschild metric in coordinates regular at the future
horizon $r=2Gm$~\citeat{heinicke-hehl}{Table~3}{page=23}.
The weak-field acceleration is
\[
 \ddot\br\simeq-\frac{Gm}{r^3}\br.
\]

\Needspace{24\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Kerr.}
Let the constant real vector $\vstyle{\omega}$ be the outer horizon's
angular velocity relative to a nonrotating observer at infinity. For
$2Gm\lVert\vstyle{\omega}\rVert\le1$, define the positive scalar
\[
 \rho=\frac{4(Gm)^2}{1+4(Gm)^2\vstyle{\omega}^2}.
\]
The angular momentum per unit mass is $\rho\vstyle{\omega}$;
$\rho$ has dimensions of length squared. This scaling follows from
the Kerr horizon angular-velocity relation
\citeat{heinicke-hehl}{Eq.~(145)}{page=57}.
Use the positive ellipsoidal-radius branch $\epsilon>0$, on the
coordinate region where it is smooth. It is determined by $\br$ and
the spin, and generally differs from $\lVert\br\rVert$:
\begin{equation}
 \epsilon^4-\epsilon^2(\br^2-\rho^2\vstyle{\omega}^2)
 -\rho^2(\vstyle{\omega}\dotprod\br)^2=0.
 \label{eq:kerr-radius}
\end{equation}
The standard Kerr black-hole solution is represented by
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \bu&=\frac{\epsilon\br-\rho\vstyle{\omega}\times\br
       +\rho^2\vstyle{\omega}(\vstyle{\omega}\dotprod\br)/\epsilon}
      {\epsilon^2+\rho^2\vstyle{\omega}^2},\\
 f(t,\br)&=\frac{Gm\,\epsilon^3}
       {\epsilon^4+\rho^2(\vstyle{\omega}\dotprod\br)^2}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:kerr-fields}
\end{equation}
The radius equation verifies the unit direction:
\[
 \bu^2=
 \frac{\br^2+\rho^2(\vstyle{\omega}\dotprod\br)^2/\epsilon^2}
      {\epsilon^2+\rho^2\vstyle{\omega}^2}=1.
\]
Substituting into \eqref{eq:kerr-schild-blocks} gives the explicit metric blocks
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 g_{tt}&=1-\frac{2Gm\,\epsilon^3}
                   {\epsilon^4+\rho^2(\vstyle{\omega}\dotprod\br)^2},\\
 \bg_{tr}&=-\frac{2Gm\,\epsilon^3}
                   {\epsilon^4+\rho^2(\vstyle{\omega}\dotprod\br)^2}
                   \bu^{\mathsf T},\\
 \bg_{rr}&=-\bI-\frac{2Gm\,\epsilon^3}
                   {\epsilon^4+\rho^2(\vstyle{\omega}\dotprod\br)^2}
                   \bu\bu^{\mathsf T},
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:kerr-metric-blocks}
\end{equation}
where $\bu$ is given explicitly in \eqref{eq:kerr-fields}.
Thus the scalar--vector determinant gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \dd s^2=\det(\dd\pX)
 &=g_{tt}\dd t^2+2\dd t\,\bg_{tr}\dd\br
       +\dd\br^{\mathsf T}\bg_{rr}\dd\br\\
 &=\dd t^2-\dd\br^2
  -\frac{2Gm\,\epsilon^3}
        {\epsilon^4+\rho^2(\vstyle{\omega}\dotprod\br)^2}
       (\dd t+\bu\dotprod\dd\br)^2.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:kerr-interval}
\end{equation}
This is the Cartesian Kerr--Schild vacuum solution with signature
$(+,-,-,-)$~\citeat{bini-kerr-schild}{Sec.~1, Eqs.~(5)--(6)}{page=2}.
Setting $\vstyle{\omega}=\vstyle{0}$ gives
$\epsilon=\lVert\br\rVert$ and recovers Schwarzschild.
The outer horizon is the larger root of
$\epsilon^2-2Gm\epsilon+\rho^2\vstyle{\omega}^2=0$:
\[
 \epsilon=\frac{\rho}{2Gm}
 =\frac{2Gm}{1+4(Gm)^2\vstyle{\omega}^2}.
\]
The metric remains regular there, with $\det\bg=-1$.
At this radius, the tangent displacement
$\dd\br=(\vstyle{\omega}\times\br)\dd t$ gives $\dd s^2=0$,
verifying that $\vstyle{\omega}$ is the horizon's angular velocity.
The determinant construction therefore reproduces the Schwarzschild
and Kerr metrics and their Newtonian limit. The Kerr fields are supplied
from the known vacuum solution; a general paravector formulation of
Einstein's equation relating curvature to matter remains to be developed.


\clearpage
\appendix

\section{Pauli-matrix representation}
\label{sec:matrix}

The paravector formulas are verified in matrix form in this appendix.  The decomposition $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ is used, with $c\in\C$ and $\bZ\in\C^3$.
The matrix map is
$\operatorname{mat}:\C\oplus\C^3\to\C^{2\times2}$.
The notation $\bM=\operatorname{mat}(\pZ)\in\C^{2\times2}$ is used.
Real paravectors map to Hermitian matrices, not necessarily real matrices.
The identity matrices are $\bI_2$ and $\bI_4$ in their stated dimensions.

\subsection{Sigmas and products}

The sigmas are represented by the Pauli matrices~\citeat{lounesto-intro}{p.~11}{page=24}
\[
 \operatorname{mat}(\pvsigma_1)=\begin{Bmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{Bmatrix},\quad
 \operatorname{mat}(\pvsigma_2)=\begin{Bmatrix}0&-\ii\\\ii&0\end{Bmatrix},\quad
 \operatorname{mat}(\pvsigma_3)=\begin{Bmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{Bmatrix}.
\]
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{mat}(c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig)=
 \begin{Bmatrix}c+Z_3&Z_1-\ii Z_2\\Z_1+\ii Z_2&c-Z_3\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:matrix}
\end{equation}
The sigma product rule is satisfied by these matrices, which are linearly independent over
$\C$ together with $\bI_2$.  Thus every complex $2\times2$ matrix corresponds to exactly one paravector.
The map preserves addition, sends $1$ to $\bI_2$, and satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 \operatorname{mat}(\pZ'\pZ)=\operatorname{mat}(\pZ')\operatorname{mat}(\pZ).
\end{equation*}
Here $\pZ'$ is any second complex paravector.  Thus algebra multiplication
is ordinary matrix multiplication.

\subsection{Conjugations and product order}

For $\bM,\bN\in\C^{2\times2}$, transpose $^{\mathsf T}$ exchanges row
and column indices; entrywise complex conjugation $^{\mathsf C}$ sends each
$a+\ii b$ to $a-\ii b$ for real $a,b$.  Their composition is
$\bM^{\mathsf H}=(\bM^{\mathsf C})^{\mathsf T}$.
Introduce the fixed matrix $\bm S\in\R^{2\times2}$:
\[
 \bm S=\begin{Bmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{Bmatrix},\qquad
 \bm S^{-1}=-\bm S.
\]
The paravector sigma conjugation has the matrix image
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{mat}(\pZ^*)=\bm S^{-1}\bM^{\mathsf C}\bm S=: \bM^*.
 \label{eq:matrix-sigma-conjugation}
\end{equation}
Substituting the basis in \eqref{eq:matrix-sigma-conjugation} gives
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c c c c c}
\toprule
Algebra basis & $1$ & $\ii$ & $\pvsigma_k$ & $\ii\pvsigma_k$\\
\midrule
Hermitian image & $\bI_2$ & $-\ii\bI_2$
 & $\operatorname{mat}(\pvsigma_k)$ & $-\ii\operatorname{mat}(\pvsigma_k)$\\
Sigma-conjugate image & $\bI_2$ & $-\ii\bI_2$
 & $-\operatorname{mat}(\pvsigma_k)$ & $\ii\operatorname{mat}(\pvsigma_k)$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Here $k=1,2,3$.  These are exactly the operations of
Section~\ref{sec:conjugate-operators}, so
$\operatorname{mat}(\pZ^{\mathsf H})=\bM^{\mathsf H}$.
Their product-order rules are
\begin{align*}
