 (\bM\bN)^{\mathsf T}&=\bN^{\mathsf T}\bM^{\mathsf T},\\
 (\bM\bN)^{\mathsf C}&=\bM^{\mathsf C}\bN^{\mathsf C},\\
 (\bM\bN)^{\mathsf H}&=\bN^{\mathsf H}\bM^{\mathsf H}.
\end{align*}
For sigma conjugation the middle factors cancel:
\[
 (\bM\bN)^*=\bm S^{-1}\bM^{\mathsf C}\bm S
                   \bm S^{-1}\bN^{\mathsf C}\bm S.
\]
Thus sigma conjugation preserves product order:
\begin{equation*}
 (\bM\bN)^*=\bM^*\bN^*.
\end{equation*}
This verifies \eqref{eq:sigma-conjugate-product} in matrices.
Hermitian and sigma conjugation commute, and applying either twice
restores the original matrix.
The matrix-only operations can be recovered as
\begin{align*}
 \bM^{\mathsf C}&=\bm S\bM^*\bm S^{-1},\\
 \bM^{\mathsf T}&=\bm S(\bM^*)^{\mathsf H}\bm S^{-1}.
\end{align*}

\subsection{Scalar part, determinant, and norm}

For $\bM=(m_{jk})\in\C^{2\times2}$, with $m_{jk}\in\C$, its trace is $m_{11}+m_{22}$ and its determinant
is $m_{11}m_{22}-m_{12}m_{21}$.  Substituting \eqref{eq:matrix} gives
\begin{align}
 \scpart {\pZ}&=\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}\bM=c,
 \label{eq:matrix-scalar-part}\\
 (\vecpart {\pZ})_k&=\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}
                    (\operatorname{mat}(\pvsigma_k)\bM)=Z_k,\notag\\
 \det \pZ&=\det\bM=c^2-\bZ^2.
 \label{eq:matrix-determinant}
\end{align}
By \eqref{eq:matrix-scalar-part}, matrix-trace cyclicity is precisely the scalar-part cyclicity used
in the main text.  For $\bM'=\operatorname{mat}(\pZ')$,
\[
 \scpart{\pZ'\pZ}=\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}(\bM'\bM)
             =\scpart{\pZ \pZ'}.
\]
The remaining extraction and norm formulas are
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{mat}(\vecpart {\pZ}\dotprod\bsig)&=\bM-\scpart {\pZ}\,\bI_2,\notag\\
 \operatorname{mat}(\repart {\pZ})&=\tfrac12(\bM+\bM^{\mathsf H}),
 \label{eq:matrix-real-part}\\
 \operatorname{mat}(\impart {\pZ})&=(\bM-\bM^{\mathsf H})/(2\ii),
 \label{eq:matrix-imaginary-part}\\
 \lVert \pZ\rVert^2&=\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}(\bM^{\mathsf H}\bM)
                  =\lVert c\rVert^2+\lVert\bZ\rVert^2.
 \label{eq:matrix-norm}
\end{align}
Equations~\eqref{eq:matrix-real-part}--\eqref{eq:matrix-imaginary-part}
extract Hermitian matrices, so a real paravector has a Hermitian image.
The squared norm in \eqref{eq:matrix-norm} is half the sum of the squared
magnitudes of the matrix entries.  The determinant in
\eqref{eq:matrix-determinant} may be complex.

The matrix adjugate is
\begin{equation*}
 \adj\bM=(\operatorname{tr}\bM)\bI_2-\bM
        =\operatorname{mat}(\adj {\pZ})=(\bM^*)^{\mathsf H}.
\end{equation*}
It satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 \operatorname{mat}(\adj {\pZ}\,\pZ)=\det(\bM)\bI_2.
\end{equation*}
Consequently $\bM^{-1}=\adj\bM/\det\bM$ when the determinant is
nonzero.  The determinant product and adjugate order rules are the same
in both representations.  The quadratic identity~\eqref{eq:minimal} is
precisely Cayley--Hamilton for a $2\times2$ matrix.

\subsection{Boost and rotation matrices}
\label{sec:matrix-boosts}

Notation: the real unit axis $\bu=\{u_1,u_2,u_3\}$ and real rapidity
$\theta$.  The matrix image of \eqref{eq:boost-exponential} is
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{mat}(\pL)=\cosh(\theta/2)\bI_2
 -\sinh(\theta/2)
 \begin{Bmatrix}u_3&u_1-\ii u_2\\u_1+\ii u_2&-u_3\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:matrix-boost}
\end{equation}
The displayed axis matrix is Hermitian, has square $\bI_2$, and has
eigenvalues $\pm1$.  Thus \eqref{eq:matrix-boost} has eigenvalues
$e^{\mp\theta/2}$, determinant one, and squared norm
$\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{mat}(\pL)^{\mathsf H}
\operatorname{mat}(\pL))=\cosh\theta$.
For a real rotation angle $\theta$, replacing the hyperbolic coefficients
by $\cos(\theta/2)$ and $\ii\sin(\theta/2)$ gives
$\operatorname{mat}(\pR)$, which is unitary.

Multiplication preserves the distinct spacetime and field laws:
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{mat}(\pX')&=\operatorname{mat}(\pL)
                \operatorname{mat}(\pX)\operatorname{mat}(\pL),\\
 \operatorname{mat}(\pF')&=\operatorname{mat}(\pL)
                \operatorname{mat}(\pF)\operatorname{mat}(\pL)^{-1}.
\end{align*}
Here $\pX=t+\br\dotprod\bsig$ is real and
$\pF=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\dotprod\bsig$ is complex, with
$t\in\R$ and $\br,\bE,\bBf\in\R^3$.
Extracting scalar and vector coefficients by \eqref{eq:matrix-scalar-part}
and the preceding component rule gives
\eqref{eq:lorentz-time}--\eqref{eq:lorentz-position} and
\eqref{eq:electric-boost}--\eqref{eq:magnetic-boost} for the same arbitrary
axis.  The first matrix stays Hermitian; the second stays traceless.

\subsection{Matrix amplitudes and column spinors}
\label{sec:matrix-spinors}

The matrix image of $\pvpsi\in\C\oplus\C^3$ is denoted by
$\bM_\pvpsi\in\C^{2\times2}$ at one event $\pX\in\R\oplus\R^3$.
For a nonnegative weight $\pZ\in\R\oplus\R^3$, the local densities
\eqref{eq:probability-density} and \eqref{eq:weighted-density} become
\begin{align*}
 \lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2
 &=\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}(\bM_\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\bM_\pvpsi),\\
 \lVert\pvpsi\rVert_\pZ^2
 &=\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}
    (\bM_\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\operatorname{mat}(\pZ)\bM_\pvpsi).
\end{align*}
The first is one half the sum of the squared magnitudes of the matrix
entries: the squared Frobenius norm with the factor $1/2$ fixed by
$\operatorname{sc}=\operatorname{tr}/2$.  The second is its weighted form.
Both are pointwise; neither contains a spatial integral.
Matrix multiplication gives exactly the congruence identity
\eqref{eq:weighted-density-pullback}.

\paragraph{An arbitrary spin basis.}
Notation: the arbitrary constant perpendicular
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vectors}
$\bu,\ba\in\R^3$ of \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-basis}.
The projector $\pvPi_+=(1+\bu\dotprod\bsig)/2$ is defined, and a constant complex
paravector $\pvpsi'$ with $\pvPi_+\pvpsi'\ne0$ is chosen.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \begin{Bmatrix}\pvpsi_+\\\pvpsi_-\end{Bmatrix}
:=\frac1{\lVert\pvPi_+\pvpsi'\rVert}
 \begin{Bmatrix}\pvPi_+\pvpsi'\\(\ba\dotprod\bsig)\pvPi_+\pvpsi'\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:ideal-spin-basis}
\end{equation}
This constructs \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-basis} with
$(\bu\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_\pm=\pm\pvpsi_\pm$ and
$\scpart{\pvpsi_s^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_{s'}}=\delta_{ss'}$ for $s,s'=+,-$.
The two complex scalar components give
\begin{equation}
 \pvpsi=c_+\pvpsi_++c_-\pvpsi_-=(c_++c_-\ba\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi_+.
 \label{eq:ideal-components}
\end{equation}
For fields, $c_\pm:\R^4\to\C$, and
\eqref{eq:probability-density} is
$\rho=\lVert c_+\rVert^2+\lVert c_-\rVert^2$.

\paragraph{The two-component column.}
Every column of $\bM_{\pvpsi_+}$ has eigenvalue $+1$ under
$\operatorname{mat}(\bu\dotprod\bsig)$, whose $+1$ eigenspace is
one-dimensional.  Since $\lVert\pvpsi_+\rVert=1$, its matrix therefore
factors as $\bM_{\pvpsi_+}=\sqrt2\,\bpsi_+\bm v^{\mathsf H}$ for
constant columns $\bpsi_+,\bm v\in\C^2$ satisfying
$\bpsi_+^{\mathsf H}\bpsi_+=\bm v^{\mathsf H}\bm v=1$.

\Needspace{3\baselineskip}
By left multiplication,
\mbox{$\bM_{\pvpsi_-}=\sqrt2\,\bpsi_-\bm v^{\mathsf H}$}, where
$\bpsi_-=\operatorname{mat}(\ba\dotprod\bsig)\bpsi_+$.
The columns $\bpsi_+,\bpsi_-$ are orthonormal.  Thus every amplitude
in \eqref{eq:ideal-components} has the conventional Pauli column
\begin{equation*}
 \bpsi:=\frac1{\sqrt2}\bM_\pvpsi\bm v
 =c_+\bpsi_++c_-\bpsi_-\in\C^2.
\end{equation*}
Conversely, $\bM_\pvpsi=\sqrt2\,\bpsi\bm v^{\mathsf H}$, so this
correspondence is one-to-one.  For two amplitudes in this fixed basis
and any complex multiplying paravector $\pZ$, by substitution,
\begin{align*}
 \scpart{\pvpsi'^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi}&=\bpsi'^{\mathsf H}\bpsi,\\
 \scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi}
 &=\bpsi^{\mathsf H}\operatorname{mat}(\pZ)\bpsi.
\end{align*}
For nonnegative $\pZ$, the second line is $\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_\pZ^2$.
Dividing by $\rho=\bpsi^{\mathsf H}\bpsi>0$ gives
$\langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi$, the ordinary finite-component expectation.
Left action $\pT\pvpsi$ maps to $\operatorname{mat}(\pT)\bpsi$ for any complex paravector $\pT$.

$\vstyle{C}\in\C^{2\times2}$ is defined as the matrix with columns
$\bpsi_+,\bpsi_-$.  Then $\vstyle{C}^{\mathsf H}\vstyle{C}=\bI_2$ and
$\bpsi=\vstyle{C}\{c_+,c_-\}^{\mathsf T}$.
For a complex paravector $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$, its matrix in this basis is
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{mat}'(\pZ):=\vstyle{C}^{\mathsf H}\operatorname{mat}(\pZ)\vstyle{C}
 =\begin{Bmatrix}
 c+\bu\dotprod\bZ&(\ba-\ii\bu\times\ba)\dotprod\bZ\\
 (\ba+\ii\bu\times\ba)\dotprod\bZ&c-\bu\dotprod\bZ
 \end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:matrix-arbitrary-axis}
\end{equation}
Here the prime denotes only this change of matrix basis.
For nonzero squared norm $\rho=\bpsi^{\mathsf H}\bpsi$, the polarization
\eqref{eq:spin-state} and probabilities for
$\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bsig)/2$ become
\begin{align}
 \bu\dotprod\bu'&=\bpsi^{\mathsf H}\operatorname{mat}(\bu\dotprod\bsig)\bpsi/\rho,
 \label{eq:matrix-state}\\
 \mathrm{prob}(\pm\mid\bu)&=\bpsi^{\mathsf H}\operatorname{mat}(\pvPi_\pm)\bpsi/\rho.
 \label{eq:matrix-born}
\end{align}
Equations~\eqref{eq:matrix-state} and \eqref{eq:matrix-born} are the ordinary
spin expectation and Born rule.

\subsection{Dirac columns}
\label{sec:matrix-dirac-columns}

For a Dirac block $\bpsi=\{\pvpsi_+,\pvpsi_-\}^{\mathsf T}$, the corresponding columns
are denoted by $\vstyle{c}_+,\vstyle{c}_-\in\C^2$.  Then
\[
 \Wop(\pZ)\bpsi\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad
 \begin{Bmatrix}0&\operatorname{mat}(\adj {\pZ})\\
 \operatorname{mat}(\pZ)&0\end{Bmatrix}
 \begin{Bmatrix}\vstyle{c}_+\\\vstyle{c}_-\end{Bmatrix}.
\]
The block squared norm in \eqref{eq:dirac-norm} becomes the squared norm
of a four-component column:
\begin{equation*}
 \rho=\vstyle{c}_+^{\mathsf H}\vstyle{c}_++\vstyle{c}_-^{\mathsf H}\vstyle{c}_-.
\end{equation*}

\paragraph{Characteristic polynomial.}
On one Fourier mode, $s\in\C$ is the spectral parameter and $\Box$
denotes the scalar eigenvalue of the wave operator.  The two off-diagonal blocks multiply to
$-\Box\bI_2$, so block elimination gives the ordinary matrix determinant
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \det\begin{Bmatrix}
 -(s+m)\bI_2&\operatorname{mat}(\ii\pvpartial)\\
 \operatorname{mat}(\ii\pvpartial^*)&-(s+m)\bI_2
 \end{Bmatrix}
 &=\det\!\left(\bigl((s+m)^2+\Box\bigr)\bI_2\right)\\
 &=\bigl((s+m)^2+\Box\bigr)^2.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:matrix-dirac-characteristic}
\end{equation}
This verifies \eqref{eq:dirac-characteristic}, including multiplicities.
The polynomial identity also holds at $s=-m$.

\Needspace{14\baselineskip}
\subsection{Four components and the standard Dirac basis}
\label{sec:matrix-dirac-components}

The fixed basis elements of \eqref{eq:ideal-spin-basis} are denoted by
$\pveta_\pm\in\C\oplus\C^3$. Both entries are expressed in this complex span:
$\pvpsi_+=a\pveta_++b\pveta_-$ and $\pvpsi_-=c'\pveta_++d\pveta_-$,
where $a,b,c',d:\R^4\to\C$ are the four scalar components,
forming a column in $\C^4$ at each event.
Their columns are $\vstyle{c}_+'=\{a,b\}^{\mathsf T}$ and
$\vstyle{c}_-'=\{c',d\}^{\mathsf T}$; the primes on the columns denote the basis change
$\vstyle{c}_\pm'=\vstyle{C}^{\mathsf H}\vstyle{c}_\pm$.
Notation: real mass $m>0$, real charge $q$, and
real potentials $V:\R^4\to\R$ and $\bA:\R^4\to\R^3$.
The gauge-covariant energy and kinetic momentum operators are $\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$ and
$\hbpq=-\ii\pdr-q\bA$.  Their momentum components in this frame are
\[
 \hat P_{q,1}=\ba\dotprod\hbpq,\qquad \hat P_{q,2}=(\bu\times\ba)\dotprod\hbpq,
 \qquad \hat P_{q,3}=\bu\dotprod\hbpq.
\]
Using \eqref{eq:matrix-arbitrary-axis}, the coupled paravector equations give
\begin{align}
 (\hEq-\hat P_{q,3})c'-(\hat P_{q,1}-\ii \hat P_{q,2})d&=ma,
 \label{eq:matrix-dirac-a}\\
 -(\hat P_{q,1}+\ii \hat P_{q,2})c'+(\hEq+\hat P_{q,3})d&=mb,
 \label{eq:matrix-dirac-b}\\
 (\hEq+\hat P_{q,3})a+(\hat P_{q,1}-\ii \hat P_{q,2})b&=mc',
 \label{eq:matrix-dirac-c}\\
 (\hat P_{q,1}+\ii \hat P_{q,2})a+(\hEq-\hat P_{q,3})b&=md.
 \label{eq:matrix-dirac-d}
\end{align}
Operators in \eqref{eq:matrix-dirac-a}--\eqref{eq:matrix-dirac-d}
act on the scalar immediately to their right.  Their Hamiltonian
on $\{a,b,c',d\}^{\mathsf T}$ is
\begin{equation}
 \hH=\begin{Bmatrix}
 qV-\hat P_{q,3}&-\hat P_{q,1}+\ii \hat P_{q,2}&m&0\\
 -\hat P_{q,1}-\ii \hat P_{q,2}&qV+\hat P_{q,3}&0&m\\
 m&0&qV+\hat P_{q,3}&\hat P_{q,1}-\ii \hat P_{q,2}\\
 0&m&\hat P_{q,1}+\ii \hat P_{q,2}&qV-\hat P_{q,3}
 \end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:matrix-dirac-hamiltonian}
\end{equation}
This is the component image of \eqref{eq:dirac-hamiltonian}.

\Needspace{10\baselineskip}
Conventional gamma matrices are identified through
$\bm\sigma_k=\operatorname{mat}(\pvsigma_k)$ for $k=1,2,3$.
Here $\bm\sigma_k\in\C^{2\times2}$ and the gamma matrices below lie in
$\C^{4\times4}$. The $\bm\sigma_k$ are also the images under $\operatorname{mat}'$ of
$\ba\dotprod\bsig$, $(\bu\times\ba)\dotprod\bsig$, and $\bu\dotprod\bsig$,
respectively.  Their numerical entries apply to every choice of $\bu,\ba$.
The following matrices are defined~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eqs.~(4.16)--(4.17)}{page=89}
\begin{align}
 \gamma^0&=\begin{Bmatrix}0&\bI_2\\\bI_2&0\end{Bmatrix},
 \label{eq:gamma-time}\\
 \gamma^k&=\begin{Bmatrix}0&\bm\sigma_k\\-\bm\sigma_k&0\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:gamma-space}
\end{align}
Equations~\eqref{eq:gamma-time}--\eqref{eq:gamma-space} obey
$(\gamma^0)^2=\bI_4$, $(\gamma^k)^2=-\bI_4$, and
$\gamma^j\gamma^k+\gamma^k\gamma^j=0$ for distinct
$j,k=0,1,2,3$.  The standard chiral Dirac operator in this basis,
with signature $(+,-,-,-)$, is~
\begin{equation*}
 \Wop(\hPq)
 \quad\longleftrightarrow\quad
 \gamma^0\hEq-\sum_{k=1}^3\gamma^k\hat P_{q,k}.
\end{equation*}
Here $\pA=V+\bA\dotprod\bsig$ is the
real potential paravector.

The unitary change to the standard Dirac basis commutes with the scalar
rest-phase factor explained in \eqref{eq:dirac-rest-phase}.
Together they define the envelope columns
\begin{equation}
 \begin{Bmatrix}\bm\phi_+\\\bm\phi_-\end{Bmatrix}
 =\frac{e^{\ii mt}}{\sqrt2}
 \begin{Bmatrix}\bI_2&\bI_2\\-\bI_2&\bI_2\end{Bmatrix}
 \begin{Bmatrix}\vstyle{c}_+'\\\vstyle{c}_-'\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:matrix-envelope-map}
\end{equation}
Here $\bm\phi_+,\bm\phi_-\in\C^2$ are the coefficient columns in the same
spin basis of the paravector fields
$\pvphi_+=e^{\ii mt}(\pvpsi_++\pvpsi_-)/\sqrt2$ and
$\pvphi_-=e^{\ii mt}(\pvpsi_--\pvpsi_+)/\sqrt2$.
Before removing the rest phase, the basis change in
\eqref{eq:matrix-envelope-map} sends \eqref{eq:matrix-dirac-hamiltonian} to
\begin{equation}
 \hH'=\begin{Bmatrix}
 (qV+m)\bI_2&\sum_k\hat P_{q,k}\bm\sigma_k\\
 \sum_k\hat P_{q,k}\bm\sigma_k&(qV-m)\bI_2
 \end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:matrix-standard-dirac}
\end{equation}
Multiplying the full column by $e^{\ii mt}$ subtracts $m$ from its
Hamiltonian: $\ii\partial_t(e^{\ii mt}\bpsi)=e^{\ii mt}(\ii\partial_t-m)\bpsi$.
Thus \eqref{eq:matrix-standard-dirac} becomes $\hH'-mI_4$:
its diagonal entries are $qV$ and $qV-2m$.
Moving $qV$ into $\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$ gives the exact envelope equations
\begin{align*}
 \hEq\bm\phi_+&=\sum_k\hat P_{q,k}\bm\sigma_k\bm\phi_-,\\
 (\hEq+2m)\bm\phi_-&=\sum_k\hat P_{q,k}\bm\sigma_k\bm\phi_+.
\end{align*}
These identify the large and small amplitudes used in the Pauli limit.

For real electric and magnetic components $E_k,B_k$ in the same frame,
the squared Dirac field coupling has the explicit image
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{mat}'(\ii q\pF)
 &=q\begin{Bmatrix}
 \ii E_3-B_3&\ii E_1+E_2-B_1+\ii B_2\\
 \ii E_1-E_2-B_1-\ii B_2&-\ii E_3+B_3
 \end{Bmatrix},
 \\
 \operatorname{mat}'(\ii q\pF^*)&=\operatorname{mat}'(\ii q\pF)^{\mathsf H}.
\end{align*}
The indexed $E_k$ are field components;
whereas $\hEq$ is the gauge-covariant energy operator.
The two couplings are $-q\bBf\dotprod\bsig\pm\ii q\bE\dotprod\bsig$.

\subsection{Quaternion matrix image}
\label{sec:quaternion-matrix}

For the quaternion $\pQ=a+\bQ\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)=a-\ii\bQ\dotprod\bsig$
of Section~\ref{sec:quaternions}, with $a\in\R$, $\bQ\in\R^3$, and
$\pQ\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3$, the explicit
matrix image is~\citeat{lounesto-intro}{p.~10}{page=23}
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{mat}(\pQ)=
 \begin{Bmatrix}a-\ii Q_3&-Q_2-\ii Q_1\\
                 Q_2-\ii Q_1&a+\ii Q_3\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:quaternion-matrix}
\end{equation}
Here
$\operatorname{mat}((-\ii\pvsigma_k))=-\ii\operatorname{mat}(\pvsigma_k)$.
The conjugation and norm identities become
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{mat}(\pQ^{\mathsf H})&=\operatorname{mat}(\pQ)^{\mathsf H},\\
 \operatorname{mat}(\pQ)^{\mathsf H}\operatorname{mat}(\pQ)
 &=(a^2+\bQ^2)\bI_2,\\
 \det(\operatorname{mat}(\pQ))&=a^2+\bQ^2.
\end{align*}
For a unit quaternion, $a^2+\bQ^2=1$, these identities give a unitary
matrix of determinant one.

\clearpage
\section{Equation reference}
\label{sec:reference-summary}
\begingroup\allowdisplaybreaks[1]

Each displayed formula repeats a numbered result; its right-hand number
links to the definition or derivation.  Natural units are $\hbar=c=1$.
The linked text supplies domains, component expansions, and approximation
conditions.

\Needspace{8\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Algebra.}
Notation: complex paravectors $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ and $\pZ'=c'+\bZ'\dotprod\bsig$,
with $c,c'\in\C$, $\bZ,\bZ'\in\C^3$, and $\pZ,\pZ'\in\C\oplus\C^3$.
The prime here labels a second element.  Their product is
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \pZ \pZ'={}&(cc'+\bZ\dotprod\bZ')\\
 &+(c\bZ'+c'\bZ+\ii\bZ\times\bZ')\dotprod\bsig.
 \end{aligned}\tag*{\eqref{eq:para-product}}
\end{equation*}
The basic operations are
\begin{align*}
 \pZ^{\mathsf H}&=c^*+\bZ^*\dotprod\bsig,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:algebra-H}}\\
 \pZ^*&=c^*-\bZ^*\dotprod\bsig,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:algebra-star}}\\
 \operatorname{adj} \pZ&=\pZ^{*\mathsf H}=c-\bZ\dotprod\bsig,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:adjugate}}\\
 \operatorname{tr}\pZ&=2\scpart {\pZ},
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:trace-scalar}}\\
 \repart {\pZ}&=\tfrac12(\pZ+\pZ^{\mathsf H}),
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:algebra-re}}\\
 \impart {\pZ}&=(\pZ-\pZ^{\mathsf H})/(2\ii),
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:algebra-im}}\\
 \det \pZ&=\adj {\pZ}\,\pZ=c^2-\bZ^2,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:det}}\\
 \pZ^{-1}&=\adj {\pZ}/\det \pZ\qquad(\det \pZ\ne0),
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:inverse}}\\
 \lVert \pZ\rVert^2&=\scpart{\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ}.
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:hermitian-norm}}
\end{align*}
The coefficient form is \eqref{eq:coefficient-norm}; product-order and
cyclicity rules are \eqref{eq:hermitian-product},
\eqref{eq:sigma-conjugate-product}, and \eqref{eq:scalar-cyclic-three}.
Projectors and the spectral rule are \eqref{eq:projectors} and
\eqref{eq:complex-function-projectors}.  The quaternion component map is
\eqref{eq:quaternion-paravector}; matrix images are
\eqref{eq:matrix} and \eqref{eq:quaternion-matrix}.

\Needspace{8\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Rotations and boosts.}
Here $\bu\in\R^3$ is a \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{unit axis},
$\theta\in\R$ an angle or rapidity,
$\pR\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3$, and $\pL\in\R\oplus\R^3$;
$\beta=\tanh\theta$ and $\gamma=\cosh\theta$ in the boost formulas.
The factors and their actions are
\begin{align*}
 \pR&=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)
   =\cos(\theta/2)-\ii\sin(\theta/2)\bu\dotprod\bsig,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:rotation-exponential}}\\
 \pL&=\exp(-\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2)
   =\cosh(\theta/2)-\sinh(\theta/2)\bu\dotprod\bsig,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:boost-exponential}}\\
 \pL&=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma+1}{2}}
     \left(1-\frac{\gamma\beta}{\gamma+1}\bu\dotprod\bsig\right),
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:boost-gamma-form}}\\
 \pL^2&=\gamma(1-\beta\bu\dotprod\bsig),
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:boost-square}}\\
 \pZ'&=\pR \pZ \pR^{\mathsf H},
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:rotation-action}}\\
 \pZ'&=\pL \pZ \pL,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:boost-action}}\\
 \pF'&=\pL \pF \pL^{-1}.
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:boost-field-action}}
\end{align*}
Both $\pZ$ transformations use $\pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}$, with
$\pR^{\mathsf H}=\pR^{-1}$ and $\pL^{\mathsf H}=\pL$.
The $\pZ$ boost acts on a real four-vector; the complex electromagnetic
field $\pF$ uses similarity.  Both refer to the same frame.
Rodrigues' rotation is \eqref{eq:rodrigues}; boost components are
\eqref{eq:boost-scalar-component}--\eqref{eq:boost-vector-component}.
The general determinant-one congruence is
\eqref{eq:general-lorentz-congruence}; interval preservation is
\eqref{eq:boost-interval}.  Physical field components are derived in
Section~\ref{sec:transformations}, with matrix verification in
Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix-boosts}.

\Needspace{8\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Spacetime and particles.}
Notation: $\pX,\pU,\pP\in\R\oplus\R^3$, $s,t,E\in\R$,
$\br,\bv,\bp\in\R^3$, and constant $m\in\R_{>0}$.  The real four-velocity $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$ is an
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$.
The real kinetic four-momentum $\pP$ has particle energy $E$ (including
rest energy, excluding potential energy) and spatial kinetic momentum $\bp$.
The kinematic identities are
\begin{align*}
 \pvpartial&=\partial_t+\bsig\dotprod\pdr,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:paravector-derivative}}\\
 \Box&=\pvpartial^*\pvpartial=\partial_t^2-\pdr^2,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:dalembertian}}\\
 \dd s^2&=\det(\dd \pX)=\dd t^2-\dd\br^2,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:interval}}\\
 \pU&=\dd \pX/\dd s=\gamma(1+\bv\dotprod\bsig),
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:proper-velocity}}\\
 \pU^*\pU&=1,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:proper-velocity-star-normalization}}\\
 \pP&=m\pU=E+\bp\dotprod\bsig,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:four-momentum}}\\
 E^2-\bp^2&=m^2.
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:mass-shell}}
\end{align*}
Here $t$ is coordinate time, $s$ is proper time along the time-like path,
and $\gamma=\dd t/\dd s=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\bv^2}}$. Four-velocity is real,
with \hyperref[eq:unit-determinant-paravector]{determinant one}; Hermitian unitarity is the separate condition
\eqref{eq:unitary-transform}.
For real proper times $s_0<s_1$ and coordinate times $t_0<t$,
\begin{align*}
 \pX(s_1)-\pX(s_0)&=\int_{s_0}^{s_1}\pU(s)\dd s,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:worldline-integral}}\\
 s(t)-s(t_0)&=\int_{t_0}^{t}\frac{\dd t'}{\gamma(t')}
 =\int_{t_0}^{t}\sqrt{1-\bv(t')^2}\dd t'.
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:elapsed-proper-time}}
\end{align*}
Clock comparison is \eqref{eq:maximum-proper-time}; constant proper
acceleration gives \eqref{eq:hyperbolic-worldline}; the future cone is
\eqref{eq:future-light-cone}.  Proper acceleration, inertia, and particle
action are \eqref{eq:proper-acceleration-components},
\eqref{eq:relativistic-inertia}, and \eqref{eq:free-particle-action}.
Moving-frame velocity and its causal bound are
\eqref{eq:velocity-boost}--\eqref{eq:velocity-causal-bound}; clock dilation
and measured contraction are \eqref{eq:time-dilation} and
\eqref{eq:length-contraction}; energy and momentum transform by
\eqref{eq:energy-boost}--\eqref{eq:momentum-boost}.
\Needspace{8\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Electromagnetism.}
Notation: the real four-velocity $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$, an
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$.
Notation: $V,\rho,S,q\in\R$, $\bA,\bE,\bBf,\bJ\in\R^3$,
$\pA,\pJ\in\R\oplus\R^3$, and
$\pF\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3$.
The coefficient fields depend on real spacetime; $q$ is constant charge.
Their boosted arguments and components are
\eqref{eq:boosted-field-arguments} and
\eqref{eq:electric-boost}--\eqref{eq:magnetic-boost}.
The field and particle equations are
\begin{align*}
 \pF&=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\dotprod\bsig,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:em-field-definition}}\\
 \pvpartial \pF&=\pJ^*,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:maxwell}}\\
 \Box \pF^*&=\pvpartial \pJ,\qquad \Box \pF=\pvpartial^*\pJ^*,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:maxwell-wave}}\\
 \pF^*&=\pvpartial \pA-S,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:potential-field}}\\
 \pF^2&=\bE^2-\bBf^2+2\ii\bE\dotprod\bBf,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:field-invariants}}\\
 \frac{\dd \pP}{\dd s}&=q\repart{\pF \pU},
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:lorentz}}\\
 T(\pX)&=\tfrac12\pF \pX \pF^{\mathsf H},
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:electromagnetic-stress-map}}\\
 \partial_tu+\pdr\dotprod\bS&=-\bJ\dotprod\bE.
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:poynting}}
\end{align*}
Here $T:\R\oplus\R^3\to\R\oplus\R^3$ is the stress map,
$\pX\in\R\oplus\R^3$ its argument, $u\in\R_{\ge0}$ field energy
density, and $\bS\in\R^3$ energy flux.
Potential components are \eqref{eq:electric-potential}--\eqref{eq:magnetic-potential};
force components are \eqref{eq:lorentz-momentum} and \eqref{eq:lorentz-energy}.
The action derivation uses \eqref{eq:maxwell-lagrangian} and
\eqref{eq:charged-particle-action}.

\Needspace{8\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Relativistic waves.}
Notation: $m\in\R_{>0}$, $q,V\in\R$, $\bA\in\R^3$, and
$\pA=V+\bA\dotprod\bsig\in\R\oplus\R^3$.
Here $\hEq$ is a scalar differential operator and $\hbpq$ a triple of them;
$\psi:\R^4\to\C$ is a KG field.
The Dirac field $\bpsi$ has two entries in the same fixed spinor space,
hence four complex coordinates.
Here, subscript $0$ labels the uncoupled operators of
\eqref{eq:canonical-operators}. The operators and wave equations are
\begin{align*}
 \hEq&=\hE_0-qV=\ii\partial_t-qV,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:mechanical-energy-operator}}\\
 \hbpq&=\hbp_0-q\bA=-\ii\pdr-q\bA,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:mechanical-momentum-operator}}\\
 \hPq&=\hP_0-q\pA=\hEq+\hbpq\dotprod\bsig,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:mechanical-paravector}}\\
 (\hbpq\dotprod\bsig)^2&=\hbpq^2-q\bBf\dotprod\bsig,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:kinetic-sigma-square}}\\
 [\hEq,\hbpq\dotprod\bsig]&=\ii q\bE\dotprod\bsig,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:kinetic-electric-commutator}}\\
 (-\hEq^2+\hbpq^2+m^2)\psi&=0,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:kg-field}}\\
 \Wop(\pZ)&=\begin{Bmatrix}0&\adj {\pZ}\\\pZ&0\end{Bmatrix}.
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:dirac-block-map}}
\end{align*}
Here $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$; $\Wop(\pZ)$ and $\bI$ are represented by
matrices in $\C^{4\times4}$, whereas $\hD$ and $\hDq$ are matrix
differential operators.
For the free field ($q=0$),
\begin{align*}
 \hP\,\adj\hP&=\det\hP=\hE^2-\hbp^2=-\Box,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:free-momentum-product}}\\
 \hD(m)&=\Wop(\hP)-m\bI,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:dirac-operator}}\\
 \hD(m)\bpsi&=0,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:dirac}}\\
 \hD(-m)\hD(m)&=-(\Box+m^2)\bI.
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:dirac-free-factorization}}
\end{align*}
For fixed $q$ and $\pA$, the interacting definition and equation are
\begin{align*}
 \hDq(m)&=\Wop(\hPq)-m\bI,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:dirac-field-operator}}\\
 \hDq(m)\bpsi&=(\Wop(\hPq)-m\bI)\bpsi=0.
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:dirac-field}}
\end{align*}
Both $\hD(-m)$ and $\hDq(-m)$ change only the sign of the mass term.
The free equations are \eqref{eq:kg} and \eqref{eq:dirac};
the two-branch solution is \eqref{eq:kg-general-solution}.
Massive Dirac reconstruction is given by \eqref{eq:kg-dirac-complete-lift}
and \eqref{eq:dirac-field-reconstruction}; the squared interacting entries
are \eqref{eq:dirac-squared-first} and \eqref{eq:dirac-squared-field}.

\Needspace{12\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Low-energy limits.}
After removing rest energy by \eqref{eq:dirac-rest-phase} and
\eqref{eq:kg-rest-phase}, the complex scalar envelope $\psi$ obeys
Schr\"odinger's equation to leading order:
\begin{equation*}
 \ii\partial_t\psi=\left(qV+\frac{\hbpq^2}{2m}\right)\psi.
 \tag*{\eqref{eq:schrodinger}}
\end{equation*}
The envelope $\pvphi_+$ in the fixed two-dimensional complex spinor space
of $\C\oplus\C^3$ obeys Pauli's equation:
\begin{equation*}
 \ii\partial_t\pvphi_+=\left(qV+
 \frac{\hbpq^2-q\bBf\dotprod\bsig}{2m}\right)\pvphi_+.
 \tag*{\eqref{eq:pauli}}
\end{equation*}
The derivations and approximation conditions are in
Section~\ref{sec:low-energy-limits}; these are the same leading order
for scalar and spinor amplitudes respectively.

\Needspace{10\baselineskip}
\paragraph{Spin and probabilities.}
Notation: a nonzero $\pvpsi$ in the fixed spinor space, $\rho\in\R_{>0}$,
and polarization and measurement axes $\bu',\bu\in\R^3$ of unit norm.
The real observable $\pZ\in\R\oplus\R^3$ acts from the left.  A rotation uses
\begin{equation*}
 \pvpsi'=\pR\pvpsi.
 \tag*{\eqref{eq:spin-half-law}}
\end{equation*}
It preserves the norm and inner product; the expectation transforms as
$\langle \pZ\rangle_{\pvpsi'}=\langle \pR^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pR\rangle_\pvpsi$ by
\eqref{eq:amplitude-pullback}.
All formulas are pointwise.  For a nonnegative real weight $\pZ$
from \eqref{eq:weighted-density}, the densities and measurements are
\begin{align*}
 \rho&=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2=\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi},
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:probability-density}}\\
 \lVert\pvpsi\rVert_\pZ^2&=\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi},
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:weighted-density}}\\
 \bu\dotprod\bu'&=\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}(\bu\dotprod\bsig)\pvpsi}/\rho,
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:spin-state}}\\
 \mathrm{prob}(\pm\mid\bu)&=\tfrac12(1\pm\bu\dotprod\bu'),
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:spin-probability-intrinsic}}\\
 \langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi&=\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi}/\rho.
   \tag*{\eqref{eq:paravector-expectation}}
\end{align*}
The expectation formula also applies to signed real observables.
The matrix verification is \eqref{eq:matrix-state}--\eqref{eq:matrix-born}.

\endgroup

\clearpage
\section{Conventions}
\label{sec:conventions}

Unadorned source symbols below retain each source's meaning.
In the coefficient notation, $\pZ,\pG\in\C\oplus\C^3$,
$\pX,\pY,\pA,\pP,\pJ\in\R\oplus\R^3$, and
$\pF\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3$.
Physical factors $\pG$ have determinant one; spinor amplitudes lie in the
fixed ideal of \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}.

\paragraph{Names and algebra classifications.}
The name \emph{space--time algebra} is used in this paper for the
formulation in complex paravectors defined by the sigma multiplication rules.
In Clifford-algebra terminology, this eight-real-dimensional algebra is
classified as $\mathrm{Cl}_{3,0}$ and is commonly called the
\emph{algebra of physical space} (APS). It is isomorphic to the even
subalgebra $\mathrm{Cl}_{1,3}^{+}$ of the sixteen-real-dimensional algebra
$\mathrm{Cl}_{1,3}$, for which the name \emph{spacetime algebra} (STA) is
used by Hestenes~\citeat{baylis2004}{pp.~1--2}{page=1}.
In $\mathrm{Cl}_{p,q}$, $p$ positive and $q$ negative basis-vector squares are
specified; the signature $(+,-,-,-)$ is denoted by $(1,3)$, and the
opposite signature by $(3,1)$.

In this terminology, the sigmas are called \emph{generators}.
The basis elements $1$, $\pvsigma_k$, $\ii\pvsigma_k$, and $\ii$ are
assigned grades $0$, $1$, $2$, and $3$, respectively, and are called
scalars, vectors, bivectors, and pseudoscalars.
Thus scalar and pseudoscalar grades are combined in the complex scalar
part, and vector and bivector grades in the complex vector term.
Hermitian conjugation is called \emph{reversion} in APS; sigma conjugation
is called \emph{grade involution}; and adjugation is called
\emph{Clifford conjugation}~\citeat{baylis2004}{pp.~4--6}{page=4}.
These correspondences depend on the algebra: the four-dimensional
comparison is given below.

A rotation factor is also called a \emph{rotor}, a projector an
\emph{idempotent}, and an invertible element an \emph{algebraic unit};
neither unit norm nor determinant one is implied by the last name.
The fixed two-dimensional complex spinor space of
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components} is called a \emph{minimal left ideal}:
it is preserved by left multiplication by every paravector.
In particle dynamics, \emph{mechanical momentum} is another name for
kinetic momentum. The quantity $\gamma m$ in
Section~\ref{sec:energy-inertia} is sometimes called \emph{relativistic mass};
invariant mass is used throughout this paper.

\paragraph{Mapping to the literature.}
The conventions of Baylis and Sobczyk are compared below
\citeat{baylis2004}{pp.~4--6}{page=4}.
The table translates the notation of the \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Paravector&oldid=1318953844\#The_three-dimensional_Euclidean_space}{\emph{Paravector} article}; $A$ denotes a general element and $x$ a real paravector.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Object or operation & This paper & APS notation\\
\midrule
Spatial basis & $\pvsigma_k$ & $\mathbf e_k$\\
Central imaginary unit & $\ii=\pvsigma_1\pvsigma_2\pvsigma_3$ & $i=\mathbf e_{123}$\\
Real paravector & $\pX=t+\br\dotprod\bsig$ & $x=x^0+\mathbf x$\\
General algebra element & $\pZ=c+\bZ\dotprod\bsig$ & $A$ (complex paravector)\\
Reversion & $\pZ^{\mathsf H}$ & $A^\dagger$\\
Clifford conjugation & $\operatorname{adj}\pZ$ & $\bar A$\\
Grade involution & $\pZ^*$ & $\bar A^\dagger$\\
Complex scalar part & $\scpart {\pZ}$ & $\langle A\rangle_S$\\
Complex vector part & $\vecpart {\pZ}\dotprod\bsig$ & $\langle A\rangle_V$\\
Real part & $\repart {\pZ}$ & $\langle A\rangle_R$\\
Imaginary part & $\ii\,\impart {\pZ}$ & $\langle A\rangle_I$\\
Spacetime quadratic form & $\det \pX=t^2-\br^2$ & $x\bar x$\\
Projectors & $\pvPi_+,\pvPi_-$ & $P_{\mathbf u},\bar P_{\mathbf u}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Real paravectors have four real components; complex $\pZ$ packages all eight
APS components.  Here $\scpart {\pZ}$ includes the pseudoscalar, and
$\impart {\pZ}$ removes the imaginary part's factor $\ii$.

In particular, the bar is represented here by the adjugate, not by sigma conjugation:
\[
 \bar A\ \longleftrightarrow\ c-\bZ\dotprod\bsig,
 \qquad
 \bar A^\dagger\ \longleftrightarrow\ c^*-\bZ^*\dotprod\bsig.
\]
They coincide only for real paravectors.  Complex coefficients are conjugated by sigma conjugation,
which is identified with grade involution on the full algebra.
Wikipedia's \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Paravector&oldid=1318953844\#Paragradient}{paragradient} is represented here by the starred derivative:
\[
 \partial_{\mathrm{article}}=\partial_t-\nabla
 \ \longleftrightarrow\ \pvpartial^*,\qquad
 \bar\partial_{\mathrm{article}}\ \longleftrightarrow\ \pvpartial,
 \qquad \Box=\pvpartial\pvpartial^*.
\]

Baylis's rotor action $x'=GxG^\dagger$ is written here as $\pX'=\pG \pX \pG^{\mathsf H}$;
Baylis's field $\mathbf E+i\mathbf B$ and law $F'=GF\bar G$ are written here as
$\pF=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\dotprod\bsig$ and $\pF'=\pG \pF \pG^{-1}$ for $\det \pG=1$
($c=1$)~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(23)}{page=19}\,\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(24)}{page=20}\,\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(33)}{page=22}.
Baylis's positively oriented spatial-plane generator is
$\mathbf e_2\mathbf e_1=-i\mathbf e_3$, giving
$\pR=e^{-\ii\theta\pvsigma_3/2}$ about $+\pvsigma_3$
\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eqs.~(17)--(19)}{page=14}.
Baylis's positive-rapidity boost factor, denoted here by $\pB$, satisfies
\[
 \pB=e^{+\theta\bu\dotprod\bsig/2}=\pL^{-1},\qquad
 \pB^2=\gamma(1+\beta\bu\dotprod\bsig),\qquad
 \pX'=\pB^{-1}\pX(\pB^{-1})^{\mathsf H}=\pL \pX \pL^{\mathsf H}.
\]
Thus the passive boost is obtained by inversion of Baylis's positive active boost
\citeat{baylis-intro}{p.~23}{page=23}; the active/passive distinction is
explicit in~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(38)}{page=12}.  The algebra is standard;
exponent signs must be read with the transformation direction and sandwich order.

For translation to Hestenes's four-dimensional STA, an observer axis
$\gamma_0$ is chosen, with $\gamma_0^2=1$ and $\gamma_k^2=-1$.  The identification
with its even subalgebra is~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(40)}{page=12}\,\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(41)}{page=13}
\[
 \pvsigma_k\longleftrightarrow\gamma_k\gamma_0,\qquad
 \operatorname{adj}\pZ\longleftrightarrow\widetilde {Z}_{\mathrm{STA}},\qquad
 \pZ^{\mathsf H}\longleftrightarrow
 \gamma_0\widetilde {Z}_{\mathrm{STA}}\gamma_0.
\]
Here $\gamma_\mu\in\mathrm{Cl}_{1,3}$ are spacetime basis vectors,
not the numerical gamma matrices of Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix-dirac-components}.
The image $Z_{\mathrm{STA}}$ lies in the even subalgebra
$\mathrm{Cl}_{1,3}^{+}$, and the tilde reverses STA products: reversion in APS and reversion in STA are different operations.

\clearpage
\paragraph{Translating the physical comparisons.}
\begin{SIunits}{Comparison with Tong}
SI units are used in Tong's component electrodynamics;
$c=\epsilon_0=\mu_0=1$ is used outside these boxes.
\end{SIunits}

Tong's gauge parameter in Eq.~(5.13) is
$\chi=-\lambda$, whereas Eq.~(6.2) is written with the same plus-sign convention
\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.13)}{page=114}\,\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(6.2)}{page=142}.  Tong's positive-exponent spatial rotation is a passive
axis rotation; the negative-exponent boost is identified with the passive boost used here
\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.38)}{page=159}\,\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.39)}{page=160}.

Chappell et al.'s scalar $Z$ is represented here by $c$, and the vector--bivector
$F$ by $\bZ\dotprod\bsig$, with $|F|^2=-\bZ^2$.
Thus the trigonometric exponential is represented here by the hyperbolic form
\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(52)}{page=12}.

For the quaternion basis, the Hamilton units are identified by Baylis with
$\mathbf e_{32},\mathbf e_{13},\mathbf e_{21}$, respectively
\citeat{baylis-applications}{p.~92}{page=103}.  With
$\mathbf e_k\leftrightarrow\pvsigma_k$, the units are represented here by
$(-\ii\pvsigma_1),(-\ii\pvsigma_2),(-\ii\pvsigma_3)$.
Higham's spectral and repeated-root formulas apply through the faithful
matrix image in Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix}; no change of the intrinsic
product is required~\citeat{higham-functions}{pp.~3--4}{page=4}.

Lenz's scalar potentials $\phi,\Phi$ are represented here by $V$, with $\hbar=c=1$ and
$e=q$. The Dirac Hamiltonian is represented by the matrix form
\eqref{eq:matrix-standard-dirac}~\citeat{lenz-quantum}{Eq.~(412)}{page=75}.

For Hestenes's Maxwell equation, the spacetime derivative and current
are denoted by $\nabla_{\mathrm{STA}}$ and $J_{\mathrm{STA}}$, respectively.  Using the
STA embedding above,
\[
 \nabla_{\mathrm{STA}}=\gamma_0\pvpartial,\qquad
 J_{\mathrm{STA}}=\pJ\gamma_0=\gamma_0\pJ^*,\qquad
 \pvpartial \pF=\pJ^*\ \Longleftrightarrow\
 \nabla_{\mathrm{STA}}\pF=J_{\mathrm{STA}}.
\]
Hestenes's explicit spacetime split is represented here by the component equation
\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eqs.~(175)--(177)}{page=29}.
For a real spacetime vector $n\in\R^{1,3}$,
$\pX=n\gamma_0\in\R\oplus\R^3$ is used in the observer's paravector identification.  Hestenes's stress map
$T_{\mathrm{STA}}(n)=\tfrac12Fn\widetilde {F}$, with
$F^{\mathsf H}=\gamma_0\widetilde {F}\gamma_0$, gives
\[
 T_{\mathrm{STA}}(n)\gamma_0=\tfrac12\pF \pX \pF^{\mathsf H}=T(\pX),
\]
including the energy and flux components in Eq.~(180) of that source
\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(167)}{page=28}\,\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(180)}{page=30}.

For Dirac and Pauli, the complex unit is represented by Hestenes and
Gurtler--Hestenes through right multiplication of the real spinor
by the spatial bivector $i\pvsigma_3$; it acts as ordinary $\ii$ in the corresponding column spinor
\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eqs.~(187)--(188)}{page=31}\,\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eqs.~(192)--(193)}{page=33}.
With $e=q$ and $\hbar=c=1$, Gurtler--Hestenes's potential energy $V$
is represented here by $qV$; after removal of rest energy, the operators
$K$ and $p$ are identified with $\hEq$ and $\hbpq\dotprod\bsig$.
The same components are then obtained for the large/small equations and Pauli limit
\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eqs.~(2.11)--(2.19)}{page=6}.

Charge is absorbed into the potential by Feynman--Gell-Mann. With charge restored,
Eq.~(6) is identified with the first squared Dirac block because
\[
 -\ii q\pF^*=q(\bBf+\ii\bE)\dotprod\bsig,\qquad
 -\ii q\pF=q(\bBf-\ii\bE)\dotprod\bsig.
\]
The two blocks therefore have the same magnetic sign and opposite
electric signs~\citeat{feynman-gellmann}{Eq.~(6)}{page=2}.

Baylis et al.'s $RP_3$ is a two-component minimal-ideal spinor; its
rotations are represented by the left amplitude action. From the density-operator
formula is obtained the same $(1+\bu\dotprod\bu')/2$ probability after basis
normalization, extending to mixed states with $\lVert\bu'\rVert\le1$
\citeat{baylis-spin}{Eqs.~(66)--(67)}{page=12}.
Passive basis rotations are used in Feynman's spin chapter; its $2\pi$ sign and
probability statements agree, while an active rotor uses the inverse
rotation~\citeat{feynmanspin}{Eq.~(6.19)}{Eq:III:6:19}.

\clearpage
The right-handed sigma product is fixed, and the following notation is used:
$\pN=\bu\dotprod\bsig\in\{0\}\oplus\R^3$, with $\bu\in\R^3$,
$\bu^2=1$, and $\theta\in\R$.  The alternatives below change
notation or transformation direction, not the underlying physics.
The main text uses $\pR=\pR_-$ and $\pL=\pL_-$, with
\[
 \pR_\pm=e^{\pm\ii\theta \pN/2},\qquad
 \pL_\pm=e^{\pm\theta \pN/2},\qquad
 \pR_\pm^{\mathsf H}=\pR_\pm^{-1}=\pR_\mp,\qquad
 \pL_\pm^{\mathsf H}=\pL_\pm,\quad \pL_\pm^{-1}=\pL_\mp.
\]
Here $\pR_\pm\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3$, $\pL_\pm\in\R\oplus\R^3$, and
$\theta$ is a rotation angle or rapidity respectively.

\paragraph{Exponent sign and sandwich order.}
For $\pX=t+\br\dotprod\bsig$, the four choices for each kind of factor are
summarized by the two action columns:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Factor $\pT$ & $\pX'=\pT \pX \pT^{\mathsf H}$ & $\pX'=\pT^{\mathsf H}\pX \pT$\\
\midrule
$\pR_-$ & Active rotation $+\theta$ & Active rotation $-\theta$\\
$\pR_+$ & Active rotation $-\theta$ & Active rotation $+\theta$\\
$\pL_-$ & Frame velocity $+\beta\bu$ & Frame velocity $+\beta\bu$\\
$\pL_+$ & Frame velocity $-\beta\bu$ & Frame velocity $-\beta\bu$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For rotations $t'=t$.  Both $+\theta$ entries give
\[
 \br'=\br\cos\theta+(\bu\times\br)\sin\theta
       +(1-\cos\theta)(\bu\dotprod\br)\bu.
\]
This is the active right-handed Rodrigues formula~\eqref{eq:rodrigues};
a passive rotation of axes uses the inverse map.

For boosts, $\beta=\tanh\theta$ and $\gamma=\cosh\theta$.
Writing $\br_\parallel=(\bu\dotprod\br)\bu$ and
$\br_\perp=\br-\br_\parallel$, both sandwiches with $\pL_\pm$ give
\[
 t'=\gamma(t\pm\beta\bu\dotprod\br),\qquad
 \br'=\br_\perp+\gamma(\br_\parallel\pm\beta t\bu).
\]
The minus choice sends $\br=\beta t\bu$ to $\br'=0$, fixing its frame
velocity.  Moving the adjoint reverses a rotation but has no effect on a
boost; reversing the boost exponent gives the inverse frame transformation.
The same $\pL_+$ map can instead describe an active boost to velocity
$+\beta\bu$ in fixed coordinates.

\paragraph{Hamilton quaternions fix the basis orientation.}
With the sigma orientation held fixed, the two uniform sign choices obey
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 (-\ii\pvsigma_j)(-\ii\pvsigma_k)&=-\delta_{jk}+\epsilon_{jkl}(-\ii\pvsigma_l),\\
 (+\ii\pvsigma_j)(+\ii\pvsigma_k)&=-\delta_{jk}-\epsilon_{jkl}(+\ii\pvsigma_l).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Repeated $l$ is summed. Hamilton's orientation is obtained with
$(-\ii\pvsigma_k)$, and the opposite orientation with $(+\ii\pvsigma_k)$.
For the Hamilton basis, Section~\ref{sec:quaternions} gives
\[
 \pQ=e^{\theta\bu\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)/2}=\pR_-,\qquad
 \pQ^{\mathsf H}=\pQ^{-1},\qquad
 \br'\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)=\pQ(\br\dotprod(-\ii\bsig))\pQ^{\mathsf H}.
\]
Since $\br\dotprod(-\ii\bsig)=-\ii\br\dotprod\bsig$ and $\ii$ commutes with $\pQ$,
the same sandwich rotates $\br\dotprod\bsig$, with no extra sign.
For example, $\pQ=(1-\ii\pvsigma_3)/\sqrt2$ sends $\pvsigma_1$ to $\pvsigma_2$.

Using $(+\ii\pvsigma_k)$ with
$\widetilde {\pQ}=e^{\theta\bu\dotprod(+\ii\bsig)/2}=\pR_+$ and the right-adjoint
sandwich instead gives $-\theta$.  $+\theta$ is recovered by reversing either
the exponent or the sandwich.  Reflecting the basis also restores Hamilton
multiplication, but changes its identification with the physical axes.
Replacing a factor by its negative leaves both sandwiches unchanged;
the spinor acquires an overall minus sign.  Consequently rotations are
$2\pi$-periodic on vectors and $4\pi$-periodic on amplitudes.

\paragraph{Rotating a state versus changing the measured observable.}
With the Hamilton basis, the two consistent descriptions of the same
active $+\theta$ rotation are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
Choice & Quaternion relation & Rotated observable & Rotated amplitude\\
\midrule
$\pR=\pR_-$ & $\pR=\pQ$ & $\pZ'=\pR \pZ \pR^{\mathsf H}$ & $\pvpsi'=\pR\pvpsi$\\
$\pR=\pR_+$ & $\pR=\pQ^{\mathsf H}$ & $\pZ'=\pR^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pR$ & $\pvpsi'=\pR^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Both have physical amplitude factor
$e^{-\ii\theta \pN/2}$ and spin generator $\pN/2$.
Changing only the vector sandwich would reverse its rotation relative to
the spin polarization.

For $\pG\in\C\oplus\C^3$ with $\det \pG\ne0$ acting as $\pvpsi'=\pG\pvpsi$, the definitions
\eqref{eq:probability-density} and \eqref{eq:paravector-expectation} give
\begin{align*}
 \rho&=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2,\qquad
 \rho'=\rho\langle \pG^{\mathsf H}\pG\rangle_\pvpsi,\\
 \langle\pvvarphi'\mid\pvpsi'\rangle
 &=\scpart{\pvvarphi^{\mathsf H}\pG^{\mathsf H}\pG\pvpsi},\\
 \langle \pZ\rangle_{\pvpsi'}
 &=\frac{\langle \pG^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pG\rangle_\pvpsi}
         {\langle \pG^{\mathsf H}\pG\rangle_\pvpsi}.
\end{align*}
Here $\pvvarphi'=\pG\pvvarphi$, and $\rho>0$.  For a rotation $\pG^{\mathsf H}\pG=1$:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\langle \pZ\rangle_{\pvpsi'}=\langle \pG^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pG\rangle_\pvpsi,
 \qquad
 \langle \pG \pZ \pG^{\mathsf H}\rangle_{\pvpsi'}=\langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi.}
 \label{eq:conventions-expectations}
\end{equation}
The first identity measures fixed $\pZ$ in the rotated state; the second
rotates the observable with the state.  Thus the opposite sandwich orders
in \eqref{eq:conventions-expectations} are required, not competing conventions.
For $\pG=\pL_\pm$, the denominator is $\langle \pL_\pm^2\rangle_\pvpsi$:
boosts generally change the local positive density and inner product.

\paragraph{Fields and wave amplitudes under either notation.}
$\pG$ is taken to be the constant physical factor of determinant one, so $\pX'=\pG \pX \pG^{\mathsf H}$.
For notation $\pT \pX \pT^{\mathsf H}$, $\pG=\pT$ is used; for $\pT^{\mathsf H}\pX \pT$,
$\pG=\pT^{\mathsf H}$ is used. At corresponding events the laws are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \pZ'&=\pG \pZ \pG^{\mathsf H},&& \pZ=\pX,\pP,\pA,\pJ,\\
 \pF'&=\pG \pF \pG^{-1},&&\pvpartial'=\pG^*\pvpartial \pG^{-1},\\
 \pvpsi_+'&=\pG^*\pvpsi_+,&&\pvpsi_-'=\pG\pvpsi_-,\\
 \psi_{\mathrm{KG}}'(\pX')&=\psi_{\mathrm{KG}}(\pX).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:conventions-field-laws}
\end{equation}
These are the covariance laws of Section~\ref{sec:transformations};
$\pG^*=(\pG^{-1})^{\mathsf H}$.  The field $\pF$ uses similarity, not the
four-vector sandwich.  Equation~\eqref{eq:conventions-field-laws} preserves
the Maxwell, Klein--Gordon, and Dirac equations.

For either rotation description, both Dirac entries use $\pR_-$.
For $\pG=\pL_\pm$, they use $\pL_\pm^{-1}$ and $\pL_\pm$, respectively;
the factor already contains half the rapidity.
With $\pX'=\pT^{\mathsf H}\pX \pT$, the same laws read
$\pvpsi_+'=\pT^{-1}\pvpsi_+$ and $\pvpsi_-'=\pT^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_-$.
The scalar paired quantity $\scpart{\pvpsi_+^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_-}$ is invariant;
the sum of the two positive densities generally changes under boosts.
The conserved Dirac current transforms as a four-vector; its time component
alone is not invariant.
For products, the physical factors compose as $\pG_2\pG_1$.  Hence the
right-adjoint parameters compose as $\pT_2\pT_1$, but the left-adjoint
parameters compose as $\pT_1\pT_2$.

\paragraph{Preserved quantities.}
The sign of the angle or rapidity changes the direction, not these
invariance statements.  Here $\pZ$ is a complex paravector and
$\lVert \pZ\rVert^2=\scpart{\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ}$; ``no'' means not generally preserved.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
Action & $\det \pZ$ & $\operatorname{tr}\pZ,\ \scpart {\pZ}$ & $\lVert \pZ\rVert^2$\\
\midrule
Rotation $\pR \pZ \pR^{\mathsf H}$ & Yes & Yes & Yes\\
Boost $\pL \pZ \pL^{\mathsf H}$ & Yes & No & No\\
Field similarity $\pG \pZ \pG^{-1}$ & Yes & Yes & If $\pG$ is unitary\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For real four-vectors transformed together, both rotations and boosts preserve
\[
 \scpart{\pX'^*\pY'}=\scpart{\pX^*\pY}.
\]
Similarity also preserves $\scpart{\pZ \pY}$ when both factors transform by
similarity.  The positive amplitude inner product is rotation invariant;
boost invariance instead applies to the spacetime metric and the paired
Dirac quantity above.  Normalized expectations remain equal for a state
and observable rotated together, not for an arbitrary fixed observable.

The main convention $\pR=\pQ=\pR_-$, $\pX'=\pR \pX \pR^{\mathsf H}$, $\pvpsi'=\pR\pvpsi$
uses the Hamilton quaternion directly.  The alternative
$\pR=\pQ^{\mathsf H}=\pR_+$, $\pX'=\pR^{\mathsf H}\pX \pR$, $\pvpsi'=\pR^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi$
is equally consistent.  Either can use $\pL^{\mathsf H}\pX \pL=\pL \pX \pL^{\mathsf H}$
with the boost sign fixed by the chosen frame velocity.
